We also study a BCH-based variant that avoids codebook storage but generally reduces robustness. The Levenshtein variant improves resilience to deletions and light paraphrasing, while insertions and stronger rewrites remain challenging. We analyze collision probability, codeword separation, extraction time, tokenization consistency, and text quality, highlighting both the practicality and limitations of codebook-based watermarking for short-form attribution.


### arXiv 2611.16886 - LLM-Assisted Verification in Dafny

- categories: cs.LO, cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-12T14:48:38Z
- authors: Wieger Wesselink, Kees Huizing, Huub van de Wetering
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16886

Formal verification of complex algorithms requires expertise in program specification, invariant construction, mathematical proof, and the management of automated theorem provers. Although large language models (LLMs) can generate program code and proof fragments, reliably developing complete machine-checked verifications remains challenging: it requires an iterative process of constructing and validating specifications and invariants, building proofs, diagnosing solver failures, and optimizing verification performance. This paper presents a systematic ten-step workflow for LLM-assisted verification of scientific algorithms using the Dafny verification system. The workflow integrates algorithm formalization, contract development, empirical invariant validation, proof construction, bottleneck profiling, and resource-driven proof optimization. We applied the workflow using Antrhopic's Claude Opus 5 and Opus 5.5 models, combining LLM-driven development with automated testing and verification feedback, while retaining human oversight for correctness-critical decisions and strategic interventions. We evaluate the approach on 37 complex algorithms from graph theory and labelled transition systems. Notably, we verified the Hopfcroft-Tarjan triconnectivity test, demonstrating that even highly challenging algorithmic verification tasks can be carried out through a structured LLM-assisted workflow. Many of our machine-checked verifications were obtained within hours to a few days of interactive development. Our results suggest that LLM-assisted workflows can shift the role of the human verifier from detailed proof construction toward supervision, specification review, and strategic guidance.


### arXiv 2611.16881 - Protein Language Models Fail To Capture Contact Predictions Under Invariant Relabeling

- categories: q-bio.QM, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T14:45:07Z
- authors: Paarth Batra, Aryan Keluskar
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16881

Protein language models (pLMs) produce highly accurate contact maps from single-sequence inputs. People often take this accuracy as proof that these models implicitly learn invariant coevolutionary statistics, extracting the same signals as classical alignment-based methods like Potts. In this work, we introduce a diagnostic framework around a global bijective relabeling of the amino-acid alphabet. This transformation permutes residue letters while leaving all alignment-level information, column entropies, and pairwise edit distances unchanged. Pseudolikelihood Potts models and inverse-covariance estimators remain invariant under this transformation, but pLMs fail the test. Across multiple architectures (ESM-2, MSA Transformer, AMPLIFY) and model scales (8M to 3B parameters), pLM contact maps collapse under arbitrary relabeling. Using Hamming distance controls and biochemical grouping interventions, we attempt to isolate what likely causes this degradation and find that these models rely on explicit, canonical residue identities (``alphabet anchoring'') rather than pure coevolutionary statistics. Increasing scale makes models more responsive to biochemical classes, but their performance still degrades under relabeling. Our findings show a gap between how pLMs and classical coevolutionary models compute contacts. More broadly, our work shows how diagnostic perturbations that leave classical models provably unchanged can expose hidden representation biases and prevent over-reliance on neural black boxes in biological modeling.


### arXiv 2611.16879 - Context Graph: Explainable Multi-Agent Decision Chains in AI-RAN

- categories: cs.NI, cs.AI, cs.IT
- published: 2026-11-12T14:43:22Z
- authors: Xijun Wang, Zhaoyang Liu, Xiang Chen, Howard H. Yang, Chenyuan Feng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16879

AI-RAN distributes network control across multiple autonomous agents, namely xApps and rApps operating over shared radio resources. When an alert or degradation appears, operators currently lack a structured way to trace which decision caused it, what context triggered that decision, and how other agents interacted. Existing explainability tools focus on single-model feature attribution or linear event logging, neither of which captures cross-agent decision chains. We propose the Context Graph, a heterogeneous directed graph that organizes policies, measurements, decisions, and alerts into a queryable explanation layer. Its foundational primitive is the Decision-Event node acting as a causal junction, where incoming edges capture triggering context and outgoing edges track downstream consequences that are progressively upgraded through counterfactual verification. Instantiated over existing E2, A1, and O1 data flows, our proof-of-concept demonstrates that compliance and diagnosis queries are resolved at sub-millisecond latency over graphs of tens of thousands of nodes, while reliably distinguishing true causal impacts from spurious correlations.


### arXiv 2611.16871 - DPA: Decentralized Primal Averaging with Quasi-Global Momentum for Highly Heterogeneous Data

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T14:38:34Z
- authors: Diyako Ghaderyan, Stefan Werner
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16871

Decentralized training of deep learning models keeps training data local and supports on-device learning over networks. In realistic scenarios, heterogeneity across agents' local data poses an optimization challenge and can severely degrade test accuracy, especially on sparse communication graphs. Building on recent advances in centralized primal averaging, we propose Decentralized Primal Averaging (DPA), a decentralized learning algorithm that combines primal averaging with a local quasi-global momentum buffer. DPA maintains three coupled sequences for optimization, gradient evaluation, and output averaging, while using consecutive mixed iterates to construct a network-aware momentum direction without transmitting an auxiliary tracking variable. For smooth nonconvex objectives, we establish a convergence bound for DPA and identify conditions for linear speedup in the number of agents for sufficiently many iterations. We also introduce DPA-1G, a variant that uses one communication round per iteration and matches the per-iteration communication cost of DSGD-style baselines. Experiments following the GUT decentralized image-classification benchmark show that DPA compares favorably with DSGD, gradient tracking, QG-DSGDm, momentum tracking, and QG-GUTm across datasets, architectures, topologies, and heterogeneity levels, with the largest gains under high heterogeneity on sparse rings. At the same total communication cost, both DPA variants achieve higher mean test accuracy than the best baseline on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with ResNet-20. Our code is publicly available.


### arXiv 2611.16831 - A Modular Framework for ESG Prediction through Domain-Specific Language Modeling and Graph Learning

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T14:02:59Z
- authors: Sofia Baiocco, Gianluca Bonifazi, Christopher Buratti, Michele Marchetti, Camilla Mazzoli, Federica Parlapiano
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16831

Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) scores are increasingly used to evaluate corporate sustainability, supporting investment decisions, ensuring regulatory compliance and assessing risk. However, current ESG evaluations largely rely on proprietary methodologies that often produce inconsistent ratings. This motivates the development of data-driven prediction frameworks. In this paper, we present ESGPred, a modular framework for predicting ESG scores by combining domain-specific language modeling with graph-based learning over a network that models the relationships among the companies being evaluated. In addition to presenting the rationale behind ESGPred and detailing its ESG score prediction process, we show that ESGPred can effectively predict both overall ESG ratings and their individual Environmental, Social, and Governance components. We evaluated ESGPred using a dataset of 5,266 sustainability reports published by 2,134 publicly listed companies between 2020 and 2023. In this evaluation, we used ESG ratings provided by LSEG Data & Analytics as ground truth. Our experimental results demonstrated that integrating graph-based relational learning improves prediction performance over purely text-based baselines.


### arXiv 2611.16767 - Evaluating Video LLMs' Understanding of Corner Cases in Autonomous Driving

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T13:15:27Z
- authors: Gabriel Manalu, Minh Son Hoang, Hyeontaek Hwang, Jaesung Lee, Hyojin Choi, Tran Tuan Phong
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16767

Natural language explanations promised by modern Video Large Language Models (Video LLMs) offer autonomous driving systems a path toward detailed scene description and interpretable decision making on the road. While recent benchmarks have begun assessing these models on driving scenes, they mainly target routine traffic conditions and general comprehension tasks based on existing driving datasets and therefore fail to evaluate model performance on the rarest and most safety-critical driving events, also known as "corner cases". To provide a comprehensive evaluation of Video LLMs on precisely these scenarios, we introduce DashBench, a benchmark testing 25 Video LLMs on 1,709 carefully curated driving videos spanning not only collision-related corner cases but also rare non-collision ones. Through extensive prompts and open-ended question answering, we expose a critical capability gap: all evaluated models perform substantially below the threshold required for safety-critical driving applications, frequently misjudging or missing dangerous events on the road ahead and hallucinating non-existent agents or actions in their explanations. We believe DashBench, combining an extensive dataset with a rigorous evaluation toolkit and a detailed diagnosis of current model limitations, drives progress toward safer, more explainable autonomous driving systems. The code and data will be shared at: cornercase-dashbench.github.io


### arXiv 2611.16752 - AgriSense: Vision-Language Decision Support and Robotic Intervention in a Virtual Greenhouse Copy title

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-12T13:04:57Z
- authors: Lyes Saad Saoud, Oualid Doukhi, Reza Ghorbani, Moussa Ayyash
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16752

Autonomous greenhouse management increasingly requires robotic systems that integrate perception, navigation, environmental regulation, and plant-level intervention. This paper presents AgriSense, a framework combining an Isaac Sim virtual greenhouse, a quadruped mobile manipulator, RGB and LiDAR-inertial sensing, environmental control, and supervised vision-language decision support. Frozen DINOv2 and BERT encoders, together with structured environmental inputs, support joint prediction of twelve environmental references, a target zone, and an intervention flag. Checkpoint replay reproduces 41/43 zone decisions. Across five seeds on a source-grouped test set, the full, image-removed, and language-removed configurations each achieve 43/44 correct zone decisions, providing no evidence of incremental multimodal zone-accuracy improvement. The full model achieves mean absolute errors of 0.249 degrees C for temperature, 2.030 percentage points for humidity, and 0.997 percentage points for soil moisture. Sensor-only inputs match full-model zone accuracy, while language-only inputs achieve 68.64%. External real-image benchmarks reveal distribution-shift limitations. Five offline Point-LIO routes yield rigidly aligned trajectory errors of 5.22-17.32 mm. Of 40 virtual-arm targets, 32 satisfy geometric acceptance criteria and eight are rejected at boundaries. Six long-horizon climate cases and fault protocols characterize integration limits. Navigation and integrated treatment rely on simulator-state inputs; physical greenhouse performance and agronomic treatment efficacy remain unvalidated. The contribution is an evaluated decision-support and robotic-intervention framework for a virtual greenhouse.


### arXiv 2611.16749 - ORCA:Orthogonal Residual Consensus Alignment for Multi-View Clustering

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T13:00:39Z
- authors: Xiaojian Ding, Xian Li, Xiaoying Zhu, Lin Zhao, Kaixiang Wang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16749

Deep multi-view clustering often suffers from redundant information accumulation during fusion and indiscriminate alignment across representation levels. We propose Orthogonal Residual Consensus Alignment (ORCA) to address these challenges. Its semantic incremental recurrent fusion module extracts cross-view orthogonal residuals as non-redundant increments and adaptively weights recurrent states through divergence-guided gating. Its consensus-calibrated hierarchical contrastive alignment module uses the global consensus to weight contrastive alignment of view-level, pairwise, and recurrent representations. Together, these modules balance view-specific structure with cross-view consistency. Experiments on five benchmarks against fifteen competing methods demonstrate ORCA's effectiveness, including a 5.25 percent accuracy improvement over the strongest baseline on 100leaves. Ablation studies validate both modules. Code is available at https://github.com/XiaojianDing/2026-NeurIPS-ORCA.


### arXiv 2611.16748 - PhysLoop: Harnessing LLMs' Physics Awareness for Self-Improving Video Generation Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:59:35Z
- authors: Yuxuan Chen, Xiaojie Li, Hao Chen, Juncheng Ma, Kewei Zhang, Zhongjing Du
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16748

Video generation models produce visually realistic videos but still struggle with physical dynamics and causal consistency. Learning from generated videos requires fine-grained physical supervision, yet reliable quality judgments, correction pairs, and controlled variations are difficult to obtain. Large language models (LLMs) possess physical knowledge, but struggle to reliably guide video generation models in producing their training videos. We present PhysLoop, a unified harness designed to support physically grounded self-improvement in video generation models. The harness guides and constrains LLMs throughout video generation, selection, and correction, distilling their physical knowledge into physically consistent videos. The framework supports controllable training-data construction and inference-time video repair. By identifying and correcting failures, PhysLoop marks a key milestone toward recursive self-improvement (RSI) in video generation models with limited human intervention. We release 100K videos spanning diverse, realistic indoor and outdoor scenes, along with generation tools and two evaluation datasets: PhysLoop-Gen and PhysLoop-Repair. We evaluate controllable generation and localized repair on these datasets, respectively, and assess physical behavior on the external Physics-IQ Verified benchmark.


### arXiv 2611.16744 - Scale-Induced Semantic Shift in Visual Prompt Injection Against VLMs and Web Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:57:11Z
- authors: Yiheng Zhao, Runze Ji, Chenyu Yi, Yi Yu, Bingquan Shen, Alex Kot
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16744

Visual content in multimedia systems may be resized, compressed, or re-rendered before it is interpreted by a vision-language model. We study a security failure mode in which these transformations change the machine-readable semantics of an image: a textual instruction is not recovered at a $512\times512$ presentation resolution under our evaluation protocol but becomes recoverable after aggressive downsampling. We formulate this behavior as Scale-Induced Semantic Shift (SISS) and develop a closed-loop black-box framework that combines dual-scale verification, selective self-hardening, shallow-layer low-rank adaptation, and pixel-space noise regularization. On a fixed 300-case benchmark, the third SISS refinement round obtains a $58.75\%$ average attack success rate across four closed-source vision-language models, compared with $41.08\%$ for the strongest generation baseline. A controlled sweep bounds the effect to aggressive compression: average success decreases to $17.68\%$, $6.85\%$, and $5.60\%$ at $128\times128$, $224\times224$, and $336\times336$, respectively. In a downstream web-agent study, the generated images increase hidden-instruction perception, but absolute behavioral compromise remains about $4\%$. Generic input transformations provide little protection in a separate defense protocol, whereas the proposed ScaleGuard consistency detector reduces non-adaptive success from $61.4\%$ to $24.3\%$ and one-round defense-aware success from $42.5\%$ to $15.0\%$, with no observed rejection among matched clean controls. The results characterize a bounded but reproducible semantic inconsistency in multimedia preprocessing and motivate scale-consistent visual-language inference.


### arXiv 2611.16728 - EgoX: Egocentric Cross-Embodiment Manipulation with Embodiment Dreaming

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-12T12:40:24Z
- authors: Yaru Niu, Binghong Chen, Changwei Yao, Shangtao Li, Zhenlong Fang, Revanth Krishna Senthilkumaran
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16728

Generalist manipulation policies can benefit from the complementary experience of humans, real robots, and simulated agents. However, differences in morphology, sensing, and control interfaces make it difficult to learn effectively from their combined demonstrations. We introduce EgoX, an egocentric cross-embodiment learning framework that organizes heterogeneous demonstrations through semantically corresponding observation and action modalities. EgoX pretrains a Modularized Cross-Embodiment Transformer with Embodiment Dreaming (MXT+) on this mixture and fine-tunes it on target-embodiment demonstrations. Within each embodiment, MXT+ assigns a dedicated tokenizer to each observation modality and a dedicated expert to each action modality, with a Transformer trunk shared across embodiments. A training-only embodiment-dreaming objective predicts latent representations of future visual and proprioceptive states, encouraging the shared trunk to model each embodiment's dynamics. Across 13 task-embodiment pairs spanning five tasks and six robot embodiments, including five physical robots and one simulated humanoid, cross-embodiment pretraining raises MXT+'s average normalized task progress from 0.63 to 0.80 over training from scratch. The gains are particularly pronounced under out-of-distribution conditions, where average task progress increases from 0.53 to 0.77. Ablations demonstrate the benefits of broader pretraining mixtures, modular interfaces, and embodiment dreaming, with dreaming producing more similar latent geometry across embodiments. Project page: egox-dream.github.io.


### arXiv 2611.16727 - From Foundation VLM to Versatile Driving VLM: A Triplet Incremental Training Approach

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:39:25Z
- authors: Shengtao Wu, Zichao Dong, Weixuan Qiao, Xiaolu Liu, Xin Zhan, Junbo Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16727

Adapting general Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to Autonomous Driving (AD) is thought to require heavy 3D spatial priors or intrusive architectural overhauls to compensate for a lack of spatial grounding. In this work, we present a counterintuitive pilot study: off-the-shelf general VLMs already exhibit remarkable adaptability across diverse driving tasks, while precise metric depth remains the primary missing link, a bottleneck readily addressable via onboard sensors and depth foundation models. To this end, we introduce the Triplet Incremental Training Framework, a non-intrusive paradigm that seamlessly instills 3D geometric awareness into a general VLM backbone while fully preserving its open-world capabilities. Driven by automatically constructed (image, depth, QA pair) triplets, our framework operates via a unified tri-stream training pipeline: an unfrozen primary VLM stream to adapt to driving tasks, an auxiliary depth stream that guides the visual backbone to implicitly imagine 3D scene geometry via explicit geometric constraints, and a frozen twin VLM stream employing logit-level KL distillation to prevent semantic drift. Crucially, the auxiliary depth branch is discarded during deployment, ensuring the enhanced geometric awareness comes with zero inference overhead. We evaluate our framework across four major benchmarks targeting four diverse capabilities spanning 3D spatial perception, driving-oriented language reasoning, end-to-end planning, and general multimodal understanding. Our approach outperforms state-of-the-art driving VLMs, rivals domain-specific expert models in 3D perception, and successfully retains its original general reasoning capabilities. This demonstrates that Triplet Incremental Training offers a unified, deployment-ready paradigm for transforming general foundation models into robust driving intelligences.


### arXiv 2611.16726 - From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:38:36Z
- authors: Afonso Eduardo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16726

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of ten Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology.


### arXiv 2611.16722 - Mind the Accuracy-Approval Gap: How Users Evaluate Sycophantic AI in Everyday Tasks

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:35:03Z
- authors: Wajiha Naveed, Brandon Fain
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16722

Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, a tendency to agree with users, flatter or affirm them, and preserve their face, even when independent judgment or critical feedback would be more useful. Prior work has largely evaluated sycophancy through benchmark evaluations, but less is known about how users perceive and respond to sycophantic AI in everyday tasks. We combine benchmark evaluations with two large-scale user studies. We study four personas defined through system prompts: objective (instructed to prioritize accuracy over agreement), soft-sycophantic, hard-sycophantic, and baseline (no persona-specific system prompt). Benchmarks show that even baseline exhibits substantial sycophancy, agreeing with users' stated views 71.7% of the time on NLP Survey, compared with 54.8% for objective and 89.5% for hard-sycophantic. Under conversational pressure on TruthfulQA, accuracy ranged from 81.9% for objective to 59.6% for baseline and 8.7% for hard-sycophantic. Participants were assigned to one of the four personas, and either discussed contested policy topics or collaborated with AI on email writing. Despite its higher accuracy under pressure, objective AI was rated significantly less respectful and satisfying than the other personas, and less trustworthy than baseline. Baseline and sycophantic personas did not differ significantly. Persona did not significantly affect how often participants changed their opinions, although participants who discussed with objective AI had greater odds of reduced confidence. Objective AI prompted greater writing expansion than soft-sycophantic AI, but final writing quality did not differ significantly across personas. These findings highlight a tension between accuracy and user approval: personas with substantially lower accuracy under conversational pressure received more favorable ratings for respect and satisfaction than objective AI.


### arXiv 2611.16718 - MusicRWKV: A Multimodal RWKV Framework for Music Representation Learning

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI, eess.AS
- published: 2026-11-12T12:34:08Z
- authors: Terry Tan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16718

Recent music foundation models have achieved remarkable progress in music representation learning, yet most existing approaches rely on Transformer architectures whose quadratic attention complexity introduces substantial computational and memory overhead when modeling long-duration music recordings. In this work, we propose MusicRWKV, a multimodal music representation learning framework built upon the RWKV architecture. MusicRWKV jointly models waveform, spectrogram, and metadata information within a unified self-supervised learning framework. To improve efficiency for long musical sequences, we introduce an invertible compression module that reduces sequence length while preserving reconstruction capability. A FiLM-based conditioning strategy is employed to facilitate modality-aware representation learning without introducing expensive cross-attention operations. We further investigate the integration of Soft ROSA as a differentiable retrieval mechanism for neural-symbolic interaction. Experimental evaluation across a diverse collection of Music Information Retrieval (MIR) benchmarks demonstrates that the proposed RWKV-based architecture can be effectively adapted to heterogeneous music understanding tasks. A controlled pretraining ablation shows that Soft ROSA improves validation performance, while layer-wise telemetry analysis reveals a progressive transition from distributed retrieval to selective retrieval in deeper network layers. These results suggest that RWKV provides a promising architectural alternative to Transformer-based models for multimodal music understanding.


### arXiv 2611.16710 - Acoustic Respiratory Phase Detection on Mobile Devices

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-12T12:26:15Z
- authors: Julian Szymanski, Tomasz Sankowski, Piotr Sulewski, Iwo Czartowski, Javier Medina Quero
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16710

Breathing monitoring is important for well-being, stress regulation, and guided breathing exercises, yet existing approaches often rely on contact-based sensors or controlled environments. This paper presents a non-invasive framework for respiratory phase detection using acoustic signals recorded on commodity smartphones. The proposed hybrid CNN-Transformer architecture combines local spectral feature extraction with long-range temporal modeling to capture the cyclic structure of breathing. The model is evaluated using a Leave-One-Subject-Out (LOSO) protocol on the Foundation Dataset and on an independent unseen test set comprising over 1300 breathing cycles and 2 hours of continuous recordings. The proposed 3-class formulation achieves 72.85% frame-level accuracy and 96.6% cycle-level exhalation detection efficiency in the LOSO evaluation, while achieving 76.94% frame-level accuracy, a Cohen's Kappa score of 0.65, and 99.2% cycle-level exhalation detection efficiency on the unseen test set. Results suggest that accurate respiratory monitoring may be achievable under data-constrained conditions, indicating that appropriate temporal modeling can learn transferable respiratory representations and generalize to previously unseen participants. An end-to-end ONNX deployment pipeline enables privacy-preserving on-device inference with latency below 20 ms, supporting real-time monitoring on mobile and wearable devices.


### arXiv 2611.16694 - When Expert Disagreement Hurts: Prestige and Selectivity in LLM Decision Revision

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-12T12:11:03Z
- authors: Yupeng Tang, Mingfeng Lin
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16694

When language models revise a decision after another agent disagrees, neither reversal counts nor final accuracy shows whether they are fixing mistakes or abandoning correct answers. We introduce a controlled two-pass audit that holds the question, the evidence, and the model's initial answer fixed and varies only the revision context: neutral reflection, anonymous disagreement, or disagreement attributed to a domain expert. For binary decisions the accounting is exact: revision raises accuracy only if it is selective enough, reversing wrong answers more than $a/(1-a)$ times as often as correct ones, where $a$ is initial accuracy (nine times at 90%). Across five models and 1,989 evidence-grounded decisions in medicine, science, and law, no model clears this bar under expert disagreement, and accuracy falls by up to 16.1 points. The expert label, a prestige cue, adds reversals for four of the five models, yet its effect on accuracy ranges from a 5.2-point loss to a 2.4-point gain, so reversal counts are a poor proxy for harm. With identical arguments from both sources, the label still raises reversal for three proprietary models and in a pooled analysis of four newer models. Models also respond to stated source reliability. Revision pipelines should therefore be evaluated with paired, conditional metrics, per model and protocol.


### arXiv 2611.16693 - MobileProbe: Adaptive Security Evaluation of Mobile Agents via Trajectory-Aware Injection

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T12:08:43Z
- authors: Yingbin Jin, Ji'an Wang, HaoYang Chai, Hanjun Luo, Li Hu, Xinfeng Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16693

Mobile GUI agents read the screen of a phone and operate apps on a user's behalf to complete multi-step tasks such as online shopping or messaging. Because the agent acts on whatever the screen shows, an attacker can embed malicious instructions into ordinary UI content and redirect its actions. We refer to this threat as environmental injection attack (EIA). Existing benchmarks measure this risk by pinning hand-crafted payloads to a fixed step of a fixed task. This approach faces two limitations. First, each payload is bound to one task in one app and does not transfer without manual redesign. Second, agents given the same task may follow different routes, so an agent that never reaches the injected page is recorded as safe without being tested. This insufficient test coverage can cause fixed-point benchmarks to underestimate the security risk of deployed mobile agents. In this work, we propose MobileProbe, a trajectory-aware red-teaming framework that evaluates mobile agents adaptively. MobileProbe discovers attacker-controllable content surfaces through automated pentesting, localizes vulnerable decision points along the agent's trajectory, and iteratively refines context-aware payloads through a surrogate-filtered evolutionary loop. We construct a benchmark of 83 long-horizon attack instances spanning 12 mobile applications. Each is annotated with a Confidentiality, Integrity, or Availability (CIA) attack goal to cover the principal categories of security threats facing mobile agents. On three representative agent architectures, MobileProbe attains higher attack success rates than human-written, zero-shot LLM, and prior automated red-teaming baselines adapted to the mobile domain. MobileProbe can serve as a pre-deployment auditing tool to systematically uncover vulnerabilities and inform security hardening of mobile agents.


### arXiv 2611.16678 - Edge-Gradient Augmentation under Reinforcement Learning for Temporal Graph

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:53:35Z
- authors: Yongqing Zhou, Jiawei Chen, Wenjun Wang, Xinyu Wang, Wei Yu, Qiyao Peng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16678

Temporal link prediction is critical for understand- ing dynamic interactions in evolving networks, informing appli- cations ranging from social network analysis to recommendation systems. Existing methods typically rely on raw temporal graph data, often neglecting explicit edge-change information that can significantly improve predictive performance. To address this gap, we propose edge-gradient reinforced learning (EG-RL), a framework that explicitly encodes edge-gradient events (edge births and deaths) through dedicated node and link encoding modules. EG-RL uses a policy-driven reinforcement-learning mechanism to dynamically select and augment the most infor- mative edge snapshots, guided by critic-based feedback to opti- mize prediction accuracy. The model comprises distinct modules for node encoding, link encoding, token mixing, and channel mixing, integrated through global pooling and followed by a two-layer multilayer perceptron classifier. Extensive experiments on multiple dynamic graph datasets demonstrate substantial gains in area under the receiver operating characteristic curve and average precision. Ablation studies further confirm the necessity of adaptive edge-gradient augmentation and validate the effectiveness of the proposed encoding and reinforcement- learning mechanisms.


### arXiv 2611.16666 - Natural Gradient Learning in Predictive Coding through Lateral Circuits

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:42:17Z
- authors: Lhea Beumer, Sander de Haan, Niclas Pokel, Pau Vilimelis Aceituno, Benjamin F. Grewe
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16666

Predictive coding (PC) trains neural networks through local energy-minimization dynamics without depending on a global error signal that backpropagation (BP) requires. Although PC is a promising alternative algorithm for efficient learning, the computational cost of inference dynamics currently outweighs the resulting learning benefits in practice. In this work, we take inspiration from optimization theory to extract more learning benefit from each inference phase. We introduce Natural PC, which augments standard PC with a lateral circuit, enabling exact and efficient natural gradient learning locally in space and time. Our lateral circuit learns the activity correlations that determine the Fisher information matrix and implicitly computes its inverse, avoiding expensive matrix inversion. Across all tested image reconstruction benchmarks, Natural PC achieves state of the art in terms of sample and compute efficiency compared to existing PC approaches. Most notably, Natural PC scales to ImageNet and matches the performance of BP trained with AdamW and various second-order optimizers at an order of magnitude lower computational cost while also using two orders of magnitude fewer training samples. Our results demonstrate that local natural gradient learning makes PC competitive with BP in both sample efficiency and computational cost in reconstruction tasks.


### arXiv 2611.16662 - MuSeToM: Multi-Sensory Evaluation of Belief Revision with Textual Cues Across Six Senses

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:38:52Z
- authors: Chunkit Chan, Kawai Chung, Sze Heng Douglas Kwok, Yuxuan Liu, Yauwai Yim, Tianshi Zheng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16662

Theory of Mind (ToM), the ability to infer mental states in others, is pivotal for human social cognition. Existing evaluations of ToM in large language models (LLMs) mainly focus on static belief attribution and false-belief reasoning, leaving a critical aspect of social reasoning underexplored: can LLMs decide whether perceptual evidence is sufficient to justify another person's belief revision? To address this gap, we present MuSeToM, a human-validated, text-based benchmark for evaluating epistemic belief revision in ToM reasoning. MuSeToM contains 2,415 instances adapted from BigToM and OpenToM, with textual cues spanning six sensory categories, and tests whether models can determine when a protagonist should revise a belief and infer the resulting updated belief state. Using MuSeToM, we systematically evaluate state-of-the-art LLMs under direct and chain-of-thought prompting, revealing a pronounced dissonance: while models often detect that a belief may change, they struggle to identify the updated belief and frequently over-update on perceptually salient but epistemically insufficient cues.


### arXiv 2611.16661 - Reducing Batch Makespan for LLM Agent Workloads through Inference-Environment Coordination

- categories: cs.DC, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:38:21Z
- authors: Wei Feng, Meiling Chen, Boxiao Chen, Lu Liu, Bin Zi, Libin Ye
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16661

Efficient batch execution of agent tasks is critical to LLM training and development workflows, including benchmark evaluation, synthetic-data generation, and RL rollouts. Since these tasks alternate between LLM inference and environment interaction, we present coordinated optimizations to reduce batch makespan, the time required to complete all tasks in a batch. For hybrid LLMs, a workload-based resource model guides GPU-memory allocation between key-value (KV) cache and recurrent state and provides a reference sample concurrency to exploit parallelism while limiting recomputation. Trajectory-aware routing and scheduling promote cache reuse while balancing worker load. Under constrained environment capacity, we decouple sample admission from environment execution and introduce Chain-Advancing Action Scheduling (CAAS), which prioritizes long chains using observed progress to shorten the batch tail. Controlled replay combines actual inference with recorded environment responses and durations for ablations of individual optimizations. Live evaluations with Ling-3.0-Flash and Ling-3.1-Flash on the BFCL, SWE-bench Multilingual, WideSearch, and MCP-Atlas benchmarks demonstrate makespan reductions of 10.4% to 65.0%, while evaluation scores remain aligned. Furthermore, these methods have been successfully applied to training and evaluation workflows for the Ling-3.x model series, accelerating the model-development cycle.


### arXiv 2611.16647 - InertiaMPDD: Multimodal Personalized Depression Detection via Emotional Inertia-Inspired Dynamics Modeling

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:22:19Z
- authors: Changzeng Fu, Songyang Wang, Chang Gao, Xinyi Zhang, Peilin Li, Mo Meng
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16647

Multimodal depression screening estimates risk from speech, visual behavior and language. Current methods mainly predict labels, with limited evidence of departures from a non-depressed reference. Recording-level supervision also leaves fine-grained emotional dynamics unannotated. Motivated by emotional inertia, the persistence of affect, InertiaMPDD learns healthy next-state patterns using a world-model-inspired latent predictor. Using pretrained multimodal representations, it predicts from observed history and context. Reference deviation, residual decay rate and a Jacobian spectral descriptor form reference-derived emotional dynamics descriptors, evaluated as candidate proxy biomarkers for standalone screening and personalized decision correction. Across five cohorts from four datasets, fusion reaches 98.41% F1 on D-Vlog and 84.98% on LMVD. Standalone descriptors outperform fixed additive fusion on MPDD but discriminate weakly on AVEC2014. These findings distinguish reference informativeness from fusion benefit and reveal context-dependent utility.


### arXiv 2611.16642 - Dataset Distillation via Drifting

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-12T11:17:40Z
- authors: George Cazenavette, Angelina Quan, Giannis Daras, Antonio Torralba, Vincent Sitzmann
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16642

The task of dataset distillation concerns the learning of a small set of synthetic samples that can serve as an effective replacement for a large real dataset when used for downstream tasks. While many solutions have been proposed since the problem's inception, most of them primarily seek to optimize the distilled data with respect to a classifier. In this work, we propose a new distillation method that learns a set of samples such that they directly approximate the per-class data distribution. Specifically, we "drift" our distilled data in the latent space of a feature extractor such that they move both toward the real data and away from each other without using a classification objective. We find that our drifted data cover the real distribution significantly better than existing methods while still serving as powerful training samples for classification. Furthermore, we showcase our method's use as a visualization tool for better interpreting models and extend it beyond images to other modalities. Our work advances the frontier of dataset distillation, offering a novel synthesis method and proposing distribution-based metrics as a complementary evaluation. More broadly, our findings help address the growing need to understand both data and models by uncovering more about how they interact.


### arXiv 2611.16630 - When Chekhov's Gun Fires Anyway: Over-Realization in Text-to-Image Models

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T11:03:25Z
- authors: Jiahao Huang, Kon Woo Kim, Song-Duo Ma, Yuka Kitamura, Felix Wagner, Yunshan Huang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16630

In text-to-image generation, a target that the prompt mentions often appears in the generated image in a way the prompt does not call for. We call this failure over-realization. Mental-spaces theory points to the situations where it can happen: under a negation, inside what a character believes or imagines, as the vehicle of a figurative comparison, or behind a viewpoint that hides the target. Prior work has studied only negation in depth, and the conventional alignment and preference metrics do not penalize the failure. We build OverReal to measure it: OverReal-Gen, 698 prompts covering the four situations, on which we measure each generator's over-realization rate; and OverReal-Det, 2,841 generated images human-labeled for whether and how the target is over-realized, on which we verify the automatic judge that produces this rate. Our evaluation of eight text-to-image systems reveals that all of them over-realize, and that conventional metrics such as CLIPScore reward the failure rather than penalize it. Prompt expansion only partly mitigates the failure. Inside the generator, the diffusion backbone attends to a mentioned target almost as much as to a requested one. The cue words that should hold the target back receive less attention than ordinary words.


### arXiv 2611.16628 - Safety as a Budget: Calibrating the Control Channel of Constrained GRPO for Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-12T11:00:14Z
- authors: Bo Han
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16628

Reinforcement learning can teach vision-language models (VLMs) to decline harmful image-text requests, but how safe the result becomes is set indirectly, through reward weights found by trial runs. Stating safety as budgets on the rate of harmful assistance and the rate of over-refusal, enforced by Lagrange multipliers inside GRPO with an off-the-shelf scorer as the cost, is the natural fix, and it fails twice. The multiplier compares the scorer's mean with the budget, and scorers built for ranking get the mean wrong: a fixed-point analysis shows that the mean bias, not the budget, then decides whether the multiplier settles, saturates or switches off. And standard GRPO standardizes the penalized reward within each group, which cancels the multiplier before it reaches the gradient. Two-channel constrained GRPO repairs both: the base reward keeps the raw score for the within-group comparison, the penalty and the constraint estimate pass through a monotone map fitted on judge-labeled training responses, and the advantage keeps the multiplier. On a Qwen3-VL-2B policy with a training-free readout of an open 4B model as the scorer, held-out harm on 1,037 adversarial prompts falls from 8.9% under standard GRPO to 4.8%, lower in every seed, where the uncalibrated budget gives 7.6%; over-refusal stays at most 2% on 400 benign queries and MMBench is unchanged. The gain is largest where the intent sits in the image (10.1% -> 5.1%) and grows on the unseen HADES benchmark (28.9% -> 12.8%). Order-preserving distortions of the scores reproduce each predicted controller state in every seed, and the results hold under a map fitted on another judge's labels, an image-reading judge and a human audit.


### arXiv 2611.16627 - Folio: Recoverable Multi-Resolution Context Folding for Long-Horizon Language Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T10:59:40Z
- authors: Yubo Ruan, Tao Peng, Jiao Zhang, Chuanguo Ma, Lei Xu, Han Zhou
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16627

Long-horizon language agents accumulate tool results and replies until the history no longer fits the context window. Current agents shrink this history by having a model summarize it, so each compaction rereads and rewrites the history it replaces, and cache-aware triggers decide when to compact while taking the size of the result as given. We present Folio, a context manager that records, folds, and recovers history through one set of handles. While the agent runs, Folio records three linked levels: a short turn note the model writes as part of its ordinary reply (L1), an event manifest of every call written by the runtime (L2), and the captured source records (L3). Because these views already exist, the runtime folds the context programmatically, selecting coarser views for older turns. The fold prints the handles that index L2 and L3, so recovery tools that read bounded pages, compute over full text in files, and keep findings in a persistent dossier reach the evidence the agent needs with little added input. The fold trigger prices an explicit landing point that includes the retained budget. On 75 LOCA-bench tasks with DeepSeek-V4-Pro behind every system, Folio solves 66 tasks against 54 for the same runtime with model-written compaction and 49-59 for Claude Code, Codex, and SoL-Pi, in about half their working time, at 2.4 times lower cost than that runtime; each fold takes a median of 0.6 seconds.


### arXiv 2611.16621 - Studies on Sign Language Retrieval in Low-Resource Dictionary Settings

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T10:57:50Z
- authors: Ushnish Sarkar, Vineet Kumar Rakesh, Bhaswar Chattopadhyay, Tapas Samanta
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16621

Most sign languages have no annotated corpus, only small dictionaries with one or a few example videos per sign. This study examines how well a pose encoder trained on a single sign language serves such languages without any target-language training. An encoder trained with metric learning on American Sign Language (ASL-Citizen) is evaluated frozen on six datasets of Turkish, Russian, Indian, Argentinian, Pakistani and Sri Lankan sign languages, with one or four clips per sign enrolled and, where signer identities are available, the query's signer excluded from the gallery. With one enrolled clip, the encoder retrieves the correct sign first in 36.4-80.7 % of queries, against 16.0-69.6 % for the best learning-free method (angle-based dynamic time warping) and a six-target mean of 39.3 % for an ASL-trained OpenHands graph network. In a crossed ablation of the encoder (embedding geometry, aggregation of body and hand embeddings, training objective, and attention between articulators), two choices matter: a per-pair InfoNCE objective outperforms supervised contrastive and cross-entropy training, and blocking attention between body, left hand and right hand inside the encoder raises accuracy by up to 5.1 points. The best configuration reaches 68.0 % one-shot accuracy (59.0 % for the reference encoder). On CISLR, an Indian Sign Language dictionary of 4,765 words with one video per word, the encoders find the correct word first for 17.3-19.0 % of queries, above the 16.8 % of the original RGB baseline. Over eight seeds with the joint encoder, spherical, Euclidean and hyperbolic embedding spaces differ by at most 1.6 points, except the hyperbolic token-attention head trained with InfoNCE, which is 1.4-2.0 points worse, so the geometry is a minor factor.


### arXiv 2611.16609 - A Dual-Branch Graph and Convolutional Network for Interpretable Movement EEG Classification

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.LG, eess.SP
- published: 2026-11-12T10:47:37Z
- authors: Maryam Ostadsharif Memar, Behzad Nazari
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16609

Decoding upper-limb movements from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) is an important problem for brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), but many deep learning models provide limited insight into the brain regions underlying their predictions. We propose a two-branch architecture that combines an LGGNet-inspired local-global graph branch with an EEGNet-style convolutional branch for movement-versus-rest classification. The graph branch groups 61 EEG electrodes into anatomically informed regions and learns their spatial relationships, providing a direct representation of the regions emphasized by the model. We evaluate the proposed architecture using leave-one-subject-out cross-validation on the Ofner upper-limb movement dataset. The model achieves a mean F1-score of 0.74 +/- 0.06 across subjects. Beyond classification performance, we analyze the learned graph structure to identify the regions with the strongest connectivity. Across all held-out subjects, the highest connectivity is consistently observed in the Parietal Left Low region, corresponding to electrodes P1 and P3. This region is opposite to the performed right-arm movements and lies within the posterior parietal area previously associated with upper-limb movement processing. These results show that incorporating a region-based graph into the decoder can provide an anatomically interpretable representation while maintaining effective movement classification, offering a practical approach toward more transparent EEG-based BCIs.


### arXiv 2611.16591 - Singularity-Bench: Can Agents Build Agents?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T10:24:51Z
- authors: Yang Zhou, Krish Agarwal, Zhuoming Chen, Zerui Wang, Zhenyu Zhang, Wenshuang Li
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16591

Frontier AI agents increasingly succeed on realistic, long-horizon tasks and are beginning to improve the systems through which they operate. Yet existing benchmarks often restrict agents to narrow action spaces, use simplified environments, or rely on verification methods that are unreliable for evaluating arbitrary agent-generated systems. We introduce Singularity-Bench, an open-ended benchmark for autonomous AI development with six end-to-end tasks spanning pre-training, mid-training, post-training, inference, the agent harness, and cluster orchestration. Each task requires agents to implement core systems from a minimal starting point and evaluates them against mature human-engineered baselines on identical hardware. Additionally, through extensive red-teaming covering 9 of the 10 categories in the OWASP Top 10 for Agentic Applications, we expose reward-hacking vulnerabilities in the conventional Harbor framework and develop Breakwater, an extension of Harbor that blocks all attacks tested in our study. Across 11 agents built from 7 frontier models and 2 harnesses, Opus 5.5 with Claude Code matches or outperforms the human baseline on all six tasks with a Singularity-Index of 1.25, suggesting it may have the skills needed to reach the singularity. The remaining agents average only 0.58. Our vision is for Singularity-Bench to track how much of AI development agents can already automate and when they may transition from assisting to taking end-to-end responsibility for building stronger successors.


### arXiv 2611.16590 - AstroMemBench: A Benchmark for Memory Systems in Embodied Spacecraft Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-12T10:23:15Z
- authors: You Wu, Guhong Zhang, Hongwei Li, Xinyue Hong, Yuan Jia
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16590

Long-term spacecraft autonomy requires embodied agents to use operational history under communication delays, time-critical operations, environmental uncertainty, complex physical interactions, power and computational constraints, and safety and reliability requirements. We introduce AstroMemBench and a Scenario-Challenge-Capability design method that maps these requirements to executable memory tests. The benchmark covers 16 memory subchallenges through 140 templates and 2240 instances across nine tasks in planetary surface operations, on-orbit servicing, and deep-space exploration. A deterministic environment scores actions for task completion, state changes, safety, and resource use. Ablations under nine conditions show that the benchmark is sensitive to how accurately agents recover and use historical information and to differences in memory-system design. Main experiments compare six configurations. Mem0, Chain-of-Memory, LightMem, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improve same-session mean scores over native Pi Agent, with no clear advantage of the three memory systems over RAG. After a session restart, Mem0 outperforms RAG by 12.25 points. Benefits favor instruction retention and result reuse over ongoing state tracking and energy reservation. AstroMemBench supports the design, development, and selection of memory systems for embodied spacecraft agents. AstroMemBench 1.0 and its evaluation code are publicly available: https://github.com/flyingView/AstroMemBench-v1.


### arXiv 2611.16586 - Large Language Models as Digital Twins: a Distribution-Aware Questionnaire-Based Evaluation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-12T10:18:16Z
- authors: Francesco Di Cursi, Chiara Boldrini, Andrea Passarella, Marco Conti
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16586

This paper investigates whether Large Language Models can answer a personality questionnaire as a specific individual would. Given a user's social-media posts, the model completes the BFI-2-60 Big Five questionnaire as that user. The predicted responses are converted into trait scores and compared with the user's actual scores. We evaluate GPT-4, Gemma, Llama 3.1, and Mistral on the MyPersonality dataset. To determine whether predictions reflect information from the user's writing, we compare them with answers produced without user text and with values derived from general population tendencies. We measure prediction error across all users and separately for individuals with low, intermediate, and high trait scores. We also test whether an LLM can express an explicit personality profile when given only the target Big Five scores, without user text, and examine how predictions change as more text is provided. The results show that user text does not consistently improve performance. Models perform better near the middle of the trait distribution, while at the lower and upper ends they often remain close to central values or perform well at one end but poorly at the other. When given an explicit profile, GPT-4 most clearly translates trait information into consistent questionnaire responses, particularly for Extraversion and Neuroticism. Increasing context does not systematically reduce error: open-source models fluctuate more, whereas GPT-4 remains comparatively stable. Overall, the evaluated models do not consistently reproduce the questionnaire behavior of specific individuals.


### arXiv 2611.16561 - SPARC: Accurate Photonic Matrix Multiplication via Representative Vectors

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:47:42Z
- authors: Yasuhiro Fujiwara, Tatsuhiro Nakamori, Hideyuki Kawashima
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16561

Photonic neural network accelerators have emerged as a promising solution for reducing the power consumption of deep neural network (DNN) inference by executing matrix multiplication on analog photonic hardware. However, their inference accuracy is limited by low-bit quantization required for photonic processing and by analog photonic noise. This paper proposes an approach for improving inference accuracy by exploiting the high similarity among activation vectors in DNNs. Specifically, our approach constructs a representative vector for each group of similar activation vectors, computes its inner product digitally with high precision, and offloads the residual inner products to the photonic accelerator. Since the activation vectors within each group are highly similar, the residual vectors have much smaller norms, thereby reducing the effects of quantization errors and analog photonic noise. Furthermore, our approach preserves the photonic inner-product computation pipeline, allowing seamless integration into existing and future photonic accelerators without hardware modification. Experimental results on six representative DNN models demonstrate that the proposed approach can improve inference accuracy across diverse network architectures and application domains.


### arXiv 2611.16527 - AI has its own style in mathematical proving

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:27:02Z
- authors: Xiaokai Li, Ziyu Zhou, Qibin Cheng, Yangcheng Wu, An Zeng, Ying Fan
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16527

As large language models increasingly generate mathematical proofs, whether they develop a proving style distinct from that of human mathematicians remains an open question. This question is particularly pressing as mathematicians increasingly voice concerns that proofs generated by large language models are difficult to interpret. Here we ask three large language models to independently reprove all 465 propositions of Euclid's \textit{Elements} and compare their citation choices against the human-proof baseline through Jaccard similarity and complex network analysis. We find that AI-generated proofs differ from human proofs, and this difference is stable and reproducible: mean pairwise Jaccard similarity among AI runs (~0.68) is nearly twice that between AI and human proofs (~0.37), and cross-run classification consistency is also high. By examining citation structure in detail, we identify the concrete signature of this difference: approximately 40% of AI citations point to foundational elements (definitions, common notions, and postulates), compared with only 13\% in human proofs; even among theorem-to-theorem citations, AI tends to cite more distant results. Roughly half of AI's extra citations trace to the upstream dependencies of the human proof. Together, these findings suggest that the AI proving style is a tendency to construct arguments on its own from foundational elements rather than directly citing later, more established results, which we term "building from the ground up". Our findings offer a new perspective on how AI systems organise and invoke mathematical knowledge, with implications for understanding the interpretability of AI-generated proofs.


### arXiv 2611.16526 - Lost in Delegation: Studying the Effectiveness of Task Orchestration in Generative Search

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-12T09:25:33Z
- authors: Tamara Stojanovska, Elisabeth Kirsten, Leon Swazinna, Muhammad Bilal Zafar
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16526

LLMs have quickly evolved from models used in isolation to agentic systems that delegate tasks to external tools and other LLMs to solve complex assignments. This agentic augmentation has led to impressive capability gains. However, when and how delegation improves performance remains poorly understood. For example, it is unclear whether delegation helps when tasks involve post-cutoff information that the orchestrating LLM cannot reliably resolve from its own knowledge. In this work, we use purpose-built datasets with pre- and post-cutoff splits to compare various delegation modes. We find that delegation provides no consistent accuracy improvement over non-delegated setups, with effects varying across models and tasks. When tasks involve knowledge unavailable to the orchestrator, delegation can substantially degrade accuracy, while direct tool use with the same LLM performs much better. Our analysis identifies distorted delegated queries, inefficient tool use, and failures to preserve and use information between agents as recurring failure modes. These findings suggest that effective orchestration requires both well-calibrated delegation decisions and effective exchange of information between agents.


### arXiv 2611.16525 - ZONOS3-Mini Technical Report

- categories: cs.SD, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:24:22Z
- authors: Gabriel Clark, Sofian Mejjoute, George Close, Beren Millidge
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16525

ZONOS3-Mini is an ultra-compact, ultra-fast, zero-shot voice-cloning TTS model. The model can run in real time on a laptop or smartphone. For its latency and size, it achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and cloning fidelity. To achieve this we propose a latent MoE backbone with only 886M parameters (100M active per token) and a new audio codec, Zyphra Audio Codec 1 (ZAC-1), which represents 44.1 kHz audio at a 15 Hz frame rate. On the new ZTTS2-Eval benchmark, ZONOS3-Mini reaches a 6.71% macro-average WER over 55 language sets (35 clean and 20 in the wild), which is competitive with larger and slower state-of-the-art models. We release the model weights, inference, and training code.


### arXiv 2611.16524 - A Joint LLM and Optimization Agent Solution for Drone Scheduling in Victim Search Missions

- categories: eess.SY, cs.AI, cs.ET
- published: 2026-11-12T09:20:54Z
- authors: Yinghao Zhang, Yalin Liu, Hong-Ning Dai, Yan Zhang, Qubeijian Wang, Bishenghui Tao
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16524

Victim search in wilderness areas is time-critical. Drones enable rapid area search, but scheduling them is challenging because victims may continue moving, detection results are uncertain, and effective decisions must combine structured mission information with unstructured contextual clues and domain knowledge. To address this, we propose an agent-aided drone scheduling system. The system employs an agent at the ground control station (GCS) to coordinate multiple drones over successive search cycles. In each cycle, the agent applies a scheduling policy that combines a large language model (LLM) for reasoning about where to search with an optimizer for determining how to dispatch the drones under energy constraints. Simulation results show that the proposed system consistently completes the victim-search mission, whereas none of the six non-LLM baselines does so and direct LLM scheduling is considerably less reliable. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/1643204431/llm-uav-victim-search.


### arXiv 2611.16523 - RESYNC: Residual Latent-State Synchronization for Deadline-Bound Streaming Inference

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:19:39Z
- authors: Eljas Linna, Hao Dong, Juho Kanniainen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16523

Predicting every observation in a high-rate stream requires a model to finish before the next arrival. A large model may offer better predictions but miss that deadline. We propose RESYNC: the large model runs asynchronously, while a fast path updates its latest exact but stale latent state using the observations received since that state was computed and applies its unchanged prediction head. The updater learns from the frozen model's representations without task labels. Across one real and two synthetic streams and five architecture families, RESYNC beats the best compact or distilled alternative with a paired 95% interval above zero in all twelve dataset--architecture pairs with a material teacher advantage at anchor ages of 4--12 observations, and in eleven pairs at older tested ages up to 32. The share of the teacher's gain preserved remains complete on the real stream but is lower and generally declines with anchor age on the synthetic streams. In live replay with 1 ms arrivals, the synchronous large models cannot deliver any predictions on time while RESYNC serves at least 99.4% of arrivals within the interval, with 0.35--0.62 ms median latency on one CPU thread: around the same speed as or faster than the compact model, and 7.9--17.7$\times$ faster than synchronous large-model inference. RESYNC targets settings with background compute available but a strict per-observation response deadline.


### arXiv 2611.16522 - PISim: A Unified Photonic Inference Simulator for Hardware-Aware Cross-Layer Design-Space Exploration

- categories: physics.optics, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:18:27Z
- authors: Andreas Falkenberg
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16522

Photonic neural accelerators offer ultra-low latency linear algebra, yet their usable numerical precision, sustainable throughput, and application accuracy remain tightly bounded by surrounding electro-optical interfaces and physical non-idealities. Pre-fabrication design-space exploration (DSE) requires a cross-layer electronic design automation (EDA) framework capable of evaluating disparate optical topologies under identical neural workloads and physical assumptions. We present \textbf{PISim}, a unified Photonic Inference Simulator that couples pretrained deep neural network execution with physics-based models of six accelerator architecture families, six electro-optic modulator technologies, four optical source organizations, and direct or coherent readout schemes. PISim embeds stage-resolved impairment operators---covering control and analog-to-digital quantization, finite extinction ratio, shot noise, correlated relative-intensity noise (RIN), thermal drift, propagation loss, and channel crosstalk---alongside an analytical cycle-level interface timing model to expose electronic-optical bottlenecks. Using simulated MNIST multilayer perceptron (MLP) benchmarks, we demonstrate how PISim identifies optimal precision operating points under multi-impairment stress (peaking at 6 bits for Mach--Zehnder and microring modulators), quantifies a 52.5 percentage-point accuracy gap between VCSEL-array and parallel-fanout laser architectures arising, under the modeled conditions, from power partitioning and correlated source noise, and models interface-limited throughput scaling across emitter-array widths from 32 to 128 channels. This comprehensive framework establishes an extensible, reproducible foundation for accuracy-constrained Pareto optimization prior to photonic tape-out.


### arXiv 2611.16502 - SignRot: LLM Quantization with Massive Outlier-Aware Sign-Adjusted Rotation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:05:14Z
- authors: Jaehun Gim, Jaewoo Kim, Gyuwan Kim, Seo Yeon Park
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16502

While quantization compresses LLMs and accelerates inference, outlier activations impede efficient low-bit representation. In particular, massive outliers cause substantial performance degradation. Although rotation-based methods that rely on randomized Hadamard transforms for online inference aim to mitigate this, they can produce fragmented distributions with empty bins when a massive outlier dominates a token's activation. This fragmentation severely undermines uniform quantization performance. Moreover, massive outliers concentrate in the down-projection input, where the preceding nonlinearity requires the rotation to be applied at inference time. This constraint prevents training-based approaches such as SpinQuant from learning the online rotation at this position, as a learned dense matrix cannot use the Fast Walsh-Hadamard Transform and would require a full matrix multiplication during inference. To address this, we propose SignRot (Sign-adjusted Rotation), which identifies massive outlier channels and constructs a sign adjustment vector that unifies the signs of the corresponding Hadamard row, collapsing the bimodal distribution into a unimodal profile amenable to quantization. Since the aligned outlier shifts the activation mean away from zero, we pair SignRot with asymmetric activation quantization, whose zero-point absorbs this shift. As a lightweight, drop-in improvement compatible with any Hadamard-based quantization method, SignRot requires only a single calibration forward pass and adds only element-wise sign flips at inference, without an additional matrix multiplication. Applying SignRot to QuaRot achieves a WikiText2 perplexity of 5.88 and an average zero-shot reasoning accuracy of 72.73, surpassing SpinQuant on the LLaMA3-70B model. Code is available at https://github.com/HYU-ARK-Lab/SignRot.


### arXiv 2611.16499 - Recalibrating a Typed-Decision Model in an Afternoon: A Hands-On Recipe for Laya's Confidence Scores

- categories: cs.AI, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-12T09:02:57Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Aliyas Ahmad Misri
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16499

A typed-decision model such as Laya reads a text and a list of options that the caller defines, for example ticket categories, and returns a probability for each option. Code around it compares the top probability with a threshold to answer, abstain or escalate to a person. Earlier work reports, for a Turkish model, that temperature scaling can raise held-out calibration error (ECE). This tutorial gives a five-step recipe (measure, diagnose, fit, validate, deploy) in 130 lines of numpy with standard methods, none new. On 1,065 synthetic, LLM-labelled items the released Laya checkpoint has a pooled ECE of 0.073 but a bucket-macro ECE of 0.143 (0.127-0.184), the mean over buckets (questions grouped by type and option count): the 12-option bucket is over-confident by +0.22, the three-to-five-option bucket under-confident by 0.05, and they cancel. One global temperature makes pooled ECE worse (0.173); a cross-fitted temperature per bucket brings macro-ECE to 0.073 (0.078 over 10 fold assignments) and reaches 0.089 with 10 labelled items per bucket, but holding out one of seven evaluation sets gives 0.099. After the refit, a gate aimed at 10% error answers 46.3% of items (10.5% wrong) against 6.9% on the published confidences, but one cut-off per bucket on the published confidences answers 45.4%, so most of the gain comes from separating buckets. Confidence-only maps (Platt, histogram, isotonic) need 20 or more labelled items per bucket to approach the temperature; with 320 per bucket the lowest macro-ECE belongs to Platt. In these files the retrained checkpoints store no per-bucket table and the stored 12-option value 0.1006 is clamped to 0.5 at runtime; the refit helps 31 of 33 checkpoints on random folds and 21 with a set held out. Fit and test share one generator and LLM labels, so these are findings about these checkpoints and this data.


### arXiv 2611.16498 - How many LLM-labelled rows does a decision encoder need, and how wrong can the labels be?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:02:55Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Aliyas Ahmad Misri
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16498

A decision encoder such as Laya reads a question with named options (for example, which department should handle a ticket) and a text, and picks an option. It reads both in one forward pass and returns a probability for every option, without writing any text. We ask how many LLM-written training rows such an encoder needs and how wrong those labels may be. We ran 19 fine-tunes of Laya (ModernBERT-large) on nested subsets of a 2,818-row pool and scored them on 1,065 held-out items from the same generator, so every test item is in-distribution and no out-of-family test was run. Accuracy rises from 69.4% before fine-tuning to 74.3% with 5% of the rows, 84.8% with 50% and 86.9% with all rows. The curve flattens, and a saturating fit with bootstrap intervals cannot pin down its plateau. Seed spread at 20% of the rows (s.d. 1.6 points) exceeds some of the steps between sizes. Giving small subsets as many updates as the full pool gains 1.9 points at 5% and 1.9 points at 20% of the rows (one seed; p=0.14 and 0.13), and at 20% that is below the 2.3-point difference between two ordinary seeds. A single run with 10% symmetric label noise shows no detectable change (-0.2 points, 95% interval -1.7 to +1.4); 20% costs 3.4 points and 40% costs 8.7 points. Confusable-pair noise at 20% costs 7.4 points, and under noise the model moves from over-confident to under-confident. An independent labeller disagrees with the gold labels on 3.5% of held-out items, and about half of its choice disagreements (16 of 33, leaving each item out of the partner map) name the partner option that the confusable-pair noise uses. Laya is +0.9 points ahead of a TF-IDF classifier trained on the same rows at full size (the interval includes zero) and +21.1 points ahead at 5% of the rows. Everything is synthetic, LLM-labelled and from one backbone, one trainer and mostly one seed.


### arXiv 2611.16493 - ReA-Bench: Benchmarking Responsible Agent Auditing

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:01:24Z
- authors: Zipeng Ye, Shenyu Dai, Yong Xie, Kun Wang, Jiyang Guan, Zhikai Chen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16493

As agents perform tasks autonomously in local and cloud environments, auditing their execution trajectories can help service providers identify risks and improve agent harnesses and foundation models. Our analysis of production and red-team trajectories shows that some risks are repeatedly missed across auditing configurations. Overall recall obscures which risks are missed and whether higher recall comes with more false positives on legitimate behavior. Existing benchmarks offer limited insight into these failures. Generated trajectories can contain inconsistent tool calls and responses, conspicuous risky actions, or repetitive attack patterns. Grouping results by risk type also makes it difficult to tell whether auditors overlooked evidence, accepted an unsupported conclusion, or lacked information needed to make a judgment. We introduce ReA-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for auditing responsible agent behavior. It organizes auditing challenges into six tiers: internal safety knowledge, contradictions within trajectories, evidence retrieval in long contexts, verification of sources and reasoning, indirect steering, and missing information. It includes challenging benign cases in which actions resemble risky ones but are consistent with the task and its authorization. It also provides paired trajectory versions to test whether auditors detect the same risks more reliably when the trajectory is shortened or missing information is supplied. Most samples are drawn from real production trajectories, with scenarios manually selected and adapted to preserve realistic tasks and risks that could plausibly arise during execution.


### arXiv 2611.16492 - Time and Space: A Dual-Axis, Evidence-Grounded Context Substrate for Personal Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T09:01:06Z
- authors: Haitao Cheng, Jerry Hu, Longdong Mao, Dong Huang, Dongming Li, Lijuan Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16492

We argue that a binding constraint for personal agents is not model capacity but the form in which context is represented: the agent does not know which entity a phrase denotes, when a fact held, or which source record licenses a claim. We call the missing representation spatio-temporal context (STC) and define it on two axes. The temporal axis carries event time, validity intervals and ingestion time. The spatial axis is a relational space in which entities are points, typed relations are edges, and distance is semantic rather than geographic; physical places are one entity type among many. Every relation is anchored to immutable source records, and every answer must be closed over those records. We formalise the substrate and six invariants, a context operator that composes temporal intersection with relational expansion, an ambiguity-preserving resolution state machine, a conflict detector that converts mutually exclusive facts into user adjudication rather than silent overwrite, and a four-regime consumption taxonomy spanning query, insight, triage and proactive reminder. We describe a local-first prototype of the retrieval layer built on SQLite with on-device embeddings and hybrid lexical-dense recall, report measurements from a deployed store of 341 documents, 340,079 chunks and 190,271 embedded vectors, and specify, but do not yet implement, the analytic layer on which the framework depends. We close with an evaluation protocol with per-invariant metrics for evidence coverage, temporal correctness, conflict precision and interruption cost. The proposal targets the representation of context rather than the volume of it.


### arXiv 2611.16487 - HeMeR: Heterogeneous Memory Reconciliation for Embodied Agents via Structured KV Reuse

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:58:20Z
- authors: Saehun Chun, Wonje Choi, Jinwoo Jang, Taeyoon Kwack, Honguk Woo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16487
