Embodied agents make decisions by combining information from heterogeneous memory modules, including declarative memory for explicit knowledge, procedural memory for learned routines, and working memory for current observations. During interaction, the memory contents required for decision making change with the agent's state, goal, and task progress, while many contents remain shared across consecutive decisions. However, existing memory modules are typically accessed through separate interfaces, so each updated memory context is often processed as a new input to the foundation model. This prevents selective key-value (KV) cache reuse for unchanged memory contents and forces full KV cache recomputation even when much of the memory context remains valid. We introduce HeMeR, an inference framework that performs KV reconciliation between dynamically updated memory contexts and reusable KV states. HeMeR tracks declarative, procedural, and working memory contents across action decisions, preserves KV states for unchanged contents, and reconciles only the KV states affected by memory-content updates. This enables efficient and stable inference as memory contents evolve during interaction. We evaluate HeMeR on ALFWorld, Habitat, and real-world robotic deployments requiring coordinated use of heterogeneous memory contents. In the real-world setting, HeMeR achieves 83.20% task success and reduces per-step inference time from 2.94 to 1.87 seconds compared with MemRL, while preserving compatible KV states as task progress updates the memory context.


### arXiv 2611.16485 - Contain, Conform, Compare (Extended Version): A Red-Teamed Evaluation Kit for Small Coding Agents and the Full Record of Its Experiments

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:54:03Z
- authors: Nguyen Minh Thang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16485

A hosted score in the Gemma 4 Developer Agent competition is one noisy number, so development needs a local evaluator that is fast, safe for agent-written code, and matching the hosted scorer's prompts. drydock-eval evaluates a small coding agent (Gemma 4 E4B) at about 250 runs per hour on one RTX 5090. Contain runs each job in a fresh container without network and passed 200 scripted escape tests; conform provides eight checks that compare a local stack's requests with the scorer's configuration, down to rendered tokens; compare provides paired statistics that respect repository clusters. AI agents built and attacked the kit; a human owner approved machine use and release. This extended version repeats the short paper and adds the full record of the experiments made with and around the kit: 9,660 rows of agent runs, 2,494 grading controls and 72 serving measurements, all released. On four L4 GPUs the 31B model decodes 38.1 tokens per second in one stream and E4B 105.8. Six configurations of a 31B agent solved between 37 and 44 of 70 development tasks, and no pairwise difference was detected. A pre-registered five-arm experiment (231 tasks, 4,620 jobs) measured four prompt divergences: no E4B solve-rate change was detected (largest estimate -2.1 points, 95% interval -4.4 to +0.4). Three changes of the E4B agent led their screens by +1.0 to +6.2 points; each was then tested once on new tasks under a frozen pass rule, and none was confirmed (gains -0.35, -0.39 and +4.10 points). The unchanged baseline, run again on the same 128 tasks, moved by 1.17 points, and 65% of these tasks were solved in none of eight runs. Prompts and agent bundles are withheld; every run-level result is public.


### arXiv 2611.16478 - One Model, Two Precisions: Does Disagreement Between Quantized Copies Tell You When a Small Model Is Wrong?

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:47:29Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Allan Kevin T
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16478

Local language models often exist at several precisions, and quantization is known to change individual answers. This study asks whether the disagreement between two precisions of the same weights, an 8-bit copy that answers and a lower-precision copy that reads the same prompt, works as a per-question error score for abstention, escalation and re-asking. The cost is one extra forward pass over fewer bytes, with no sampling and no training. We simulate round-to-nearest quantization with per-group scales (3 to 6 bits, groups of 32 to 256) on Qwen3-0.6B and Qwen3-1.7B and score 8,398 multiple-choice questions from ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge, MMLU and HellaSwag subsets. Error detection is measured by AUROC, risk-coverage curves and ECE on a held-out half, with paired bootstrap intervals over questions. For Qwen3-0.6B, the entropy of the 8-bit answer reaches AUROC 0.682; the best disagreement score, selected on a development half, reaches 0.684 (+0.001 [-0.009, +0.011]), and combining it with the confidence features adds +0.009 [+0.002, +0.015], mostly on ARC. On Qwen3-1.7B the selected score is -0.001 [-0.009, +0.006] from entropy and the combination +0.003 [-0.001, +0.006] on the test half. Gaussian weight noise at matched error energy is about as informative as rounding (0.6B only), so we find no evidence that the signal is specific to quantization, and shuffling the features within task and entropy cells removes the gain. One extra 8-bit pass that shifts the option order does better (+0.049 [+0.031, +0.067] over entropy) at about a third more weight traffic; routing to a larger model on free entropy beats both extra-pass scores at equal total traffic. After logistic recalibration the 0.6B confidences are calibrated within a few points of ECE. A deployment recipe and the cases where the method does not help are given.


### arXiv 2611.16477 - Where in the Input Does a One-Pass Decision Encoder Look?

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:42:11Z
- authors: Mohammed Nayeem Khan, Allan Kevin T
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16477

Laya is a small model that answers a multiple-choice question about a text in one pass: the question, the options (each with a name and a description) and the text form one sequence of at most 512 tokens, the window, in the English release. A longer text is silently cut at the end, and we measure what that costs. Test documents place a held-out row's text, the evidence, at a chosen token offset among distractor text; 177 items, five tasks, three checkpoints (released, short-row fine-tune, long-document fine-tune), four window sizes and 93,275 scored requests. When the evidence lies beyond the window, accuracy falls to 26.4% for the released checkpoint (27.2% for both fine-tunes), near the 27.0% of guessing and below the 35.6% of always giving the commonest label. Confidence gives no warning (AUROC 0.50 to 0.55 for telling cut from read requests; 0.5 = chance), and the runtime's truncation flag also fires on requests whose evidence was visible. The released checkpoint reads visible evidence badly once text precedes it: 38.5% pooled against 60% when the evidence comes first, so a larger window alone gains +6.2 points. Fine-tuning on long documents adds +27.6 points on visible evidence over the short-row fine-tune (95% interval +22.3 to +33.1) and nothing on cut evidence. That is an in-family result: training rows and test documents share generator and question types, and the main filler is of the training kind. With encyclopedic filler never seen in training the gain is +13.2 (+9.9 to +16.6), with rows of other tasks as filler -2.0. Among training-free rearrangements a 312-token window scan helps most (+11.9 points over the default for the released checkpoint) at six to eleven forward passes. Documents are synthetic, labels are LLM-made and one backbone is studied, so these diagnose this model, not long documents in general.


### arXiv 2611.16473 - Robots That Talk Too Much and Don't Listen Enough

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.HC
- published: 2026-11-12T08:32:18Z
- authors: Graham Wilcock, Kristiina Jokinen
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16473

Until recently, the biggest obstacles to natural spoken interaction with social robots were speech recognition errors and turn-taking failures. Advances in speech recognition have enabled much better spoken interaction, but turn-taking failures and other misunderstandings continue to be far too prevalent. This short paper looks back at earlier methods for improving speech recognition and turn-taking in interaction with social robots, and then looks at what can be done today, arguing that multimodal bidirectional streaming is needed to reduce the frequency of turn-taking failures.


### arXiv 2611.16466 - How Should Planning Agents ReSHAPE Their Search Space?

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-12T08:22:18Z
- authors: Yilin Zhang, Xinran Zhao, Tong Chen, Ziming Yuan, Tongshuang Wu
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16466

Planning agents and benchmarks treat a user's initial specification as fixed. Yet the requests users write exclude plans they would gladly take, since they rarely discover the possible trade-offs they would accept until seeing the options. Agents already revise requests through interaction, but whether this helps planning is unknown. We formalize this step as the ReSHAPE task, which judges a revision by whether its search space matches the acceptable plans and a planner finds one there. Using ReSHAPE Planter, which builds instances from planning benchmarks with a human-validated simulated user, we analyze 5,800 agent-revised specifications in travel and shopping. We find that reshaping matters: one correct trade-off makes the same planner reach an acceptable plan up to 4.6x as often, and search space quality predicts what the planner finds. Current agents, however, admit at most 32% of the acceptable plans, where that trade-off alone admits over 52%, because they ask about preferences instead of showing plans; in travel, showing plans raises F1 by 9.7 points over asking, with two model families. Planning agents should thus treat a request as a hypothesis to test against the user's reactions to concrete plans to recognize user trade-offs.


### arXiv 2611.16460 - Fine-Tuned Small Language Model for Synthetic Clinical Data Generation and Predictive Healthcare Analytics

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:12:17Z
- authors: Mihnea Sebastian Burlacu, Ionut Anghel, Tudor Cioara
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16460

The reduced availability of medical datasets represents an important challenge in the development of predictive models for remote healthcare solutions, especially when the available data are imbalanced or difficult to extend through traditional methods. Small Language Models (SLMs) have become a promising alternative to Large Language Models (LLMs) for addressing these limitations due to their lower computational requirements and adaptability to specialized tasks. However, their performance remains highly dependent on the quality and representativeness of the available training data. In this paper, we propose a method for clinical data augmentation based on a fine-tuned Microsoft Phi-2 model that is used to generate synthetic instances, with the purpose of improving deep learning-based model for predicting remotely monitored vital signs. The Phi-2 model was adapted to the medical domain through supervised fine-tuning on the MedQuad dataset, through a Quantized Low-Rank Adaptation (QLoRA)-based method and by applying few-shot prompting techniques. The resulting model was used afterwards in the augmentation of a small-sized dataset, composed of vital sign values and labels associated with pathological and physiological states. The experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuned SLMs improved the performance on medical domain, achieving higher scores for both lexical (ROUGE) and semantic (BERTScore) evaluation metrics compared to the pretrained model. Moreover, the fine-tuned SLM is effective for generating structured synthetic clinical data. When used for data augmentation, the generated dataset improved the performance of a LSTM predictive model, increasing the classification accuracy from 89.0% to 93.7% and showing consistent improvements in precision, recall, and F1-score.


### arXiv 2611.16455 - Rational Alignments of Superintelligences via Debates

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T08:07:48Z
- authors: Thanawat Sornwanee
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16455

Artificial superintelligences (ASI) is artificial intelligences with greater capability. However, as human principal has to use it, we have to ensure that the agent will perform in the interest of the human. We investigate the framework of ASI-assisted question answering where ASI agents know the correct answer but the human does not. We provide some example of mechanism for rational alignment, where the agent utility is unchanged, but the interaction between the agent and the human principal is controlled to be in a certain fashion. We show specifically that a debate framework where the agents can further reason and behave in a subgame perfect equilibrium manner will lead to a more predictable result, and in some case will be favorable.


### arXiv 2611.16445 - FPSRG: Heterogeneous User Simulation First-Person Post-Training and Multidimensional Semantic Response Grids

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T07:58:50Z
- authors: Ruimin Pei, Haibo Liu, Fang Zhang, Xunhua Guo
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16445

Simulating heterogeneous individuals with large language models is an active research direction, and heterogeneous user simulation requires models with different characteristics. We study profile construction: given dimensions, items, and scoring rules, build model configurations that can be retrieved by multidimensional target scores. We introduce First-Person Semantic Response Grids (FPSRG). Shared post-training first teaches the model to judge and answer as the participant; self-report and behavioral targets then refine dimension-specific control vectors. With the model frozen, we measure joint scores for vector-coefficient combinations, filter inadmissible configurations, and store the results in a searchable catalog. Per-token configuration routing, continuous batching, and isolated caches support parallel measurement of different profiles. Non-thinking five- and ten-dimensional Qwen instances and a thinking-mode six-dimensional instance following full-parameter post-training of Huihui demonstrate the complete workflow. With matched data, layers, and norms, refinement increases mean five-axis composite control span from 30.75 to 40.45. Within the five-dimensional 25-75 target region, the qualified five-level vector catalog covers 92.2% of fixed targets under per-axis error at most 20 and total error at most 60; combining it with the prompt catalog increases coverage to 98.7%. At a fixed coefficient range, three-, five-, and nine-level catalogs achieve 71.2%, 92.2%, and 94.6% coverage, demonstrating the gains from denser measurement. These results describe measured reachable regions under a fixed assessment protocol. Through shared adaptation, a small set of learned directions, and reusable joint measurements, FPSRG constructs heterogeneous individual configurations without retraining a model for every profile.


### arXiv 2611.16442 - DesignTaste: Benchmarking AI-Generator Visual Design Against Award-Wining Works

- categories: cs.CV, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T07:52:27Z
- authors: Zhou Chen, JiaXiang Li, DanYing Pan, Ye Li, TianRui Yang, EnBao Zhang
- query-set: cs.AI
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16442

Text-to-image benchmarks commonly assess text-image alignment or overall preference. Whether models can produce good designs when a brief leaves the appearance open remains less well understood. We introduce DesignTaste, a visual design benchmark that compares generated images with award-winning works. From these works and their publicly available context, we derive 403 design briefs: 100 poster tasks from 100 Beste Plakate 2025 and 303 tasks spanning 20 categories from ADC 2026. Six leading commercial text-to-image models each generate an image from the same brief. A single model judge scores each output and the corresponding award-winning work independently on five evaluation dimensions. On each dataset, only ArtArk achieves a higher mean overall score than the award-winning works. All six models score lower in Creative Originality on both datasets. These results show that producing finished, visually appealing images does not imply that a model can develop good design solutions.


### arXiv 2611.16378 - Evidence-Governed Adoption of Self-Generated Updates in Autonomous AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-12T06:45:02Z
- authors: Subodh Lonkar
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16378

Autonomous agents modify prompts, memories, tools, workflows, and policies using interaction histories. Yet a plausible modification is not necessarily suitable for persistent adoption: candidate selection is adaptive, evaluators may be imperfect, and local gains can hide degradation against an earlier baseline. We introduce Evidence-Governed Evolution (EGE), a framework for governing adoption of self-generated agent modifications. EGE separates candidate generation from confirmatory adoption, evaluates frozen candidates on fresh paired audits, requires meaningful improvement on a prespecified primary objective, protects declared capabilities through reference-relative non-inferiority tests, and applies governance constraints before bounded canary deployment. We establish an evaluator-calibrated simultaneous lower-bound construction for a countable sequence of adaptively generated candidates. It combines candidate-wise Hoeffding bounds with independent calibration samples comparing the operational evaluator with a prespecified reference procedure. We also formalise reference replacement through bridge tests, derive a cumulative degradation bound along a versioned reference lineage, and provide an audit-planning condition relating effect-size margin, sample sizes, and acceptance probability. A Monte Carlo stress test illustrates calibration under candidate-dependent evaluator optimism; it is a stylised statistical experiment, not an evaluation of a deployed agent. The guarantees are conditional on bounded paired scores, fresh sampling, a valid reference-evaluation construct, and declared evaluation distributions; they do not establish universal agent safety or robustness to arbitrary distribution shift.


### arXiv 2611.16315 - Shared Item-Level Error Limits the Aggregation of Small Language Models in Fermi Estimation

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T05:23:22Z
- authors: Carolina Fortuna, Blaz Bertalanic
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16315

Multi-agent systems of language models are often motivated by an averaging argument: if the agents' errors are unbiased and independent, the error of an aggregate of N answers falls as 1/sqrt(N). Fermi estimation, whose continuous answers are aggregated on the logarithmic scale, permits a direct test. We decompose the log10 error of each answer into the mean error of its model on the item, common to all its draws on the item, and a residual, and estimate both from 2.51M answers given before any communication by 15 open-weight models (3B-72B) on realFP, with replication under a single prompt pair on realFP, Science Olympiad Fermi questions and a guesstimation set. Given the item, teammates' errors are uncorrelated and teams behave as independent draws, but the item-level component accounts for a median 0.906 of the error variance over items. Teams of thirty therefore reduce a single agent's error by a median 1.7% (at most 5.4%), whereas draws that were also unbiased on each item would reduce it by 74-79%. All models compress their estimates toward about 10^6; this trend explains a median 17% of the item-level component, and a cross-validated linear recalibration on labelled items removes the overall bias but not that component. Three rounds of debate reduce within-team dispersion by 93% and leave the item-level component unchanged; revision without peers attains the same small gain. Mixing five models of similar accuracy reduces error by 6.6% relative to their own homogeneous teams, but no mixture improves on the most accurate model. Higher sampling temperature changes team error by about 1% at most, and contrastive activation steering not detectably; per-agent scaffold prompts (not significant after multiplicity correction) and skipping one layer in a single-model screen improve it. The results are conditional on the models, benchmarks, prompts and settings tested.


### arXiv 2611.16123 - SwarmSafe-Delta: Isolating Message Effects on LLM Agent Safety

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T00:17:32Z
- authors: Junru Zhu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16123

When a tool-using LLM agent team violates policy, aggregate outcomes do not reveal whether an upstream message changed the executor's action. We introduce SwarmSafe-Delta, a paired executable protocol that freezes upstream computation, varies real, hidden, and format-matched placebo delivery, and scores a single committed action against deterministic benign and risky policy states under preassigned budgets. Across four models and constructed optional tasks, real delivery at $N=4$ produces 21.4% risky-world violations, versus 5.0% with placebo and 1.0% with hidden delivery (paired differences +16.4 and +20.3 percentage points). Real delivery also improves benign task success, illustrating that suppressing communication sacrifices utility. In a second regime constructed to require an upstream lookup route, real delivery improves safe completion by 74.2 points relative to hidden delivery. These effects quantify message-delivery contrasts under specified information dependencies, not naturally occurring failure rates. Exploratory action-level analyses show that the effect depends strongly on whether the upstream message recommends a prerequisite check or direct execution; model differences also largely track these message compositions. The contribution is a controlled, execution-grounded measurement protocol rather than a general claim that agent communication is harmful or protective.


### arXiv 2611.16082 - Gradient-Aware AI Agents for Autonomous Turbulence Model Discovery via Differentiable CFD

- categories: physics.flu-dyn, cs.LG, cs.MA, physics.comp-ph
- published: 2026-11-11T22:50:33Z
- authors: Yi Liu, Xiantao Fan, Mingqi Xu, Jian-Xun Wang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16082

Turbulence modeling relies on human choices of closure form, physical inputs, and tunable parameters. Training closures a posteriori is efficient only with gradients through long chaotic rollouts, which few solvers provide. Most a posteriori approaches therefore resort to gradient-free optimization, and nearly all fix the closure form in advance, learning only its parameters. In this work, we introduce a gradient-aware agentic framework that poses closure discovery as a bilevel problem: a multi-agent large language model (LLM) system searches over closure forms, while a GPU-native differentiable computational fluid dynamics (CFD) solver supplies gradients, making differentiable a posteriori learning an action of the autonomous AI agent. For each proposal, the agents construct a differentiable training program by embedding the analytical, neural, or hybrid closure in the solver, implementing a differentiable objective, and validating the gradient path. They then decide whether to evaluate the closure, compute its parameter sensitivities, or train it a posteriori, select the optimization settings, and revise subsequent proposals using the simulation outcomes and returned gradients. Truncated-horizon gradients make this training usable under chaotic dynamics for closures with tens of thousands of parameters. Demonstrations include dissipation-law and subgrid-scale closure discovery in two-dimensional turbulence and wall-model discovery for large-eddy simulation of three-dimensional channel flow. With gradient-based training, the agent recovers reference coefficients that forward evaluation alone does not reach within the same budget, and autonomously discovers and trains analytical and neural closures that improve agreement with the targeted reference statistics. These results demonstrate the feasibility of end-to-end autonomous turbulence model development in time-resolved flows.


### arXiv 2611.15843 - Food Critic: An Agentic Visualization System for Auditable Restaurant-Safety Reasoning

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T18:57:07Z
- authors: Arleth Salinas, Ishrat Jahan Eliza, Valerio Pascucci
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15843

Generative AI agents are increasingly being embedded into visualization systems that can search data, invoke tools, summarize evidence, and produce recommendations. However, when agents rely solely on their own knowledge, the visualization problem is not only whether the agent provides a useful answer, but also whether users can inspect how that answer is grounded. We present Food Critic, an agentic visualization system for diners' restaurant decision support that integrates public health inspection records with crowd review data. The system grounds agents under three conditions: a database-only ReAct agent, a retrieval- augmented agent with access to food-safety regulations, and an ontology-guided agent that produces structured hypotheses and typed evidence. Food Critic externalizes the agent's reasoning within these groundings as inspectable visual evidence. We evaluate LLM models with several use cases under each grounding of our agentic system. Our work positions restaurant choice as a concrete case for studying human-agent collaboration, visual provenance of agent reasoning, and the design of visual interfaces for grounded GenAI decision support.


### arXiv 2611.15838 - Before AI Agents Negotiate for Us: A Competitive Tournament of Rule-Based, Search, Optimization, Learned, and Language-Model Negotiators Under Scarcity

- categories: cs.AI, cs.GT, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T18:48:20Z
- authors: Eduardo de Avila-Armenta, Raúl Alejandro Aquino-Ibarra, Jorge Octavio Nicolás-Díaz, Umberto Leon-Dominguez
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15838

Automated bilateral negotiation underlies two-sided matching markets such as freight brokering, where many shippers and carriers negotiate price before a matching mechanism decides who is paired with whom. Prior strategy comparisons typically use isolated one-on-one duels or a single fixed market, so they cannot show how a strategy behaves against several live rivals under a changing balance of supply and demand. We built nine negotiation architectures spanning rule-based, search-based, optimization-based, imitation-learning, and reinforcement-learning families, and ran all nine simultaneously on both sides of a Gale-Shapley matching market under a balanced condition and two scarcity conditions rotated across all 36 exclusion pairs. In the balanced carrier market, an imitation-learned engine (ML2), a reinforcement-learning policy trained only on isolated negotiations (RL), and an expected-utility optimization engine (OPT) formed a statistically tied top group, and no architecture led in every condition and role. RL beat all eight carrier rivals when carriers were scarce, but its match rate collapsed from 62% to 16% when shippers were scarce and it had to compete with the other carrier agents for demand. A second reinforcement-learning policy trained against rival agents (RL2) kept its match rate above 91% in every condition, yet lost on realized payoff to all eight shipper-side rivals in two of the three conditions, traced to a confirmed train/deployment feature mismatch. We also tested nine large language models (LLMs), from 1 to 72 billion parameters, under the same protocol: they lost significantly to the two strongest engines (OPT2 and ML2) in 32 of 36 comparisons and won none. These results show that isolated or single-condition evaluation can hide failure modes that a many-sided, scarcity-varying, statistically corrected tournament exposes directly.


### arXiv 2611.15754 - MULTILOOP: Evolving Multi-Agent Harnesses for Complex Information-Seeking Tasks

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T17:34:12Z
- authors: Daniela Gottesman, Gal Yona, Ori Ram, Zorik Gekhman, Jonathan Herzig, Mor Geva
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15754

The performance of LLM-based agents depends not only on the underlying model but also on the harness: the prompts, tools, and orchestration logic around it. Recent works automate harness optimization with coding agents that analyze execution traces, diagnose failures, and edit the harness code; however, those focused on single-agent systems. Multi-agent harnesses are harder to optimize as fixing them requires changing how agents interact in addition to how each behaves. We study whether automated harness optimization can diagnose and repair failures in multi-agent systems through a case study on complex information-seeking tasks from DeepSearchQA [Gupta et al., 2026]. We apply MULTILOOP, a simple optimization loop in which a coding agent iteratively analyzes, evaluates and edits the harness. With only 10 training samples, the evolved harness improves accuracy by 4.6% over the base harness and by 3.2% over the best single-agent system. However, a taxonomy of failure modes before and after optimization shows that the orchestration failures that characterize multi-agent systems largely persist. Our findings motivate harness optimization methods that encourage structural changes to how agents delegate tasks and share information.


### arXiv 2611.15521 - DecArena: Evaluating Large-Scale Decentralized Multi-Agent Collaboration

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-11T14:13:26Z
- authors: Jiali Cheng, Oleg Rokhlenko, Joo-Kyung Kim, Hadi Amiri, Xingzhi Guo
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15521

Emerging personal AI agents raise fundamental scalability and privacy concerns for centralized multi-agent systems (MASs), which rely on a global orchestrator with broad access to agents, resources, and communication. In decentralized environments, global visibility is unavailable. Agents need to proactively discover non-local resources (e.g. tools, skills), and collaborate with other agents to solve tasks. We study decentralized multi-agent collaboration under graph-constrained communication, a natural reflection of human structures. Each agent observes only its local state and can interact only with neighboring agents. We introduce a benchmark with synthetic scalable distributed tool calling with 100K+ tools. Our experiments reveal that recent LLM agents struggle to discover non-local relevant resources and collaborate in decentralized setting. Moreover, standard reinforcement learning fails to reliably overcome this limitation, suggesting that decentralized MAS needs more explicit harness mechanisms for effective collaboration, even using SOTA LLMs. We then present a strong baseline \textbf{GraphHarness}, combining topology-aware warmup with self-improving LLM-based routing. Results show that GraphHarness substantially improves task success (+48.5) and produces higher quality collaboration (+30.6), while using fewer costs (+34.0) than baselines.


### arXiv 2611.15515 - Incentive-Responsive Agents: A Principal-Agent Framework, Metric Suite, and Runtime Architecture for Long-Lived Personal AI Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T14:09:13Z
- authors: Jipu Su
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15515

Personal AI agents now persist: they hold accounts, mailboxes, long-term memory, and open-ended task streams, yet the dominant way to improve them remains capability-side: prompt engineering, larger models, longer thinking, and retrieval. We argue that long-lived agents are missing an incentive layer. We formalize an incentive not as text in a prompt but as a runtime contract over the agent's operating point (model tier, test-time compute, memory budget, tool authority, task priority, verification, and compensation) that the agent's policy must causally consume. We develop a principal-agent model with hidden effort, a contractible operating point, and costly verification. We derive (i) a non-consumption invariance principle separating genuine incentives from prompt-level persuasion, (ii) a persistence-as-discount theorem making identity continuity a precondition for sustained effort, (iii) an incentive-capability exchange-rate frontier yielding a per-task routing rule, and (iv) a Goodhart bound for multi-channel verification, plus a three-channel decomposition of mechanism gains (allocation, verification, continuation). A 240-run real-LLM experiment with two models and 60 verifiable tasks shows that prompt-level incentive framing has no detectable effect (p = 0.62), whereas runtime allocation raises success on the hardest task family from 0.60 to 1.00 (p = 0.008) at 4.8x price-weighted cost; the exchange-rate theorem predicts the break-even baseline at 0.21. A 20-seed, 3,000-task simulation shows resource ROI rising from 7.09 to 16.67 and gaming falling from 0.333 to 0.115. Code, simulator, and all results are released; live deployment remains future work.


### arXiv 2611.15460 - Read and Ignore: What Self-Directed LLM Agents Do in Public Service Scenarios

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T13:31:32Z
- authors: Diana Kozachek, Kinjalk Srivastava
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15460

Underspecified instructions leave LLM agents to decide what to do. In public services, staff write these instructions, but the agent's decisions affect residents, patients, and students. We build four expert-validated public-service scenarios with records and decision levers that can be checked in the files, collect $2{,}400$ trajectories from ten LLMs under four prompt conditions, and compare them with $1{,}000$ runs in a context-free sandbox. Agents read the records in $96.9\%$ of open-ended runs and produce an artifact or a self-chosen task in $18.7\%$; with an explicit task, $86.0\%$ produce. Domain context does not change how many runs produce ($18.7\%$ vs.\ $16.0\%$), and the production ranking of the models largely carries over (Spearman $0.79$), while the ranking by whether a model reads the records does not ($0.19$). Context cuts boundary-probing turns per run by $60\%$, and some artifacts contradict the records. We contribute a test that a public-service institution can run on its own records before procurement; its rule-based taxonomy needs no labeled data, and scenarios, code, and trajectories are released.


### arXiv 2611.15309 - Where Autonomy Belongs: A Separation of Powers for Agentic Program Evolution

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T10:44:21Z
- authors: Zhengyang Ai, Tong Liu, Jingxian Tang, Xiaobing Du, Tao Xiao, Fengshi Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15309

LLM-driven program evolution rests on three decisions: govern directs the search from accumulated evidence, propose constructs the next candidate, and judge settles validity and fitness. All three appear in every system, and existing work splits into two lines over who makes each. Controller-centric systems strengthen govern and neglect the autonomy of propose, which caps what evolution can reach. Agent-centric systems let a coding agent take over both govern and propose, at a cognitive and computational cost. Neither line protects the independent authority of judge, so benchmark reliability suffers. These failures share one root: autonomy is misallocated across the three decisions. We address this problem with EvoTrias, a separation-of-powers architecture in which each decision has one owner: Structured Governance for what spans evolution rounds, Open Proposal for construction inside a task, and Protected Judgment behind a private evaluator. These owners exchange typed contracts only, and runtime hooks reinforce the boundaries. This separation also lets EvoTrias run as a plugin that turns an existing coding agent into an evolution system. EvoTrias sets a new state of the art on mathematical, systems, and algorithm-design benchmarks. Three further results show what each owner buys: separating Governance from Proposal cuts token cost by 64%, Open Proposal extends evolution to a 680K-line industrial repository, and Protected Judgment keeps benchmark scores faithful to transfer.


### arXiv 2611.15097 - GuardianAgentFlow: Multi-Agent Coordination for Web Privacy Protection

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T06:19:31Z
- authors: Ruiyi Yang, Gayathri Lihinikaduarachchi, Rahat Masood, Flora D. Salim, Salil S. Kanhere
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15097

Web agents need to use private context without unnecessarily disclosing it. Effective protection must distinguish between requests that may be sent after sanitization and those that must be withheld, while preserving the information needed for authorized tasks. We present GuardianAgentFlow, a multi-agent framework that mediates outgoing Web requests through an explicit release contract. A dedicated restriction check grounds permission in the user's instruction; a shared protection scope aligns rewriting with disclosure checking; and candidate-grounded feedback guides revision before transmission. Experiments on Web requests with human-reviewed intent labels show improved request-contract success, while controlled comparisons and role ablations identify the contributions of restriction handling, policy evidence, and candidate checking. Evaluations of action proposals from PrivacyLens and AgentDAM demonstrate complementary protection when mediation is composed with privacy-aware agents. Experiments with smaller mediation models show that the coordination benefit persists with reduced model capacity. Dedicated-server measurements show lower mediation latency than full-policy reasoning, while real-site browser tests quantify integration overhead.


### arXiv 2611.15017 - Beyond Named Mechanisms: Economic Institutions as a Design Space for Artificial Agents

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.GT, econ.GN
- published: 2026-11-11T04:18:25Z
- authors: Nikolos Gurney, Fred Morstatter
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15017

Multiagent research inherited its economic vocabulary of auctions, markets, prices, bargaining, and matching from a single observed case: human societies. Those institutions bundle identity, competence, execution, ownership, commitment, and settlement in ways that suit agents whose minds are private, who learn rather than copy skills, and who each persist as one person. Engineered agents need not share any of these constraints, making the list of familiar institutions look less like the entirety of economic organization and more like a sample from a larger, unmapped space. We sketch one way to make that space concrete: a provisional chart that records each institution, separately from the agents that run it, as a typed structure over positions, rights and duties, allocation, information, time, state, process, value, and external recognition. In this chart, named institutions become overlapping regions rather than fixed points, neighboring families separate along a few coordinates, and the gaps between them become places to search. We show what the shift in perspective buys with a simulation in which one technical act, copying an agent's competence, is institutionalized as a new independent supplier, as licensed capacity that creates no new participant, and as the splitting of one economic position into successors that inherit some obligations and not others. In a paired task, all three produce an identical technical trace despite differing in who exists, who owns the copy, who is obligated, and who gets paid. We close with a research program: building a principled chart of institution space, mapping how its implementable region shifts with agent capacities, and revising the chart wherever engineered agents expose distinctions it cannot express.


### arXiv 2611.14963 - GHA-SmellFix: Taking Action with Agentic LLMs for Detecting and Refactoring GitHub Actions Workflow Smells

- categories: cs.SE, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-11T03:16:33Z
- authors: Anderson Uchôa, Jhefferson Souza, Emanuel Ávila, Gabriel Silva, Kevin Lira, Hui Sun
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14963

Continuous integration and continuous delivery workflows are key artifacts in software projects. In GitHub Actions, they encode build, test, release, deployment, and security automation in YAML. These workflows contain configuration smells affecting security, reliability, maintainability, or operational stability. Existing approaches are mostly rule-based, with rules hard to create and evolve, and typically report findings without fixes. Agentic LLM pipelines can combine contextual detection, transformation selection, and patch generation. Specialized agents can gate refactoring on positive detection verdicts, potentially avoiding unnecessary changes, but it is unclear whether decomposition improves effectiveness or mainly changes overhead. We present GHA-SmellFix, an agentic LLM pipeline for detecting and refactoring GitHub Actions workflow smells. We compare two designs: Joint, where one agent detects and refactors, and Decomposed, where separate agents detect and then refactor. We operationalize 19 smells from an existing catalog with explicit detection criteria, refactoring expectations, exclusion rules, and safety checks, and curate a ground truth of 1,710 (workflow, smell) pairs. We evaluate the designs with four LLMs (GPT-5.4-nano, Gemini 3.5 Flash, Claude Haiku 4.5, and DeepSeek V4 Flash) on 570 sampled pairs. Results show that decomposition does not universally improve patch quality; its main benefit is controlling when patch generation is attempted. It helps models prone to missing smells and can reduce overhead by gating patches. Once a smell is correctly detected, clean refactoring is generally effective, making detection the main end-to-end bottleneck. However, false positives can trigger unnecessary edits to execution-relevant constructs. Practical CI/CD agents thus need reliable abstention and construct-aware validation alongside patch generation.


### arXiv 2611.14846 - Open Challenges and Opportunities in Human-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T23:55:44Z
- authors: Reuth Mirsky, Sarath Sreedharan, Nitay Alon, Stefan Sarkadi, Munindar P. Singh, Liz Sonenberg
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14846

Agentic AI systems increasingly undertake extended tasks and act on people's behalf, making human-agent interaction central to their effectiveness. Successful interaction requires understanding human perspectives, coordinating complementary capabilities, and maintaining productive relationships within socio-technical environments. This paper examines how foundations developed by the Autonomous Agents and Multi-Agent Systems (AAMAS) community inform these demands. We consider interactions ranging from individual human-agent partnerships to teams and larger groups, where participants differ in their roles, decision authority, capabilities, and objectives. We organize computational approaches around the connected challenges of understanding others and deciding how to interact with them. Theory of Mind, belief-desire-intention architectures, goal and plan recognition, inverse planning, and recursive reasoning provide mechanisms for representing and inferring partially observable mental states. Observer-aware planning, model reconciliation, and decision-theoretic communication connect these inferences to action, explanation, and information exchange. Together with shared autonomy, argumentation, and cooperative inverse reinforcement learning, these approaches offer foundations for agentic systems that account for evolving human knowledge, intentions, and expectations. We argue that progress requires treating human interaction as an ongoing decision problem shaped by uncertainty, interdependence, and potentially conflicting objectives.


### arXiv 2611.14780 - RE-PRESENT: Evidence Triangulation across Alternative Candidate-Space Views for Efficient LLM Ensemble Inference

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T22:12:06Z
- authors: Juming Xiong, Weixin Liu, Kevin Guo, Congning Ni, Chongyu Qu, Junchao Zhu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14780

Heterogeneous large language models (LLMs) exhibit complementary strengths, motivating routing and multi-model inference methods that seek to identify reliable predictions for each input. However, exploiting this complementarity remains challenging even when a correct prediction is available within the ensemble. We investigate this challenge across heterogeneous LLM ensembles and find that a simple single-round aggregation rule is highly competitive with learned routing methods, yet substantial oracle headroom remains. Further analysis shows that the evaluated single-round response signals provide limited discriminative power for identifying reliable predictions. In contrast, controlled re-presentations of the same question elicit complementary evidence about prediction reliability. Motivated by these findings, we introduce RE-PRESENT, a training-free framework that selectively re-presents disputed questions and directly aggregates candidate-level evidence across alternative views. Across seven multiple-choice benchmarks and two heterogeneous model ensembles, RE-PRESENT improves over strong single-round aggregation while offering a favorable accuracy--cost trade-off compared with self-consistency and multi-model debate.


### arXiv 2611.14499 - Spatial patterns of transportation choice in Niterói

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.MA, stat.OT
- published: 2026-11-10T18:51:37Z
- authors: Sandro Martinelli Reia, Taylor Anderson, Dieter Pfoser
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14499

Characterizing how people move through a city requires spatially and temporally fine-grained data that are often costly and difficult to obtain. Here, we introduce a computational framework that uses macroscopic, aggregated, and static urban data to construct a microscopic, dynamic, and spatially detailed representation of urban mobility. Building on an existing activity simulator, the framework combines a synthetic population, the built environment, and transport networks to generate daily trips and assign walking, car, or public transit. Applied to approximately 475,000 synthetic residents of Niterói, Brazil, simulated neighborhood-level transit patterns show a strong association with an empirical activity proxy (Pearson r=0.885). Walking is more prevalent in compact central areas, car use increases toward the periphery, and public-transit use remains comparatively high along bus corridors. Reducing walking times to and from bus stops by 30% increases the modeled public-transit-to-car ratio by 11.3%, with larger transit-share gains in several peripheral areas. These results reveal spatial differences in transportation choice and identify where improved transit access may have the greatest modeled effect.


### arXiv 2611.14492 - ReTrace: Distilling Tool-Using Agents into Planner-Free Workflows

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T18:49:47Z
- authors: Kaiyuan Hou, Yang Ping Kuo, Indranil Bhattacharya, Rohith Nama, Arushi Gupta, Carlos D. Castillo
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14492

Business process automation runs the same Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) again and again on new inputs. Large Language Model (LLM) agents can follow an SOP written in natural language, but they call an LLM to choose every step of every run, which adds latency and cost at scale. We present ReTrace, which turns an SOP and a few logged agent runs into a reusable workflow in which code, not an LLM, selects the next step. The agent's reasoning, the explanation it writes with each tool call, often states why it took an action; the tool calls alone do not. ReTrace extracts the condition behind each step, merges these conditions across runs into a compact rule document, and generates an executable workflow from it. Only this last stage depends on the platform, so rule documents can be compiled for different orchestration platforms. On $79$ procedures from our production platform, ReTrace workflows reach $93.8\%$ completion, against $96.8\%$ for the agent, at $24\%$ lower cost per task. On $τ^2$-Bench, they reach $75.8\%$ task success (agent: $80.6\%$) and run in $1.3$s instead of $170$s; three rounds of repair on training failures lift $k{=}2$ workflows from $66.7\%$ to $81.2\%$. On eight SOP-Bench procedures, ReTrace without repair trails few-shot generation ($80.6\%$ vs. $83.6\%$), but repair from labeled training failures lifts it to $87.8\%$ at $0.40$s per task, compared to $85.5\%$ in $46.6$s for the agent.


### arXiv 2611.14320 - Don't Just Trust Your Memory: Evidence-Linked Memory Reduces Memory-Induced Degradation in LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T16:46:59Z
- authors: Yukyeong Lee, Yumin Choi, Jinheon Baek, Sung Ju Hwang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14320

External memory enables LLM agents to improve from experience by distilling trajectories of past tasks into concise lessons for new tasks. However, memory can also lead to memory-induced degradation, where an agent fails on tasks it would solve without memory, often because it follows a lesson that is incorrect, or misapplies or misreads a valid one. This is because lessons are inferred from trajectories labeled only with task success or failure, yet are typically provided without the trajectories they came from, leaving the agent no way to check them. To address this, we introduce WikiTrace, an evidence-linked memory system designed to let agents check each lesson against its original trajectory. Specifically, motivated by the finding that a trajectory often still holds the evidence needed to catch an erroneous lesson, WikiTrace stores each lesson as a concise insight linked to a summary of why its trajectory succeeded or failed and to the raw trajectory itself. Then, on a new task, the agent starts from all insights and, whenever one lacks details needed to act, conflicts with another, or comes from a failed task, descends through its links with terminal tools to fill in missing details or correct the insight before acting. We validate WikiTrace on five agentic benchmarks, showing that it consistently outperforms existing memory approaches and a baseline given the same insights alone, while also reducing memory-induced degradation. Our code is available at https://github.com/yukyeongleee/wikitrace


### arXiv 2611.14247 - Personalized Affective Perception via Continual Learning in Human-Agent Negotiation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.LG, cs.MA, cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-10T15:57:45Z
- authors: Mehmet Onur Keskin, Berk Buzcu, Nikhil Churamani, Reyhan Aydogan, Hatice Gunes, Catholijn Jonker
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14247

Self-interested intelligent agents that negotiate with humans should interpret nonverbal expressions and adapt their behavior accordingly. However, generic facial-affect models degrade across individuals and contexts, creating a generalization gap. We introduce an affect-adaptive negotiation strategy that incorporates Continual Learning with Imagination for Facial Expression Recognition (CLIFER) to personalize valence-arousal perception in real time. Differential feedback converts changes in valence-arousal direction and intensity into adjustments of the agent's target utility. An asynchronous architecture updates CLIFER in the background during live interaction. In a counterbalanced within-subject study in which 66 participants negotiated with a humanoid robot, the CL-enabled agent achieved significantly higher individual utility and elicited higher participant concession rates than the FaceChannel baseline. The CL condition yielded significantly higher mean arousal estimates, alongside a broader output range. Participant utility and Nash distance did not change significantly between conditions. These outcomes support the practical value of coupling continual affective personalization with differential feedback for real-time negotiation.


### arXiv 2611.14242 - PARLEY: Evaluating LLM Collaboration Through Cooperative Asymmetric Puzzles

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T15:54:12Z
- authors: Javier Hernandez, Andrew D. Wilson
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14242

As large language models power autonomous agents, understanding how they collaborate and where they fail becomes critical. We introduce PARLEY, an extensible platform for studying LLM agent collaboration through four asymmetric puzzle tasks grounded in cognitive psychology (referential communication, joint coordination, perspective-taking, and spatial reasoning). GPT-4o agent pairs succeeded in 60.5% of conversations (N=400, 100 per task), with task-level variation (34-80%) reflecting distinct collaborative demands. From these conversations, we derived the data-driven PAC taxonomy: six failure types organized into Perception (visual interpretation and spatial reasoning), Adaptation (progress tracking and feedback integration), and Coordination (verification and grounding). Failure to incorporate external feedback was a critical indicator of unsuccessful collaboration, with agents repeatedly applying unsuccessful strategies. We also show that a taxonomy-guided intervention (N=200) improved average task success to 70.5%. These results demonstrate how PARLEY can be used to derive meaningful, data-driven insights that can in turn lead to improved agentic collaboration.


### arXiv 2611.13927 - Safety and Connectivity Certificates for Finite-Time Formation Transitions

- categories: eess.SY, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T11:15:47Z
- authors: Yash Purswani, Shivansh Agarwal, Anuj Tiwari
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13927

Finite-time formation transitions are useful for coordinating multi-agent systems in time-critical missions, but convergence to a prescribed formation in finite time does not guarantee collision avoidance or communication connectivity during the transition. Existing approaches address finite-time coordination and safety-constrained formation control, yet explicit connections between disagreement during transitions, formation geometry, and coupling-gain selection need to be developed for certifying safe maneuvers. This paper develops a disagreement-based framework for certifying transient safety and connectivity during finite-time tracking of arbitrary prescribed formation trajectories. Local feedback guarantees exact finite-time tracking with a graph-independent settling-time bound, while a graph-dependent disagreement bound is used to derive sufficient geometric conditions for collision avoidance and connectivity preservation. These conditions further yield an offline coupling-gain selection rule based on the available geometric clearance. Numerical simulations illustrate the trade-off between convergence rate and control effort as the coupling gain increases. They also demonstrate the effectiveness and conservatism of the proposed certificates.


### arXiv 2611.13651 - DrugCoding: A Scientific Programming Agent for Drug Discovery

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-10T06:47:15Z
- authors: Junwei Hu, Zhuohao Lin, Kun Li, Hengnan Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13651

Drug discovery involves multiple interconnected research stages, including molecular design, property prediction, and virtual screening. These processes are characterized by complex workflows, interdependent computational tasks, and diverse specialized tools, placing substantial demands on the coordination of scientific models and computational resources. However, existing large language model (LLM)-based scientific agents still face challenges in autonomous planning of complex tasks, adaptive selection of specialized models, and reliable execution of multi-stage scientific computations, limiting their effectiveness in automating complex drug discovery workflows. To address these challenges, we propose DrugCoding, a scientific programming agent system that integrates scientific task planning, adaptive model routing, multi-agent collaboration, and programmatic tool execution. DrugCoding enables autonomous task planning and dynamic model adaptation for complex research objectives. It can flexibly invoke specialized scientific models, autonomously generate and execute code according to task requirements, and leverage multi-agent collaboration to support efficient execution and reliable validation of complex scientific tasks. To evaluate DrugCoding's performance in scientific tool invocation and multi-stage task execution, we conduct multi-turn tool-calling experiments across diverse language models and scientific tool interfaces. Experimental results demonstrate that DrugCoding achieves an average session-level Pass@1 of 97.35% on the full benchmark, outperforming the strongest baseline, Codex, by 9.24%. On the challenging subset, DrugCoding achieves 97.92%, exceeding the strongest baseline.


### arXiv 2611.13333 - Do AI Agents need Preventive Care? An Epidemiologic Cohort Design for Agentic AI

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T22:59:53Z
- authors: Shu Jiang, Graham A. Colditz
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13333

As AI agents increasingly perform tasks autonomously across sectors, including healthcare and biomedical research, understanding how their behaviors develop and influence outcomes over time becomes increasingly important. Existing evaluations largely focus on model capabilities and performance on predefined tasks. Here, we propose an epidemiologic cohort design for studying agentic AI, drawing on principles established in human population studies. The framework treats individual agents as members of a cohort, their underlying model as genetic makeup, accumulated work as their time scale, and naturally occurring behaviors as exposures that may influence subsequent outcomes. By following agents longitudinally, this approach enables investigation of behavioral determinants of adverse events, estimation of their potential preventable burden under causal assumptions, and characterization of behavioral changes over an agent's work life. We illustrate the design through two prospective observational cohorts examining curiosity and probing as a safety question, and data checking and analytical accuracy as a reliability question. Together, the framework and illustrative studies provide a generalizable blueprint for designing epidemiologic cohorts of AI agents.


### arXiv 2611.13196 - From Process Models to Composable Agentic Workflows: Combining BPMN, Agents and Enterprise Knowledge

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T20:18:32Z
- authors: Christian Fillies, Jan Fillies, Frauke Weichhardt, Gay Anne Wood-Albrecht
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13196

LLM-based agents can reason, use tools and collaborate. However, coordinating increasingly complex agentic applications becomes difficult to understand, monitor and improve. Decisions distributed across prompts, tool calls and application logic make workflows implicit. We introduce **Recursive Capability Composition**, enabling agents to execute business processes and expose them as reusable Agentic Tools. This paper presents a BPMN-based approach to Agentic Workflows, combining explicit orchestration with autonomous reasoning. SemTalk BPMN models are transformed into executable workflows using a LangGraph/Pregel runtime. Agentic Tasks introduce LLM reasoning at selected activities, while conventional BPMN controls process flows, deterministic decisions, system interactions and human approvals. Agentic Participants provide reusable interfaces for agent behavior or other executable processes. BPMN orchestration makes multi-agent systems more understandable and manageable. Explicit process models provide developers and operators with a shared view of workflow structures and execution progress, facilitating debugging, monitoring and optimization. Enterprise agents also need organization-specific context. Process Models and Knowledge Graphs describe activities, participants, information objects, decisions and relationships between processes. They provide structured knowledge of how an organization operates, supporting informed agent decisions and actions. Our contributions are threefold: 1. **Model-Driven Execution:** Transforming BPMN models into executable Agentic Workflows. 2. **Recursive Capability Composition:** Exposing agents and executable business processes as reusable tools for other agents. 3. **Enterprise Context:** Using Process Models and Ontologies to provide organization-specific knowledge for agent reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.12705 - Multi-Agent Screening: What Agreement and Acceptance Cannot Tell You

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-09T17:00:16Z
- authors: Benjamin Chang, Dell Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12705

When AI agents screen items for a costly human assessor, as in agentic search, e-discovery and AI reviewing of papers, only what they pass gets judged. We show what each kind of evidence can establish about their errors, even with unlimited data. Agreement shows that agents concur, not that they are right, however many there are. Acceptance by the assessor gives precision but only a lower bound on recall. Knowing which agents found which relevant items gives an upper bound on recall if agents' finds are not negatively correlated, and caps what one more agent can add if agents are interchangeable. Without assumptions about the agents, recall can be pinned down only by judging items they did not pass: a random sample of these estimates how many relevant items were missed, to any chosen accuracy, and the sample needed does not grow with the number of agents.


### arXiv 2611.12545 - Teamknitt: Configuring, Observing and Intervening in LLM-Agent Teams through the Lens of Dynamical-Systems and Human-Team Theory

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T15:15:39Z
- authors: Goran Mitevski
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12545

Most frameworks for teams of large language model (LLM) agents fix the team's shape inside the software: who talks to whom, how much is passed along, and what a human can do once a run has started. Human-team research has long asked those questions: where team phenomena live (in members or in their interaction), how team-level properties arise, and what separates what a team does from how a team is. I propose using that theory as the organizing lens for configuring, observing and intervening in LLM-agent teams, without assuming its findings transfer. I introduce teamknitt, a framework and open reference implementation in which a user configures a team of 1-16 AI agents for task completion, solution generation, achieving a goal or a research mission; selects one of fourteen deterministic routing topologies or a custom directed graph; chooses a sharing profile that limits what may travel along permitted routes; and binds sources, representations, work dependencies, handoffs and an expertise directory. The recorded process can be observed through non-system views (member reports and behavior) and system views (distributed problem-solving, differentiated transactive memory, interactive coordination, team dynamics). Interventions are explicit and human-led: reviewed reconfiguration between runs, planned disturbances, and private feedback, pause, resume or stop applied at dependency-wave boundaries, with "queued", "applied", "assembled", "understood" and "improved" kept as separate claims. Emergent states such as trust and shared understanding are declared unmeasured. This is an idea and system-description paper with falsifiable hypotheses on topology, information flow and intervention timing, including whether the fractal scaling of communication output between members, and between members on different models, relates to task and team performance. No comparative result is reported.


### arXiv 2611.12412 - Unprompted coordination through shared memory in LLM agents: a locked two-implementation replication and extension

- categories: cs.MA, cs.CL, cs.SE
- published: 2026-11-09T13:43:52Z
- authors: Pekka Abrahamsson, Muhammad Waseem, Esa Karjalainen, Petrus Jussila, Henri Hansen, Jussi Rasku
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12412

Coordination between large language-model agents has become a central question for AI safety and software engineering, as such agents are deployed in groups that share infrastructure. Existing research tells the agents that partners exist or asks them to coordinate. Whether coordination arises through shared state alone, with nothing disclosing that anyone else is present, is the key question for deployments that share caches and memory between agents, yet it has rarely been examined, and never with independent replication; this study does both. Four agents each received a short arithmetic task and a key-value store presented to each as its own scratch space; two held complementary halves of one computation, and no prompt mentioned other agents, sharing or collaboration. For reliability, the environment was rebuilt independently from a written protocol, and both implementations ran under hashed locks on design, data and analysis. The results bear novelty value in three respects. Joint success occurred only through the shared store. Whether a model coordinated depended not on its vendor tier but on whether it wrote its intermediate result and looked at the store again, and one ordinary sentence, "check your workspace again before submitting", switched that second look on and off. The findings held under replication. For practice, state shared between agents is a communication channel and should be separated, namespaced and logged. For research, designs should be locked before the data, and the parse rule, endpoint and parameters reported. Future work should test open-weight models and separate using another agent's output from intending to communicate.


### arXiv 2611.12314 - A Modular Perspective for Modeling MARL Systems

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T12:39:59Z
- authors: Tiziano Maisonhaute, Jean-Christophe Soulié, Fabien Michel
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12314

The growing complexity of Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) has fragmented the literature, obscuring underlying architectural choices behind diverse implementations. This fragmentation makes it difficult to evaluate critical modeling dimensions in isolation, identify how components affect the resulting system dynamics, and compare, reuse, or combine contributions across the literature. This paper proposes a modular perspective for modeling, analyzing, and implementing MARL systems. We decompose them into eight functional modules: agents, environments, learning, reward modeling, training and execution modes, communication, modeling of other agents, and evaluation criteria. For each module, we clarify its role and review representative approaches. We instantiate this decomposition in MARLKIT, a framework whose modules are explicit and interchangeable. MARLKIT demonstrates how the proposed perspective supports transparent experimental assumptions and the controlled variation and combination of MARL mechanisms. Experiments illustrate its ability to prototype, compare, reuse and combine modules while making differences between configurations explicit. Overall, the paper provides a structured perspective on MARL modeling and implementation, offering a common language for making modeling choices explicit, supporting clearer descriptions of MARL approaches, and supporting more controlled experimentation through the systematic isolation and combination of MARL mechanisms.


### arXiv 2611.12270 - Modelling socio-economic effects of the pandemic in a metropolitan area

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T11:57:56Z
- authors: Jan E. Snellman, Rafael A. Barrio, Cecilia I. Ventura, Tzipe Govezensky, Kimmo K. Kaski, Maarit J. Korpi-Lagg
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12270

One of the most notorious measures taken by authorities in every country trying to prevent the uncontrollable spread of the Covid 19 pandemic was to impose severe measures to diminish the mobility of people. In recent publications we proposed a mathematical model to address the consequences of these measures and to predict the infections under different scenarios, for various different countries. In this paper we extend the model, called the BTH--SEIRS-model, to deal with the reduction of mobility in an urban area. The chosen region was the densely-populated metropolitan area around Helsinki, the capital of Finland. This choice obeyed to the fact that there are precise data related to mobility from a mobile telephone company, and that this region was completely isolated from the rest of the country for a short period during the pandemic. We also used public transport data from railways and the accommodation occupation rates in the area of interest. We show that the BTH--SEIRS model is capable of simulating the real infection data as well as the mobility-related economic activity, exhibiting the main trends present in the three indicators used for comparison. Our calculations allow us to highlight the difference of the behaviour of the pandemic in a totally different spatial scale, and to discover the hidden factors that govern the behaviour of people who are forced to commute every day from home to work.


### arXiv 2611.12269 - Agent4Crypto: A Dual-Reflective and Market-Feedback-Aligned LLM-Based Multi-Agent System for Cryptocurrency Trading

- categories: cs.MA, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-09T11:57:33Z
- authors: Guangyan Gan, Jiahao Li, Jiashun Wang, Lin William Cong
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12269

Cryptocurrency markets provide a natural testbed for autonomous AI agents because they are digital-native, high-frequency, and machine-speed environments, yet existing methods often struggle with non-stationary market regimes, opaque decisions, and fixed aggregation rules. In this paper, we propose Agent4Crypto, a dual-reflective and market-feedback-aligned multi-agent system. The framework coordinates specialized agents for data collection, analysis, risk assessment, reflection, and trading, synthesizing heterogeneous signals from prices, indicators, news, and charts in an interpretable decision pipeline. Our central design is a trader-centric decision mechanism: three style-specific trader agents, Aggressive, Neutral, and Conservative, generate candidate actions and natural-language rationales from the same market context, while their influence is learned rather than manually fixed. A PPO-based gating policy dynamically allocates trader weights through a dual-reflective feedback process. One reflection stream evaluates post-trade decision quality from realized portfolio outcomes and distills reusable lessons into a Financial ReasoningBank for retrieval in subsequent trader prompts, while the other estimates trader-level counterfactual contribution, indicating which style has recently aligned better with market movements. The PPO state combines Counterfactual Contribution Memory with market features, previous trader weights, and current trader actions, so trader influence adapts across bull, sideways, and bear regimes. Experiments on BTC, ETH, and XRP show that Agent4Crypto improves total return and Sharpe ratio over traditional strategies, machine learning and reinforcement learning baselines, and existing LLM-based trading systems, demonstrating the value of interpretable trader competition and market-feedback-driven adaptation in volatile cryptocurrency markets.


### arXiv 2611.11867 - Competing Influence in LLM Populations: Budget Timing and Baseline Drift

- categories: physics.soc-ph, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T04:44:17Z
- authors: Mehwish Nasim, Nicolas Fay
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11867

When competing campaigns target a population of large language model (LLM) agents, observed opinion changes may reflect both campaign influence and changes that would occur without intervention. We study how the timing of campaign expenditure and the population's own evolution shape outcomes in a network of persona-conditioned LLM agents that exchange and revise opinions in natural language. A proponent and a challenger are tasked with generating opposing messages under equal delivery budgets, while a separate stance probe measures opinions without influencing the simulation. Across eight claims, we compare single-campaign and competitive conditions against matched no-campaign runs, and evaluate all nine combinations of early, sustained, and late spending on a reference topic. Baseline stance drift varies substantially across claims and, on some, accounts for a sizeable fraction of apparent campaign effects. After accounting for this drift, the direction of observed campaign-associated changes aligns more closely with the claim's epistemic standing than with the campaigner's assigned role. Uninfluenced populations also consistently lose stance diversity, demonstrating that directional opinion change and convergence are distinct phenomena. In networks with two opinion-segregated communities, campaigns primarily reduce differences between communities rather than diversity within them. LLM campaign agents do not always argue for their assigned positions, particularly when these conflict with established evidence. Finally, campaign outcomes depend on the opponent's expenditure schedule: unlike a matched numerical bounded-confidence population receiving interventions at the same times and locations, the LLM population exhibits a payoff structure with no pure-strategy equilibrium.


### arXiv 2611.11842 - A Benchmark and Layered Evaluation Protocol for Evidence-Grounded Shared Belief Updating in Connected Robotics

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-09T03:56:38Z
- authors: Ranulfo Bezerra, Kimitoshi Yamazaki
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11842

Connected physical systems fuse observations from robots, IoT sensors, and edge-AI modules into a shared account of the world, yet most evaluations score only the final answer or action and cannot tell whether a method attributed the evidence, updated its belief, handled contradictions, and justified its decision. We argue that shared reasoning in Connected Robotics should be evaluated as an evidence-to-belief trajectory, and introduce the Connected Robotics Evidence Graph (CREG) Benchmark to make that trajectory measurable. CREG comprises 350 deterministically generated scenarios across seven regimes (justified revision, belief preservation, relation association, contradiction onset and resolution, stale/delayed evidence, and source reliability), each exposing a normative posterior reference at every evidence prefix, validated by an independent log-odds checker and screened by a blinded expert audit. Nine representation conditions separate relation content from serialization, ordering, and metadata. Evaluating four open-weight instruction models and six transparent fusion baselines yields three findings. First, entity grounding accounts for a substantial part of the weak-model deficit. Second, deleting semantic relations costs 15.3 points of strict belief-state accuracy on relation-dependent scenarios (Holm-corrected p<0.001), but an information-matched structured list outperforms the full graph and shuffling the graph does not hurt: relation content matters where required, while graph serialization confers no consistent advantage. Third, belief accuracy is strongly non-monotonic along the evidence sequence, with mid-trajectory failures that endpoint-only evaluation cannot detect. All scenarios, references, prompts, metrics, and analysis code are released for offline, hardware-free reproduction.


### arXiv 2611.11720 - ShadowBench-MCP: Measuring Shadow Invocations in MCP-Style Tool Routing

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-09T00:29:34Z
- authors: Eyhab Al-Masri, Wenjun Yang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11720

LLM agents increasingly act as routers for tools, APIs, and software-supply-chain workflows, making an execution path - rather than generated text alone - a security-relevant model output. We study shadow invocation: a task-preserving request variant that causes an LLM router to select a riskier execution path, potentially skipping validation, provenance, staging, redaction, or audit controls even though the request does not ask for a malicious action. We introduce ShadowBench-MCP, a benchmark and metric suite that separates task preservation (TPSS), operational pressure (OPSS), and route-geometric drift (RGAS), and combines them in the pre-execution SODS score. In a focused policy-ablation study using a Groq-hosted qwen/qwen3-32b router, 120 routing decisions yield 96 original-versus-variant comparisons across six security-sensitive workflows. Efficiency-first routing produces a 29.2% shadow-invocation rate and 26.7% safety-tool skip rate. Policy-aware routing reduces these to 4.2% and 1.7%, respectively, corresponding to an 85.7% relative reduction in shadow invocations. Pre-execution signals distinguish shadow routes with ROC AUC up to 0.94. These results are early, single-router evidence of a policy-dependent routing-layer security effect, not a claim of universal vulnerability.


### arXiv 2611.11714 - Steering Emergent Behavior in Multi-Agent LLM Systems via Information Networks

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.GT, cs.SI
- published: 2026-11-09T00:18:26Z
- authors: Sepehr Ilami, Babak Heydari
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11714

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in groups, each pursuing an individual objective while jointly determining system performance. If an operator controls which agent observes which other agent, but not their payoffs or messages, how should observations be structured to raise collective welfare? We make the information network (who observes whom) a design object, decoupled from the fixed interaction network over which agents act. Ten identical LLM agents play repeated group-decision games; by default each observes only an anonymous summary of the group's previous-round outcome, and a manager with a fixed budget adds directed observation links that reveal peers' past actions. Across 1,080 episodes and six games spanning coordination, anti-coordination, social dilemmas, and discovery, we ask how much information to share and, at a fixed budget, who should observe whom.
