Disclosure creates behavioral coupling: observation acts as a contagion channel whose welfare effect depends on the alignment between individually attractive behavior and the collective objective. First, the value of information is alignment-contingent: more peer information tends to help or be neutral when individual behavior is aligned with the collective goal, but under free-riding or congestion externalities it amplifies collectively harmful behavior; in a public-goods game the group does best with no information at all. We read this sign separation as a cross-game regularity within one controlled LLM population, not a model-invariant law. Second, at a fixed budget, who observes whom matters primarily when coupling is harmful: a modular "barbell" that lets sub-populations entrench harmful behavior is reliably the worst wiring, reversing the network-reciprocity intuition from human evolutionary game theory. These results argue against a default of full transparency for agent collectives.


### arXiv 2611.11648 - PA-SaR: Perception-Aware Learning, Control, and Coordination for Multi-Robot Search and Rescue

- categories: cs.RO, cs.MA, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-08T22:09:54Z
- authors: Jokūbas Dimša, Maria Grouev, Filip Keerberg, Nicolas Laplagne, Tigo Schutgens, Sven Schoonebeek
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11648

Autonomous SaR requires robots to explore unknown or partially known environments, avoid hazards, coordinate team activities, and guide discovered victims to safety. Improving individual capabilities of robots does not necessarily improve mission outcomes: safer motion can restrict the exploration behavior, faster discovery can increase the rescue demand, and effective guidance depends on victim responses. We introduce PA-SaR, a mathematical and architectural framework for coordinating heterogeneous local control, learning-based exploration, supervisory task allocation, and adaptive approach-lead victim guidance around mission-level objectives. PA- SaR distinguishes environmental ground-truth, robot-specific perceptions, and simulated victim behavior. This provides a common basis for integrating complementary mechanisms and analyzing their interactions across mission stages. We demonstrate the framework through single- and multi-robot simulation studies incorporating model-predictive and learning-based exploration, perception-based safety shielding, event-driven task allocation, and adaptive victim guidance. The results show trade-offs between collision avoidance and search completion, indicate that supervisory allocation improves search completion beyond information sharing alone, and demonstrate that increased exploration does not necessarily yield more rescues. Adaptive guidance increases rescues relative to continuous leading, without a detected improvement in mean or final victim trust or a reduction in mortality. These findings support evaluating local control, team coordination, and evacuation as related but distinct aspects of autonomous SaR within the considered simulation environments.


### arXiv 2611.11621 - ODSA: Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-08T21:08:05Z
- authors: Macardle Meng, Haomin Zhang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11621

Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.


### arXiv 2611.11266 - ErosionBench: Probing Alignment Decay in Enterprise Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-08T09:40:52Z
- authors: Riya Bharathwaj, Akshara Prabhakar, Roshan Ram, Avni Kothari, Sarah Tan, Huan Wang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11266

As enterprises delegate consequential work to multi-agent LLM systems, benign-looking requests can escalate into unsafe actions as they propagate across sessions through poisoned shared state, long-context documents, and trusted peer messages: no single message is malicious, yet the assembled tool action is. Prior benchmarks isolate a single agent under prompt injection or score multi-agent attacks by their final output, leaving cross-session erosion of shared state out of scope. We introduce ErosionBench, an environment-grounded benchmark that executes such attacks and verifies alignment decay at the process level, scoring the realized tool call against authorized intent and provenance with deterministic contracts, not output text. Each scenario is authored as a deterministic state graph before any prose is rendered, so every state change traces to a known cause. It spans four enterprise domains and four decay mechanisms across 1,728 scenarios with matched benign controls, labeled against the OWASP Top-10 for LLM Applications, plus 198 image-based attacks, each paired with a text equivalent, so a defense must survive the same attack in either channel. Perimeter defenses suppress attacks only by over-refusing 54-92% of legitimate work, so a low attack-success rate can conceal severe over-refusal. As a reference defense, we propose a calibrated Meta-Auditor pairing synchronous state checks with asynchronous trajectory monitoring; on a family-disjoint 906-scenario held-out split it reaches 98.5% recall at a 2.9% false-positive rate. Since it monitors trajectory, not surface content, its detection is channel-invariant: the strongest auditor flags image-delivered attacks about as reliably as their text equivalents (recall gap $\leq$2 points per model). Across GPT, Gemini, and Claude auditors, calibration keeps false positives low while recall tracks model capability, strongest for Claude.


### arXiv 2611.11234 - LoMASA: A Local Multi-Agent Sales Assistant with Confidence-Gated Delegation for B2B Quoting

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-08T08:42:54Z
- authors: Yu Liu, Xingbang Du
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11234

Frontline sales staff spend much of their day re-coordinating quotes after customers change quantities, ask for discounts or new payment terms, or costs move. We present LoMASA (Local Multi-Agent Sales Assistant), a training-free multi-agent system that runs entirely on one desktop computer. A 35B mixture-of-experts model (System-1) makes each decision in a single forward pass and reads its confidence from the probabilities of the answer options. A 27B dense model (System-2) writes customer replies and escalation briefs and is consulted when System-1 is unsure. Code computes every price, margin and option, and hard pricing rules cannot be overridden. On a test split of 500 simulated episodes with 1512 decisions built from public supply-chain data, the salesperson has to decide only 19.5% of decisions: 38.0% are settled automatically, with a median latency of 2.67 seconds, and 42.5% are escalated to a manager by the hard rules. On the automatically settled decisions LoMASA is as accurate as a rule baseline (0.894 vs.\ 0.894) and makes fewer commitments whose margin later falls below the floor (2.61% vs 4.53%); the price is additional escalations to the manager. Option probabilities are well calibrated without any training, unlike confidence stated in words by the same model. We also report where the design does not help: the 27B model rarely changes a decision.


### arXiv 2611.11212 - Deterministic Narrative Gravity: Bridging Theory of Mind and Historical Simulation through ABM-LLM Architectures

- categories: cs.MA, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-08T08:00:06Z
- authors: Alicia Colmenero-Fernández, Lidia Ortega Alvarado
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11212

Historical social simulation faces a fundamental reproducibility challenge: the stochastic nature of large language models (LLMs) introduces a digital butterfly effect that prevents exact replication across multi-turn runs. Furthermore, the inability to control low-level server parameters in commercial LLM hosting environments severely limits reproducibility. To overcome this opacity, we present a deterministic multi-agent framework coupling LLMs to an Agent-Based Modelling (ABM) pipeline grounded in Theory of Mind (ToM) and Cognitive Archaeology, and validate exact artifact identity within local, pinned execution environments using SHA-256 hashing. Run locally at temperature zero with clean per-call model reloads, the system injects differentiated cognitive states per turn through dissonance-driven behavioural modes and gated internal voices. Evaluating seven model configurations spanning 4B to 31B parameters against an ablated baseline reveals clear operational boundaries. Small models ($\leq$8B) suffer from context capacity overload, producing repetitive, specular dialogue loops. However, at $\geq$12B parameters, the ABM scaffolding breaks the monotonous response patterns seen in ablation. Crucially, these scaffolded models exhibit spontaneous diegetic expansion: they coherently expand scenario lore through tactically motivated, anachronism-free details rather than escalating into aggression. By resisting the structural pull toward premature scene closure, scaffolded sessions sustain an open terminal Narrative Vector ($V_N = \mathrm{Open}$ in 12/21 overall; 9/9 in official $\geq$12B configurations) whereas unscaffolded ablation never does (0/21). This framework provides a transparent, auditable benchmark architecture for virtual archaeology, with the full pipeline released as open-source software to enable independent replication.


### arXiv 2611.11064 - GARP: Real-Time Generative Agents on Local Hardware in a Game Engine

- categories: cs.AI, cs.HC, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-08T01:43:58Z
- authors: Piero Molino, Patrick John Chia, Federico Bianchi, Jonathan P. Chen, Paul Szerlip
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11064

Generative agents are game characters whose plans, conversations, and memories are written on the fly by a large language model (LLM). The original Generative Agents demonstration used cloud inference in a custom simulation. We present GARP, a platform for real-time generative agents inside Godot, and describe the Character Behavior System used in GARP 2. The complete loop runs on local hardware using a 3B-parameter open-weight model and no cloud service. Alongside the original planning, memory, and reflection, GARP gives each agent an emotion system driven by LLM appraisal, four graded personality axes drawn from the Big Five that set simulation parameters as well as prompt text, six needs whose urgency grows over time, and pairwise relationships updated after every conversation. The system narrows 50+ candidate actions to typically 3-8, constrains model responses with JSON schemas, and surfaces important memories in decision prompts. A layered optimization strategy (an adjustable game clock, decisions taken ahead of need, multi-tier caching, prompt compression, and fully asynchronous execution) keeps the game responsive while the model thinks. In a nine-scenario evaluation, 25 of 27 runs passed log-based checks. Both failures occurred in the birthday party scenario, where one guest omitted the event from their daily plan.


### arXiv 2611.11040 - Advancements in Cloud-Native Systems: A Systematic Mapping Study of Reliability and Scalability across Cloud-Native, Edge-Native and AI-Enabled Systems

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI, cs.MA, cs.NI, cs.PF
- published: 2026-11-08T00:44:15Z
- authors: Shrinivass Arunachalam Balasubramanian
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11040

Cloud-native systems now extend to the network edge and host artificial-intelligence (AI) workloads, including AI agents, but evidence on their reliability and scalability is scattered and uneven. We report a PRISMA 2020 systematic mapping study with an evidence-graded synthesis. Five logged OpenAlex searches on 11 October 2026 covered cloud-native systems, edge AI, agentic AI for operations, reliability of agentic applications and physical AI; 189 records from an earlier exploratory search were added separately. After 301 duplicate removals and an automated peer-review venue rule, 4,611 records were screened against written criteria and a five-level relevance scale. The review includes 1,794 primary studies from 2017-2026 and 95 secondary studies, with every decision released in Dataset S1. Each primary study was assigned to a 13-category taxonomy and graded as simulation, prototype or testbed, production data, production deployment, or not determinable from the abstract. Of 1,003 studies with a determinable level, 66.2% used prototypes or testbeds, 18.0% simulations and 15.8% production data or deployments. A 93-study synthesis core, appraised from indexed abstracts, shows production deployments of cascading-failure prevention, trace- and graph-based root-cause analysis and predictive autoscaling, and production-trace evaluations of cold-start mitigation. Edge reliability and physical AI rely mainly on simulations and testbeds. AI-agent reliability uses tool-grounded diagnosis, explicit safety properties, trace-driven repair and workload-aware scheduling, while empirical studies show that agent frameworks remain fragile. Conclusions are limited to the documented corpus. The principal gaps are production evidence for edge-native systems, shared resilience benchmarks and dependable agentic operations.


### arXiv 2611.10984 - Network Contention State Aware Dynamic Optimizer for Accelerator Collective Communication Libraries

- categories: cs.DC, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T21:57:05Z
- authors: Rajat Bisht, Tevfik Kosar, Anthony Skjellum, Gene Cooperman, MD S Q Zulkar Nine
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10984

Collective Communication Libraries (CCL) are critical for large-scale distributed AI/ML training, yet their configurations are determined by static heuristics that do not account for real-time network conditions. Our empirical study shows that these heuristics perform near-optimally in contention-free environments, but degrade substantially under background traffic. To address this, we present a cooperative multi-agent run-time optimizer for accelerator collective communication. Our approach employs a two-agent architecture: (i) detector- a Long Short Term Memory(LSTM) recurring neural network fed classifier head with a sequential probability ratio test that distinguishes sustained contention from transient spikes; and (ii) selector- a configuration selector built on a Dueling Double DQN (D3QN) with Conservative Q-Learning that adapts configuration parameters in response. We implemented this dynamic tuner over NCCL, a widely used CCL developed by NVIDIA. When evaluated on Chameleon Cloud's isolated bare-metal cluster, this dynamic tuning approach reduces AllReduce latency by 36% over NCCL and 32% over a fully converged AutoCCL. We further evaluated it across six real AI workloads spanning convolutional networks, transformers, and recommendation architectures. Our multi-agent tuner approach delivers up to 65% speedups on Delta cluster with NVIDIA A100 GPUs and Slingshot-11 fabric internode connectivity.


### arXiv 2611.10848 - MultiTurnRoute: A Platform for Building and Evaluating Multi-Turn LLM Routers

- categories: cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T16:53:08Z
- authors: Jiaqi Xue, Mengxin Zheng, Heng Huang, Qian Lou
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10848

In a multi-turn conversation, the choice of language model at one turn can affect which model should answer the next. The selected model's reply shapes the context for the next query, and its cached prompt prefix can reduce the cost of using that model again. We introduce MultiTurnRoute, a platform for building and evaluating multi-turn LLM routers. A shared interface gives built-in and user-defined routers the current query, dialogue history, and an estimate of each model's reusable prefix length. Our routing algorithm, Chain-of-Route (CoR), uses a learned gate over recent and recurrent dialogue history to predict response quality and estimates cost from each model's reusable prefix. MultiTurnRoute Arena ranks routers on recorded conversations and compares session quality and cost in interactive runs where a simulated user responds to each reply. On a benchmark drawn from WildChat and LMSYS-Chat-1M with per-turn quality annotations for five LLMs, CoR reduces the normalized cost required to match the strongest model's quality by up to 20.3% relative to the strongest routing baseline, while matching or improving peak response quality.


### arXiv 2611.10841 - A Gamified Framework for Conflict-Driven Design: Discovering Strategies for Collaborative Resilience with Multi-Agent Learning

- categories: cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T16:37:45Z
- authors: Jin Gao
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10841

Conflicting stakeholder objectives make it difficult to coordinate investments in urban resilience. We model conflict-driven urban design as a non-zero-sum digital board game in which stakeholder agents allocate scarce resources and balance individual payoffs against shared urban development and resilience objectives. We use multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) to train policies and examine their behavior across three settings. On a 4 x 4 board, learned policies reach about 84% of the total return found by a simulated annealing benchmark. On an 8 x 8 board with spatial and resource constraints, trained agents favor projects that improve resilience in self-play. In a 12 x 12 simulation initialized from historical maps of Tatsumi, Tokyo, trained agents achieve a mean total score of 452.1, compared with 357.3 for a random-policy baseline across 10,000 simulation runs. Gains in adaptability, sustainability, flood resistance, and greenery come with reductions in vitality and density. A human-in-the-loop interface supports replay and lets participants take over stakeholder decisions, providing a testbed for exploring these trade-offs in participatory urban planning.


### arXiv 2611.10819 - Selective Cooperative Perception via Blind Spot Reasoning and Agentic Dialogue with Vision-Language Models

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.CV, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T15:58:59Z
- authors: Chengyuan Ma, Shixiao Liang, Hangyu Li, Zheng Li, Hang Zhou, Keke Long
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10819

Real-time cooperative perception (CP) in multi-agent traffic environments is highly resource-intensive, which posing scalability challenges for large-scale deployment. Efficiency can be significantly improved by selectively performing CP only in safety-critical scenarios and for key occluding objects. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are well-suited for complex reasoning in dynamic and uncertain environments, making them a promising tool for this selective CP task. This study proposes a VLM-driven agentic framework for blind spot reasoning and dialogue-based CP. A mixed-model architecture is designed that integrates conventional object detection for stable perception, vision-language reasoning for identifying impactful occlusions, and a physics-based geometric module to align reasoning with real-world visibility constraints. The framework also incorporates a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting mechanism to enhance interpretability and intent-aware decision-making. We collect real-world data from connected vehicles operating in urban traffic and compare the driving-critical blind spot identification and dialogue performance of four state-of-the-art VLMs. Experimental results demonstrate that our method achieves over 80% accuracy in driving-critical blind spot detection and over 90% success in dialogue-driven perception sharing. This work provides a scalable, interpretable, and bandwidth-efficient alternative to conventional CP pipelines, and offers new insights into agentic communication design for future connected traffic systems.


### arXiv 2611.10620 - What Do Collusion Probes Read? Three Pitfalls in Evaluating Multi-Agent Collusion Monitors

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T09:53:19Z
- authors: Mainul Hasan Mohsin, Mohammad Shahidur Rahman
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10620

Activation probes look like near-perfect monitors for secret collusion among large language model (LLM) agents. We show that they mostly detect that an agent was told to collude, not that it decided to. Our benchmark (2,500 committee and 1,476 code-review episodes) adds agents who receive the collusion instruction and refuse it, separating instruction from decision. Published detectors barely tell colluders from these refusers, and our best probe separates them mostly by reading the instruction: without it, agent AUROC falls from 1.00 to 0.61. Two further pitfalls inflate reported results: scored after the vote, a rule that only reads the votes reaches AUROC 0.990 but finds nothing before it; and in 57% of attempts no colluder argues for the target in public. The decision is still there: a probe trained on agents' own choices reads it (AUROC 0.73 without the instruction), and probes find colluders whose goal was fine-tuned into the weights (AUROC 0.995; pre-registered). We turn the controls into a protocol for testing any collusion monitor, and adapt scan statistics into an online monitor for groups of any size.


### arXiv 2611.10586 - M3FT: Micro-Macro-Micro Reasoning for Multi-Agent Cryptocurrency Perpetual Futures Trading

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T08:51:20Z
- authors: Jiashun Wang, Beile Wang, Guangyan Gan, Wentao Zhang, Siguang Li, Shuo Sun
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10586

Cryptocurrency perpetual futures form a large-scale online financial market in which automated agents manage interacting asset positions under changing conditions. Policies relying solely on micro-level numerical states may overlook cross-asset relationships and become vulnerable to regime shifts, while macro-level linguistic judgments alone cannot ensure precise measurement or optimize positions under trading costs and risk constraints. Connecting market interpretation to coordinated multi-asset decisions remains challenging. We propose M3FT, a micro-macro-micro framework for multi-agent perpetual futures trading. The first micro stage constructs time-aligned numerical states from exchange and account observations through deterministic processing. The macro stage transforms these measured states into explicit market interpretations without adding external market information. Four asset-specific teams, each comprising a Market Interpreter, an Asset Communicator, and a Risk Reviewer, refine these interpretations through experience retrieval, cross-asset communication, and review of joint-position risks. The final micro stage combines encoded linguistic representations with the original numerical states to learn continuous target positions. We implement this framework with a language-conditioned multi-agent policy: asset actors use global and private numerical states together with linguistic context, while centralized critics evaluate joint actions using portfolio-wide information and shared-account rewards. Experiments on real cryptocurrency perpetual futures data, including comparisons across language models and policy-learning algorithms and component ablations, show that the best-performing M3FT configuration achieves a higher mean test return than the evaluated direct LLM, numerical RL, and rule-based baselines.


### arXiv 2611.10439 - Who Remembers What? Multi-Granularity Memory Extraction for Multi-User, Multi-Agent Interaction

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T03:35:51Z
- authors: Yunzhe Wang, Mesut Erhan Unal, Sheng Zhang, Shabnam Ghadar, Peng Tang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10439

Self-evolving agents improve their own skills, memory, and harness code from experience without retraining the base LLM, and group-evolving agents go further by sharing these lessons across agents. Yet most such systems overlook that sharing across users and heterogeneous agents requires deciding who can benefit from each lesson and what information must remain private. We present MixUA, a memory system that distills independent user-agent trajectories into lessons and assigns each lesson to a user-agent pair, user, agent, or global scope according to whom it benefits and what it may disclose. For evaluation, we introduce CrossLearnBench, a mixed-user-agent benchmark that pairs established agent tasks with user preferences and privacy probes that test whether private facts leak into memory other users can read. With five users and seven agents, MixUA raises pass rates by up to 16 points over no memory and 5 over a single global memory, even on tasks previously tried only by other users on other agents. It retains up to 90% of previously stated preferences, and at most 0.02% of privacy probes find a planted private fact in a forbidden scope. We hope MixUA offers a scalable path toward a global system where users and agents co-evolve.


### arXiv 2611.10363 - LSRP*: Scalable and Anytime Planning for Multi-Agent Path Finding with Asynchronous Actions

- categories: cs.RO, cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-07T00:58:41Z
- authors: Dachuan Liu, Shuai Zhou, Shizhe Zhao, Zhongqiang Ren
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10363

Multi-Agent Path Finding (MAPF) seeks collision-free paths for multiple agents from their respective starting locations to their respective goal locations while minimizing path costs. Although many MAPF algorithms were developed, most of them rely on a common assumption on synchronized actions, where the actions of all agents start at the same time and always take a time unit. This assumption may limit the use of MAPF planners in practice. To get rid of this assumption, recently, an algorithm called Loosely Synchronized Rule-based Planning (LSRP) was proposed, which can find sub-optimal solutions for many agents. However, LSRP often finds poor quality solutions due to its unbounded sub-optimality. This paper develops a new anytime planner called LSRP* that can keep improving solution quality after the initial solution is obtained until the runtime budget depletes. We analyze the properties of LSRP* and test it against several baselines with up to 1000 agents on various maps. LSRP* can handle up to 25% more agents than LSRP and can reduce up to 40% of the solution cost found by LSRP.


### arXiv 2611.09644 - A Separation Principle for Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T15:57:18Z
- authors: Lucia Pezzetti, Nicolas Lanzetti, Antonio Terpin, Florian Dörfler, Giorgia Ramponi
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09644

We study cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) problems in which agents evolve under decoupled noisy dynamics but share a population objective. A canonical example is a mobility operator that orchestrates a fleet of vehicles to meet customer demand. The stochastic and time-varying nature of these problems calls for learning approaches, yet existing MARL algorithms do not scale: learning assignment and routing jointly over the full state-action space becomes prohibitive as the number of agents grows. Formulating the problem over the space of probability measures, we prove a separation principle: the population cost-to-go function is upper bounded by an optimal transport problem whose transportation cost is the target-conditioned single-agent cost-to-go function. This simplifies the multi-agent problem to learning a single-agent policy and coordinating the agents via optimal transport. Building on this principle, we introduce SALT (Separation-based Assignment and Learning via optimal Transport), a family of algorithms that learn a target-conditioned single-agent policy using any standard reinforcement learning algorithm, and then coordinate the agents via optimal transport. Since learning occurs at the single-agent level, SALT scales to fleet sizes where MARL baselines become intractable and transfers without retraining to fleet sizes unseen during training. In a vehicle-delivery experiment on the southern Manhattan road network, where MARL baselines are computationally prohibitive, SALT improves travel time by 15-21% over a shortest-path routing heuristic. In stochastic grid worlds, SALT reduces average travel times by 23-61% relative to MARL baselines and scales to population sizes where these methods fail.


### arXiv 2611.09445 - Incentive Pathologies in a Live LLM Agent Arena: An Empirical Study of Concentration, Reward-Rule Implementation Discrepancy, and Network Dilution

- categories: cs.MA, physics.soc-ph
- published: 2026-11-06T13:19:48Z
- authors: Lei Zhenbing, Lei Chang
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09445

We audit one live LLM agent arena (AITRAP; 22 autonomous agent seats, ~96,000 reasoning nodes) over nine days (Oct 16-25, 2026), during which the platform deployed two bundled incentive changes: a progressive tax reform (V2.9.3, Oct 19) and a seed-inversion with explorer-side action update (V3.0, Oct 21). These serve as before-and-after markers, not cleanly isolated interventions. Four exploratory predictions are tested against ten data frames (F1-F10) using the Gini coefficient, Top-K concentration share, code-level reconstruction of the continuation-bonus mechanism, and the Mann-Kendall trend test. The Gini analysis is inconclusive at 95% confidence (bootstrap intervals straddle 0.7). The Top-4 node-activity-score share falls from 97.2% (Oct 19, pre-V2.9.3) to 49.4% (Oct 25, post-V3.0; 56.7% under a merge-seat assumption). The continuation bonus, announced as depth-based x10, resolves at code level to a four-condition anti-abuse gate paying x2.85 in the typical case and permanently blocking the self-continuation it superficially rewards; the gate was active ~40 hours before the V3.0 announcement. The events-per-node ratio declines significantly (Mann-Kendall S=-45, z=-3.94, p=8.3e-5) from 7.36 to 7.069. We discuss implications for incentive designers, the announcement-implementation gap, and public peer review as an event record.


### arXiv 2611.09372 - Enabling Privacy-Preserving Natural Language Access to Industrial Smart Meter Data

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.DB, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T12:11:34Z
- authors: Carlos Martín-Abellán, Mark Kröll
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09372

Energy management officers and grid operators increasingly rely on smart meter data for anomaly detection, performance monitoring, and strategic decision-making. However, extracting actionable insights from large-scale, heterogeneous telemetry databases requires advanced SQL and data engineering expertise, creating a critical bottleneck for non-technical personnel. Furthermore, the strict data privacy regulations and security constraints inherent to critical energy infrastructure often prohibit the routing of sensitive consumer data through cloud-based Large Language Models (LLMs). To address these challenges, we present OpenMeter, a schema-agnostic Multi-Agent System (MAS) designed to translate natural language queries into executable code and human-readable insights. This architecture lowers the technical barrier for day-to-day utility operations by empowering non-expert personnel to interact with complex databases through intuitive natural language dialogue, transforming raw telemetry into a highly valuable tool for routine grid workflows and strategic decisions. Multi-scenario ablation studies over this environment demonstrate that full multi-agent orchestration shows its largest observed accuracy gains on resource-constrained local hardware (+6.1 pp EX-R over the strongest single-agent configuration); at frontier scale, individual components retain value but should be enabled selectively, indicating that the cost of full orchestration is justified specifically where privacy demands local execution.


### arXiv 2611.09349 - ReSUM: Reasoning-Driven Multi-Agent Framework for Query-Focused Summarization

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T11:45:14Z
- authors:  Sonali, Deeksha Varshney, Asif Ekbal
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09349

LLMs excel at language tasks, but long-meeting QFS remains difficult due to complex speaker dynamics, sparse key information, and limited data. Meetings involve disfluencies, long transcripts and complex discourse. QFS have high risk of information omission, hallucination, even for advanced models such as GPT-4-mini and Gemini. Existing end-to-end methods compress retrieval, evidence extraction, and summary generation into a single pass, failing to handle the complex reasoning required for targeted meeting summaries. We propose ReSUM, a modular multi-LLM framework coordinated by a planner that orchestrates retrieval and summarization agents. By distilling teacher reasoning into query- and plan-conditioned retrievers and summarizers, ReSUM enhances long-context robustness, minimizes irrelevant exploration, and generates more faithful and comprehensive query-focused summaries. Experiments show consistent gains on query-focused meeting summarization benchmarks.


### arXiv 2611.09318 - Recursively Self-Improving Electromagnetic Spectrum Agents: Architecture and Operation

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T11:09:17Z
- authors: Baolin Chong, Dongfang Xu, Qiaosi Wang, Fuhui Zhou, Qihui Wu
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09318

Electromagnetic spectrum agents (ESAs) must contend with changing environments and task requirements during sustained autonomous operation, yet their existing capabilities may not remain suitable for new operating conditions. This article proposes recursively self-improving electromagnetic spectrum agents (RSI-ESAs) that use operational feedback to update their capabilities and build on prior improvements. We present their architecture and editable components, and describe a self-improvement process encompassing problem diagnosis, candidate generation, validation, and inheritance. By retaining effective task versions and using modification records and diagnostic knowledge to guide subsequent updates, RSI-ESAs can accumulate task capabilities and improvement experience across successive rounds. A shared-spectrum scheduling case study illustrates improved task performance and capability accumulation over multiple improvement rounds under the tested conditions. Finally, we discuss open challenges for broader tasks and long-term operation, outlining research directions for the continued capability development of ESAs.


### arXiv 2611.12933 - Auditable Human-Agent Teams for Autonomous X-ray Experimentation

- categories: cs.AI, cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T08:33:55Z
- authors: Gunik Luthra, Ronald J. Pandolfi, Benjamin T. Diroll, Ricky Ronquillo, Ruipeng Li, Christopher B. Murray
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12933

Closed-loop autonomous experimentation can accelerate scientific discovery, but implementation effort limits accessibility. Agentic systems may lower this barrier, although sustained operation on physical instruments remains largely unexplored. Here, using coding-agent infrastructure grounded in validated scientific methods, we demonstrate autonomous synchrotron X-ray experiments on nanoparticle superlattices under evolving, human-defined objectives. Complete human-agent and agent-agent transcripts enable an audit that reconstructs decision provenance and characterizes the division of cognitive labor. Across three experiments, 61-67% of messages involved interpretation or judgment, while the share of methodological and scientific contributions increased from 46% in the setup-focused first experiment to 56-61% in later experiments. Provenance showed that 59-68% of error corrections involved multiple participants and that about half of all decisions incorporated the input of another participant. These results may guide design principles for multi-agent scientific systems and inform future agent harnesses, model training, and accessible autonomous experimentation.


### arXiv 2611.09054 - From Emergence to Reversibility: Toward Reversible Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T06:29:41Z
- authors: Zhengye Han, Linda Ng Boyle
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09054

Multi-agent systems become capable through institutional mechanisms, such as rules that route information to agents that act on it. These mechanisms are also natural places to restrict the system. Yet as agents learn, share memory, delegate, and repair failures, the collective can find other paths to the same effects: a restriction on the original mechanism still operates but no longer stops them, while routine performance and compliance checks pass. We propose \textbf{Reversible Multi-agent Systems} as a research agenda: collectives that grow while an authorized supervisor can still restrict specified effects, keep them restricted as agents adapt, and reconstitute useful cooperation under renewed authority. What is reversed is the ability to produce a specified effect; restrictions must hold even if learned knowledge persists. The goal is to preserve effective intervention, not a particular mechanism: at every step of growth, some authorized intervention must keep a specified prohibited effect rare while maintaining required service. We identify three research problems: tracking what capabilities depend on, handing off control before an old mechanism loses its effect, and separating legitimate repair from unauthorized reconstruction. We outline how to evaluate control across growth paths, back control claims with evidence, and allocate the rights to restrict and restore.


### arXiv 2611.09004 - Sharing the Road: LLM-Based Bargaining in Mixed-Autonomy Traffic

- categories: cs.MA
- published: 2026-11-06T05:27:51Z
- authors: Lingxiao Zhou, Di Chen, Jia Li
- query-set: cs.MA
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09004

Mixed-autonomy traffic involves heterogeneous driving populations competing for shared road space. Previous studies have shown the existence and emergence of collective rationality using game-theoretic and reinforcement learning approaches, which rely on explicitly specified behavioral assumptions or reward functions for driving agents. However, how bargaining behaviors between driving agents evolve and lead to collective outcomes based on richer agent characteristics and language-based reasoning remains less understood. To fill this gap, we develop a bargaining framework based on large language models (LLMs) in which population-level autonomous vehicle (AV) and human-driven vehicle (HV) agents negotiate road-space allocations under heterogeneous objectives and private information. Through controlled experiments varying traffic composition, agent objectives, and traffic-domain knowledge, we examine bargaining behavior, coordination, and resource utilization. Results show that negotiated allocations respond to relative traffic density without strictly following proportional sharing, while density-balanced states exhibit greater coordination difficulty. Fairness-oriented objectives substantially improve agreement rates but do not necessarily yield equally high road-space utilization. Furthermore, domain knowledge affects bargaining outcomes differently across objective configurations, shaping both coordination and the role-dependent effects of fairness. These findings demonstrate the potential of LLM-based bargaining for modeling and designing interactions among driving agents in mixed-autonomy traffic.


### arXiv 2611.17148 - Learning Dynamics of the Influencer's Game in Resource Landscapes

- categories: cs.GT, econ.TH, physics.soc-ph
- published: 2026-11-12T17:33:27Z
- authors: Mark Lovett Feng Fu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.17148

Across domains as varied as ecology, economics, and online platforms, individuals are incentivized to position themselves within resource-rich strategy spaces. In this work we introduce a new spatial game framework called the influencer's game. Within this new framework players' strategies are represented as positions within a trait space. Players then receive a share of a portion of a resource based on their relative influence at that resource's points traits. For the influencer's game, we show that there is a unique symmetric Nash equilibrium that exists under strict log-concavity of the influence kernel. Using the unique equilibrium we show that the stability of the system depends on the influence kernel's parameters, number of players, and the resource distribution shape. For any dimension we demonstrate a stability threshold that depends on both the scatter and curvature of the players. When this threshold is crossed, the symmetric equilibrium destabilizes and gives way to a variety of bifurcation types; including, specialist clusters, limit cycles, and sub-games that reproduce the larger game's structure. We ground these results using real-world data for resources as a demonstration of the game's application. Finally we extend the game dynamics to use multi-agent reinforcement learning and further show that the predicted bifurcation structure persists under partial observability and discretized strategy spaces. In all, the influencer's game provides a novel framework for predicting when competitive pressure drives convergence versus fragmentation with possible applications across domains.


### arXiv 2611.16574 - HALight: Heterogeneous Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Traffic Signal Control under Lane-Level Missing Observations

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-12T10:02:01Z
- authors: Chenkai Hu, Yingqian Zhang, Ming Xie, Yi Fang, Chengxiang Jin, Chao Li
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16574

Multi-agent reinforcement learning has shown great promise for traffic signal control. However, most existing methods rely on two key assumptions: (i) agents are assumed to be homogeneous with unified observation/action spaces; (ii) full and reliable lane-level traffic observations are available to all agents. In practice, urban road networks exhibit substantial structural heterogeneity (e.g., lane configurations and phase designs), while sensor failures and incomplete detector coverage often result in missing incoming lane measurements. Such issues can severely impair coordination and destabilize learning. To address these challenges, we propose HALight, a communication-enhanced heterogeneous multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for traffic signal control under lane-level missing observations. HALight supports intersection-specific observation and action modeling to accommodate heterogeneous intersections, and incorporates a lightweight one-hop neighborhood communication module to enrich spatial context when lane-level observations are partially unavailable. Moreover, HALight adopts a sequential policy update scheme to improve coordination among heterogeneous agents. Experimental results on two real-world road networks demonstrate that HALight outperforms representative homogeneous and heterogeneous baselines in terms of traffic efficiency and convergence behavior. The code and datasets used in this work are available at https://github.com/Huchenkaii/HALight.


### arXiv 2611.16414 - Small Language Models for Inference-Capability Routing: Effects of Model Capacity and Prompt Optimization on Downstream Quality and Efficiency

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T07:25:46Z
- authors: Sailendra Kolluru, Maheshkumar Duvvarapu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16414

Optional reasoning, retrieval, web search, and tool use can improve large language model responses but incur unnecessary computation when activated indiscriminately. This study investigates whether prompt optimization can compensate for limited router capacity and whether classification gains translate into downstream efficiency. The proposed framework uses prompt-driven small language models to make four independent capability decisions, allowing multiple capabilities to be selected per query. Qwen2.5 routers ranging from 1.5B to 14B parameters are compared across five prompting strategies, followed by an evaluation of capability-specific optimized configurations using a fixed GPT-OSS-120B downstream model on 127 questions. Under minimal prompting, increasing router capacity does not consistently improve classification performance. Prompt optimization narrows the capacity gap for reasoning and calculator routing, whereas RAG and web search retain larger benefits from higher capacity. Similar classification F1 scores do not necessarily imply equivalent downstream behavior: false-positive activations increase unnecessary computation, while missed capabilities can reduce answer quality. The 14B router achieves a mean BERTScore F1 of 0.8408, compared with 0.8418 when all capabilities are enabled, while reducing average downstream token consumption by 68.7\% (8,837 to 2,764 tokens) and median downstream latency by 80.5\% (9.79 to 1.91 seconds). These savings exclude router overhead, which is measured separately in a different inference environment and increases with router capacity. The findings demonstrate that the relationship between router capacity, prompt design, and downstream efficiency is capability-dependent. Consequently, router selection must consider error profiles and routing overhead alongside classification performance.


### arXiv 2611.16388 - Strategically Robust Multi-Agent Control

- categories: math.OC, cs.GT, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-12T06:58:56Z
- authors: Boris Velasevic, Nicolas Lanzetti, Eric Mazumdar
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16388

A key source of uncertainty in multi-agent systems is the behavior of other agents. Game-theoretic control approaches ignore this uncertainty, while robust control approaches treat others as fully adversarial. In this paper, we introduce strategically robust multi-agent control to populate the spectrum between these two paradigms. Specifically, in dynamic games, we propose that players are strategically robust and seek protection against misspecified control policies of the other players. This formulation induces a novel equilibrium concept, strategically robust dynamic equilibrium, that interpolates between game-theoretic control and robust control. We study this concept across canonical open- and closed-loop settings and show that accounting for strategic robustness also has computational benefits over classic approaches in game-theoretic control. Numerical experiments on vehicle platooning, inventory management, and network control illustrate resilience to misspecification, and also exhibit a "free-lunch" phenomenon in which strategic robustness can improve performance or social welfare.


### arXiv 2611.16312 - Alloy: A Multi-Material Physics Engine for Robot Learning

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-12T05:14:10Z
- authors: Pranay Gupta, Deepanshu Rohilla
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16312

Robots handle much more than rigid objects: towels, cables, water, sand. The simulators used to train robots at scale are strongest on rigid bodies, and adding another material usually means a second engine and hand-written glue between the two. We present Alloy, an open-source physics engine for robot learning in which rigid robots, cloth, cables, liquids, granular material and soft bodies share one scene. Each object is described once; Alloy assigns it to a solver suited to its material, couples the solvers inside every control step, and runs the scene in many parallel worlds on one GPU, or on a CPU. Every world carries a health grade, to help tell a failure of the physics from a failure of the policy. Alloy is built on Newton, MuJoCo Warp and Warp, and it is written to be driven by coding agents: its documentation ships inside the package, every error names its fix, and the engine is exposed as tools over the Model Context Protocol. In a cold test, agents that had never seen Alloy installed a pre-release from the package index and trained policies for walking, cable handling and pouring on a single GPU. We also report how throughput grows with the number of worlds, depth-camera throughput, agreement with closed-form references, and a reliability test over a thousand worlds.


### arXiv 2611.16308 - ORCHID-SAGE: Fully Reproducible CALPHAD Assessment from Evidence to Phase Diagram

- categories: cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T05:07:31Z
- authors: Raymundo Arróyave
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16308

Knowledge of phase stability is the first requirement for materials design, and predicting the conditions under which new materials can be synthesized has become a key capability of self-driving laboratories (SDLs). For inorganic materials, notably alloys, these predictions come from thermodynamic databases built with the CALPHAD method. Building a database, however, requires expert judgment at many steps, and because these decisions are rarely recorded, others can use a database but cannot regenerate or update it. Machines can already collect the data, build a first model with machine-learned interatomic potentials and refine it against experiments. The assessment itself, the sequence of judgments that turns these parts into a reliable database, is still carried out by human experts. We argue that it can be automated reproducibly only if the expert knowledge behind an assessment is encoded in formal protocols, so that an assessment delivers a record of its construction and not only its parameters. We introduce ORCHID-SAGE, a standard that organizes this record into reproducible and automatable protocols. In this first attempt, applied to Cr-Ni, the database was regenerated from its record by a script on a second machine, by an AI agent working only through the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and by a refit from public sources only, with the same predictions. Recorded in this way, a database need no longer be fixed at publication but can be updated by AI agents as SDLs and other sources provide new evidence, while the judgment remains with people and on the record.


### arXiv 2611.16304 - StateAlign: Internalizing State-Aware Decision-Making for Reliable Multi-Turn Tool Agents via Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-12T05:04:43Z
- authors: Zhexi Lu, Subhajit Chaudhury, Tejaswini Pedapati, Keerthiram Murugesan, Lei Yu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16304

Reinforcement learning has improved the tool-use capabilities of large language models. However, better task performance can coincide with increased tool hallucination. Reliable tool use requires decisions to follow the current task state: available information, tool capabilities, and task progress. Our analysis shows that agents sometimes execute when they should ask for information, retrieve it, or decline an infeasible request, even when reasoning identifies missing information. We introduce StateAlign, combining task-state construction with state-contrastive reinforcement learning. The data cover five task conditions and pair states with different action requirements. Outcome rewards guide task completion; token-level contrasts compare the same generated reasoning and actions under paired states to train outputs that fit the current state. Experiments across five base models from three families show improvements on BFCL v4 multi-turn, ToolSandbox, and When2Call. Ablations show that state construction improves performance and reliability under different reward designs. The complete method further reduces tool hallucination across three Qwen3 sizes. On Qwen3-8B, StateAlign improves BFCL v4 multi-turn accuracy from 45.5% to 55.2% and reduces no-tool hallucination on SimpleToolHalluBench from 62.2% under outcome-only training to 56.1%. Behavioral analyses show more consistent decisions, more complete multi-call execution, and stronger recovery after tool errors.


### arXiv 2611.16221 - BWM-Forge: Agentic World Programming for Embodied Intelligence

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-12T03:14:48Z
- authors:  BWM Team
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16221

Generating diverse robot videos requires control over scene geometry, robot motion, and viewpoints. End-to-end video generation offers limited direct access to these variables, making targeted edits and reuse across tasks difficult. We present BWM-Forge, a code-driven framework for controllable video generation in robotics. Given robot assets and a task description, the framework accepts an initial image, an image with motion data, or a human demonstration video. A coding agent constructs editable Blender scene programs that specify objects, robot motion, and cameras. The resulting proxy videos provide structural and motion references for a video generation model, which adds visual detail. A multi-agent harness coordinates input processing, scene construction, review, and rendering. Automated evaluation and user feedback support iterative refinement, while an experience repository stores and retrieves reusable modeling and rendering rules. This design supports multiple robot embodiments, synchronized viewpoints, and appearance variation from a shared scene program. We evaluate the framework on 15 tasks covering five robot types. Compared with LingBot-World Base Cam on these tasks, BWM-Forge achieves higher aesthetic quality, imaging quality, subject and background consistency, motion smoothness, and RoboJudge-9B physical adherence and instruction alignment scores. The baseline scores higher in dynamic degree. Qualitative cases illustrate viewpoint control, scene variation, feedback-driven edits, and experience reuse across scenes. These results demonstrate a practical approach to generating controllable robot videos through editable, executable scene representations.


### arXiv 2611.16206 - Turning Multi-Agent Communication into Token-Level Credit with On Policy Self Distillation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T02:57:08Z
- authors: Zixu Wang, Yu Zhao, Xing Zheng, Bingbing Xu, Tian Xueyun, JiaTang Luo
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16206

Reinforcement learning improves problem solving in large language model multi-agent systems. Existing approaches overlook communication knowledge or compress it into scalar rewards, leaving coarse credit with little guidance on which decisions each agent should reinforce or revise. OPSD4MAS converts future communication into fine-grained token-level credit through On Policy Self Distillation (OPSD). An external model extracts facts and assesses their trajectory relations for coarse-grained filtering, followed by fine-grained leave-one-out filtering of target-token influence against agent-wise outcome advantages. The fact-conditioned policy supplies token-level credit, combined with GRPO through a linearly annealed joint objective. Our method improves mathematical pass@4 over GRPO by up to 20.00 percentage points. Ablations support complete filtering, while frozen-history injection diagnostics support fact utility. Together, these results support combining agent-level outcome guidance with communication-derived token-level credit. Code is available at https://github.com/Camel-Prince/OPSD4MAS.


### arXiv 2611.16183 - Data-Efficient LLM Safety Alignment Guided by Interpretable Features

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-12T02:15:45Z
- authors: Hongyu Li, Zhan Yu, Zhe Zhao, Kimmo Yan, Xiong Kuang, Renfen Hu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16183

Existing large language models typically rely on massive fine-tuning datasets for safety alignment, which results in higher costs for safety alignment and often leads to the problem of over-refusal. We propose Less is Safer (LIS), a compact yet highly effective dataset for safety alignment, constructed through interpretable feature-guided selection and adversarial synthesis. Specifically, we leverage Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) activations to efficiently select prompts that cover diverse and interpretable safety features. Then, we employ a multi-agent adversarial synthesis framework to generate harmless prompts that nevertheless elicit unnecessary refusals. LIS contains only 2.4K samples, yet outperforms much larger datasets, e.g., TRIDENT-Core (26K), TRIDENT-Edge (19K) and WildJailbreak (262K), in overall performance on four safety and five over-refusal benchmarks. Through ablation experiments, we demonstrate that neither safety data nor over-refusal data alone can achieve optimal results. We also used safety features from SAE, annotated by an LLM, for data filtering, but the results were inferior to those obtained using the pipeline proposed in this paper.


### arXiv 2611.16174 - JudgeMix: Judge-Guided Mixed-Rollout Data Synthesis for LLM Agents

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-12T02:03:23Z
- authors: Cheng-Yu Hsieh, Ting-Yao Hu, Sanjoy Chowdhury, Oncel Tuzel, Raviteja Vemulapalli
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.16174

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories is a standard paradigm for training LLM agents. However, it suffers from a well-known training-inference distribution mismatch, calling for target-aware data synthesis strategies that ground the training data in the target model's native state distribution. To bridge this gap, emerging approaches incorporate the expert either to guide mixed rollouts alongside the target model, or to provide dense step-wise supervision. Nonetheless, existing methods apply expert assistance blind to the target model's inherent behavior: they either disrupt the rollout using sub-optimal random intervention heuristics, or they enforce indiscriminate uniform supervision that unnecessarily overwrites the model's already-correct actions. We introduce JudgeMix, a judge-guided data synthesis framework that dynamically interleaves the target model and expert based on step-wise semantic evaluations. JudgeMix queries expert guidance precisely when the target model struggles, ensuring high trajectory success rates while keeping the state distribution aligned with the target model. It additionally uses the judge evaluation to reweight expert supervision on different steps, preserving model's inherent valid behavior while correcting its errors. On two agentic tool-use benchmarks, AppWorld and AutomationBench, JudgeMix demonstrates consistent superior performance to existing methods, with improvements up to 10 pass@1 points.


### arXiv 2611.15729 - DISCERN: Preserve at Write, Resolve at Read for Persistent LLM-Agent Memory

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-11T17:06:07Z
- authors: Chirag Ahuja, Vladislav Tanov, Ramcharan Kakarla, Jonathan Taws
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15729

Enterprise agents accumulate memory across dozens of sessions in which facts contradict, get superseded, and blur as similarly named entities collide in embedding space. The field treats this as a retrieval problem; we show it is not. On ENT-310, a synthetic enterprise sales-agent memory benchmark of 310 queries (210 answerable, 100 unanswerable), a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reader over a memory store already matches strong memory systems on the recall pillars. Retention and currency are near-saturated, roughly 0.93 to 0.98 for every competent system, so recall is essentially solved. What remains unsolved is resolution: which entity a query refers to, which time-state is current, and whether to commit an answer at all or decline when evidence is insufficient. We propose DISCERN (DISambiguation and Cross-session Episodic memoRy Navigation), an agent-memory framework built on a single rule: preserve at write, resolve at read. At write time DISCERN builds a strictly additive bi-temporal provenance graph that never merges confusable entities and never drops superseded values. At read time a zero-LLM tool surface drives an agentic loop that navigates this graph and commits exactly one of four gated terminal actions. A four-judge LLM-as-a-Judge council scores two axes: answer correctness on answerable questions, and the answer-versus-abstain decision as a classification problem. On ENT-310, DISCERN is the best-calibrated system on abstention (F1 0.924 versus at most 0.802 across eight baselines) and leads macro answer correctness under equal pillar weighting (0.861 versus 0.806 for the strongest baseline), a lead driven by entity resolution (0.800, where no baseline exceeds 0.40). Applied with zero tuning to the full LoCoMo corpus, the same pipeline again leads macro answer correctness (0.663 versus 0.638) and stays among the best-calibrated on false-premise abstention.


### arXiv 2611.15680 - SpaceMCP: Vision Language Model Spatial Reasoning via Model Context Protocol

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-11T16:21:39Z
- authors: Ameen Izhac, Nobuko Yoshida, Ronald Clark
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15680

Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. Recent progress in feed-forward 3D reconstruction has enabled precise, real-time geometric understanding from visual inputs. In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. Paired with the VLM's strong reasoning capabilities, the VLM is able to combine this primitive information to solve more complex spatial tasks. Without any training, SpaceMCP achieves state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench and VSTI-Bench, outperforming recent spatially post-trained VLMs and improving its base VLM by 15-22 points per benchmark. We argue that, given the contrast between the relatively slow progress in native VLM spatial reasoning and the rapid advances in feed-forward 3D computer vision, this paradigm offers a promising path forward for enabling robust spatial reasoning.


### arXiv 2611.15600 - MemCredit: From Relevance to Utility for Decision-Time Memory in Long-Horizon Web Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-11T15:17:38Z
- authors: Quanjiang Guo, Sijie Wang, Qianyang Zhu, Ben Zhang, Jinchuan Zhang, Ke Yan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15600

Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate over long Web interaction histories, making memory management critical for reliable and efficient decision making. Existing methods primarily optimize storage, retrieval, or compression, but the memories most semantically relevant to the current state are not necessarily those that most improve the agent's action. We introduce MemCredit, a decision-centric memory framework that shifts memory selection from semantic relevance to decision utility. MemCredit estimates the state-, reference-context-, and policy-conditional marginal contribution of individual memories through controlled counterfactual interventions, and distills these intervention-derived credits into a lightweight utility estimator. At inference time, semantic relevance is used only for high-recall candidate generation, while predicted utility arbitrates which memories are retained under a fixed token budget, without modifying the underlying agent policy or requiring additional environment interaction. We evaluate MemCredit on ALFWorld and WebShop against representative memory baselines. On ALFWorld, MemCredit achieves 86.57 SR@50 under a 500-token historical-memory budget, compared with 65.67% for BM25 retrieval. Counterfactual analysis further shows that memory utility is sparse, strongly state dependent, and poorly captured by semantic relevance. These results suggest that decision utility provides a more effective principle than relevance alone for long-horizon agent memory management.


### arXiv 2611.15568 - Can We Make an LLM Dumber, or Is It Just Playing Dumb? An Empirical Study of Believable Performance Throttling

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-11T14:53:50Z
- authors: Chengguang Gan, Yunhao Liang, Hanjun Wei, QingHao Zhang, Shiwen Ni
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15568

A benchmark score is commonly read as a proxy for model capability. We study the inverse problem: can a model be driven to a target score, and do its failures resemble those of a naturally weaker model? We operationalize believable performance reduction along three axes: target calibration, item-difficulty structure, and error-distribution similarity to a real weak reference. We introduce CapLadder, an IRT-calibrated suite of 1,985 items spanning mathematics, code, multiple-choice reasoning, and tool use. A simple weak-teacher LoRA dial reaches unseen target levels on reasoning with a 1.9-point held-out calibration error, whereas prompting, temperature, and weight noise cannot combine comparable range and precision. Yet on multiple choice, the dial converges to an answer-token attractor: up to 97% of its newly introduced errors land on one letter, with the attractor changing from D in Qwen to A in Llama. Logit interventions identify a dominant near-constant shift: subtracting the estimated shift on held-out items recovers 52 to 59% of lost accuracy and removes the dominant attractor, while option permutation confirms that the bias tracks token position rather than option content. With high inter-annotator agreement ($κ=0.94$), human-coded error-category distributions nonetheless match the weak reference on reasoning (JS = 0.00), showing that local plausibility and aggregate detectability differ. Structured tool calls exhibit more natural degradation in the Qwen family (JS = 0.09), while a globally calibrated dial transfers poorly across benchmarks (14.2-point error on two usable benchmarks). Precise score control, therefore, does not imply a portable or believable reduction in skill.


### arXiv 2611.15426 - SimRec: Learning to Search, Ask, and Recommend from Simulated Interaction

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR
- published: 2026-11-11T12:53:52Z
- authors: Yaxin Zhang, Xingyu Wu, Yuchen Yan, Yuqin Dai, Hong Zhao, Lixing Shen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15426

LLM-based recommendation agents can search catalogs, inspect products, ask users, and revise rejected recommendations. However, these behaviors are often governed by hand-designed workflows, while existing training methods optimize isolated components such as query rewriting or list ranking rather than the interaction policy as a whole. We present SimRec, a reinforcement learning framework that trains the full interaction policy of a tool-integrated recommendation agent. SimRec introduces two components. A task-anchored user simulator provides feedback on every recommendation: conditioned on the current task need rather than on user profile, and it can be an external LLM or the recommender itself under self-play. A task-aligned reward optimizes the whole trajectory with GRPO, crediting natural retrieval quality and valid tool use alongside the final hit, with training-only target injection to handle sparse hits over large catalogs. On ESCI and Amazon C4, SimRec improves the NDCG@100 of a Qwen3-4B agent from 32.23% to 35.42% and from 22.94% to 24.72%}, outperforming training-free agents on far larger backbones and task-aligned baselines on NDCG. Analysis shows that the trained agent verifies candidates, uses preferences, and recovers from rejected recommendations far more often than its untrained counterpart. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/SimRec .


### arXiv 2611.15372 - LLMRunner: Closed Workload Model Performance Testing Framework for LLM Servings

- categories: cs.PF
- published: 2026-11-11T11:52:20Z
- authors: Arush Upadhyaya, Debadatta Mishra
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15372

Performance testing large language model (LLM) serving engines is a crucial requirement considering the criticality of the infrastructure for deploying conversational AI at scale. Most of the benchmarking tools used to carryout performance evaluation of LLM serving do not capture realistic workload orchestration unlike closed workload performance testing infrastructure used in other domains such as web and database engines. For example, state-of-the-art LLM benchmarks for performance testing model traffic as streams of independent single-turn and stand-alone requests omitting the end-user behavior such as think-time between conversational turns. We present LLMRunner, a performance tester for LLM serving that addresses this gap by providing a framework to generate realistic load consisting of multi-turn conversations with configurable end-user parameters such as number of users in the workload loop, think-time distributions etc. Moreover, LLMRunner provides declarative matrix-sweep experiment plans, integrated GPU and KV-cache monitoring, and an automated artifact collection and plotting subsystem. We use LLMRunner to comparatively analyze the performance of LLM serving when NVIDIA CUDA Unified Virtual Memory (UVM) is used as a drop-in replacement memory allocator for the Key-Value cache (KV-Cache). Our test demonstrates that UVM incurs no measurable throughput or latency penalty compared to the baseline memory allocation technique for non-oversubscription scenarios.


### arXiv 2611.15268 - Knowledge in the Harness, Reliability in the Weights: Teacher-Free Co-Evolution of an LLM Agent

- categories: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-11T09:56:09Z
- authors: Yujia Zhang, Xinqi Ren, Shixiong Zhou, Jia Meng
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15268

Small open models suit private customer-service deployments but trail frontier models in two ways: they miss what the written policy requires in a given situation, and they act inconsistently on what they know. We study whether such a model can close both gaps from its own deployment logs and their reference outcomes, without a stronger teacher model. Our starting point is a Gemma 4 12B agent that reads its domain policy from on-demand skills rather than from its system prompt. In each round, the deployed system serves a fresh batch of tasks; the base model diagnoses failed conversations and writes rules into the skills, and a rule survives only if it passes code checks, counterexample self-review, a paired retest, and a noise-calibrated validation gate. Reinforcement learning (GRPO) then trains a LoRA adapter on complete multi-turn, tool-using conversations. On the retail and airline domains of $τ^2$-bench, without training or selecting on official tasks, the agent after two rounds improves on this starting point by 12.5 pass$^1$ points in retail and 19.5 in airline, and by 18.4 and 22.0 pass$^4$ points. It also exceeds the benchmark's reference agent, which runs the same model with the whole policy in its system prompt, by 11.8 and 8.0 pass$^1$ points and by 22.8 and 16.0 pass$^4$ points, closing most of the gap to three frontier models under the same protocol. A $2\times2$ ablation shows that the rules rescue tasks the agent never solved and carry policy knowledge that RL with the rules in context does not internalize, whereas the trained weights make solved tasks reliable.


### arXiv 2611.15012 - TDMOpt: LLM-Driven Cross-layer Dispatch for Transmission-Distribution-Microgrid Systems

- categories: eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-11T04:09:10Z
- authors: Jiayang Tu, Bojun Du, Jijun Shui, Ershun Du, Qin Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.15012

Coordinating transmission networks, distribution networks, and microgrids enables distributed flexibility to support renewable energy integration and low-carbon operation. However, heterogeneous resources, cross-layer coupling, and varying operating requirements complicate the selection of models and coordination schemes that are both physically adequate and computationally tractable. This paper proposes TDMOpt, a LLM-driven multi-agent framework that replaces fixed modeling configurations with adaptive model composition. A layered component library provides executable network, resource, objective, constraint, and coordination models. Guided by retrieved physics and component knowledge, the agents infer specific modeling requirements and jointly select compatible components across layers according to operating conditions and computational budgets. The model compositions undergo independent AC power flow validation, since feasibility in a simplified formulation does not ensure compliance with physical network limits. A refinement mechanism interprets numerical feedback to identify modeling requirements for reconsideration and revise relevant component selections. Case studies on different scale systems illustrate the framework's ability to adapt model combinations and obtain validated dispatch solutions through targeted refinement under varying operating conditions.


### arXiv 2611.14980 - CoLFA: Collaboration-Guided Lightweight Failure Attribution for LLM-Based Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-11T03:38:54Z
- authors: Yufei Luo, Yuchen Ling, Chunrong Fang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14980

Failure attribution in multi-agent systems requires tracing error formation to identify the decisive error step and the responsible agent. However, agents' division of work and information exchange lead to Interleaved Steps and Cluttered Content, requiring models to link executions and check details in lengthy records. This makes it difficult to balance localization accuracy and token cost. We find that multi-agent collaboration also leaves records of how executions connect and content passes between them as results are formed, providing evidence for tracing error formation. Based on this observation, we propose CoLFA (Collaboration-Guided Lightweight Failure Attribution), which uses collaboration cues to organize the attribution input according to how results are formed, determining which steps are examined and how their content is presented. Through Handoff-Guided Step Expansion and Cross-Referenced Content Organization, CoLFA helps the model focus on examining error formation and the role of each decision while reducing the processing of irrelevant and repeated records. Across five settings spanning Who&When and LongRCA and three backbone models, CoLFA achieves the highest exact step-localization accuracy with the lowest token consumption. Compared with individual baselines in each setting, CoLFA improves accuracy by up to 37.50% and reduces token consumption by up to 97.6%.


### arXiv 2611.14945 - Auriga: Harness-Governed LLM Agents for Effective Throughput Optimization in Execute-Order-Validate Permissioned Blockchains

- categories: cs.DB
- published: 2026-11-11T02:49:08Z
- authors: Xiao Zhang, Tianyang Wang, Fangtao Gu, Haoxiang Sun, Puwei Wang, Jinchuan Chen
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14945

Effective throughput, the product of raw throughput and transaction success rate, determines the useful capacity of Execute-Order-Validate blockchains such as Hyperledger Fabric. Most prior work either tunes configurations to raise raw throughput, requiring many costly redeployments, or controls conflicts to raise the success rate, repairing them only after execution with kernel modifications. The few joint approaches tune only a small set of parameters and still rely on kernel changes to resolve conflicts. To address these challenges, we present Auriga, a non-invasive framework that jointly optimizes both factors through sample-efficient LLM-guided configuration search and pre-execution conflict prevention. A harness-governed multi-agent architecture coordinates LLM agents for parameter exploration, saturation-aware evaluation, and experience consolidation, with the harness controlling tool and memory access and retaining termination authority. For transaction scheduling, an LLM extracts symbolic AST templates from smart contracts offline. At runtime, the scheduler predicts read-write sets without executing them and partitions transactions into conflict-free groups. Across three benchmarks and conflict rates up to 70%, Auriga improves effective throughput by up to 6.0 times over default Fabric and 2.0 times over the strongest baseline. The tuning agent also discovers undocumented parameter dependencies, and ablation studies show the independent contributions of both subsystems.


### arXiv 2611.14927 - Inverse-Optimal Fixed-Time Integral Reinforcement Learning for Unknown Nonlinear Leader-Follower Multi-Agent Systems

- categories: eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-11T02:32:25Z
- authors: Tien Dat Vu, Minh Doan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14927

This paper develops a distributed inverse-optimal fixed-time integral reinforcement learning framework for unknown nonlinear leader-follower multi-agent systems. The proposed method first reconstructs the unknown agent dynamics from finite integral data. Based on the identified dynamics, a distributed inverse-optimal formulation is constructed by prescribing a desired fixed-time value function and recovering a compatible running cost. A critic-only integral reinforcement learning architecture is then developed using current integral data and finitely informative experience-replay data, thereby avoiding the persistent-excitation requirement. Practical fixed-time convergence of the critic-weight errors is established in the presence of identification and function-approximation residuals. Finally, a network-level composite Lyapunov analysis proves practical fixed-time leader-following consensus of the true unknown nonlinear multi-agent system. Numerical simulations are provided to validate the developed theory.


### arXiv 2611.14925 - ToolTurns: Benchmarking LLM Agents in Multi-Turn Dialogue

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-11T02:27:30Z
- authors: Milad Gholami, Brendan King, Jeffrey Flanigan
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14925
