(1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.


### arXiv 2611.12068 - MIRIX-2.0: Multi-Agent Memory System

- categories: cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-09T08:43:21Z
- authors: Wei-Chieh Huang, Weizhi Zhang, Jiayan Nan, Peng Xia, Yu Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12068

Long-term memory lets LLM agents personalize, stay consistent, and reuse what worked before. Parametric memories need training, consolidation can lose details for good, and keeping every record leaves a growing store to search. We present MIRIX-2.0, a multi-agent memory system designed to be practical, accurate, and efficient at once. It is the most accurate of the compared systems on four of five QA benchmarks. It reaches 91.4% on LoCoMo and 72.6% on LongMemEval-S, where it leads the strongest baseline by 14.3 points, and with AutoDream disabled it gains 31.0 and 22.0 points over HyperMem on single-hop and multi-hop document QA. As procedural memory, it yields the highest mean success among the compared memory methods on ALFWorld and WebShop and reaches 49.2% on MetaClaw-Bench, against 44.0% for MetaClaw's skill library. Six typed memory agents keep complete records as the system of record, and a provenance hypergraph indexes episodic and semantic records with n-ary frames that cite their rows. Retrieval runs under explicit budgets, so the evidence a question inspects does not grow with the history. AutoDream merges duplicate anchors outside the write path without rewriting any record, and it distills task sessions into skills. With AutoDream disabled, MIRIX-2.0 answers faster than HyperMem on all five timed benchmarks. It needs no fine-tuning, runs with closed and open models, and its code is public.


### arXiv 2611.12066 - Weight-Space Programming for Medical Imaging: Do We Still Need Training Data?

- categories: cs.CV
- published: 2026-11-09T08:41:33Z
- authors: Maximilian Lindholz, Richard Ruppel, Annette Milnik, Sebastian Halskov, Semil Eminovic, Charlie A. Hamm
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12066

Can a large language model (LLM) write the parameters of a medical image classifier from medical knowledge alone, without any training data? Such classifiers normally require labelled images, which are costly and often unavailable. We investigate this question through weight-space programming (WSP), in which an LLM writes or adapts the weights and biases of a neural network directly, without optimisation on training data. Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra each generate the parameters of a small LeNet-style CNN in a single reply, without tool use and without access to any image. We compare these networks against zero-shot MedSigLIP and against the same architecture trained on labels using the Adam optimiser, across 7 classification tasks in four modalities: chest X-ray, breast ultrasound and abdominal CT (all from MedMNIST), and pelvic MRI (SynthUterus). The LLM-written networks perform above chance on all six MedMNIST tasks, with a mean test AUC of 0.719 for Opus and 0.711 for Astra. Both models outperform MedSigLIP on 5 of 6 tasks while using more than 10,000 times fewer parameters. However, the same network trained with Adam on only 256 labelled images achieves a higher AUC on every MedMNIST task (mean AUC 0.828). On SynthUterus, Opus attains an AUC of 0.764, whereas Astra falls below chance (AUC 0.136) owing to a systematic error. These results show that LLMs can write functional medical classifiers, but that they do not yet replace training data. We release the protocol, logs and all generated weights at https://github.com/MaximilianLindholz/weight-space-programming-medical.


### arXiv 2611.12014 - Benchmarking language models and agents for scientific reasoning in evolving materials laboratories

- categories: cs.AI, cond-mat.mtrl-sci, cs.CE, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-09T07:51:52Z
- authors: Xuyu Dong, Yixuan Li, Jianguo Huang, Feng Chen, Yiyun Zhou, Bo An
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12014

Large language models and the multi-agent systems built around them could accelerate materials discovery by connecting scientific knowledge with experimental decisions. Existing benchmarks establish capabilities in domain knowledge, multimodal interpretation and scientific tool use. As language models increasingly support materials research workflows, their ability to distinguish closely related material states and reason accurately with quantitative evidence remains insufficiently evaluated. These abilities are essential for comparing material responses, assessing mechanisms and maintaining reliable judgements as experimental evidence accumulates. We introduce MatScope to evaluate three connected capabilities. M1 tests unaided, condition-specific reasoning through 200 tasks spanning ten research functions, using aluminium and high-entropy alloys as representative material systems. M2 tests agent-assisted transfer of related knowledge to unresolved materials questions. MatEvoBench-20 tests initial forecasts across scientific dependency chains and revisions of still-hidden responses as observations accumulate. Across eleven model deployments, including GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1, given-input calculations and many physical explanations are handled well. Fine-grained response estimates, comparisons between similar states and quantitatively supported choices remain substantially weaker. Related literature improves selected estimates, while condition and baseline mismatches limit transfer. Progressive observations calibrate some downstream responses and destabilize earlier accurate predictions. MatScope establishes measurable targets for developing reliable scientific reasoning in future self-evolving materials laboratories and materials-aware world models.


### arXiv 2611.11960 - Operation-aware Multimodal Clinical Decision Support System for ICU Discharge Risk Assessment

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-09T06:28:58Z
- authors: Guoqing Zhang, Rafik Hadfi, Takayuki Ito
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11960

Unplanned 30-day intensive care unit (ICU) readmission is a critical benchmark for evaluating healthcare quality. Most predictive models assume readmission risk depends solely on a patient's individual pathophysiological state, ignoring hospital operational context and resource capacity. In practice, discharge decisions are dynamic trade-offs between patient stability and bed turnover pressures. To address this gap, we develop an Operation-aware Multimodal Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for ICU discharge risk assessment. The system combines multi-agent collaborative extraction with self-evolving retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to eliminate template noise from progress notes, while constructing departmental workload indicators alongside admission flow features, continuous vital signs, and sparse medical codes (ICD/DRG). Addressing severe class imbalance (about 10.5% readmissions) and alarm fatigue, we implement data normalization, class reweighting, and decision-threshold calibration. In 20 independent runs across 20,000 ICU stays from MIMIC-III, empirical findings demonstrate that: vital signs alone have limited predictive capacity under real-world class imbalance (PR-AUC of 0.1387 +/- 0.0086); directly concatenating raw text embeddings degrades decision tree performance (XGBoost and LightGBM PR-AUC drop to 0.3893 and 0.4030); whereas an end-to-end multimodal fine-tuning architecture (Transformer E2E FineTune) utilizing layer-wise parameter freezing and differential learning rates reverses text negative transfer, achieving the highest AUROC of 0.8288 +/- 0.0110, PR-AUC of 0.4373 +/- 0.0310, and F1-Score of 0.4757 +/- 0.0196. Operational indicators supply independent risk signals, laying an engineering foundation for high-utility clinical decision support tools.


### arXiv 2611.13020 - ChipOmni: A General-Purpose AI Agent for Electronic Design Automation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-09T06:28:34Z
- authors: Pengkun Wang, Rui Mao, Zhe Zhao, Dongting Xie, Haibin Wen, Song Xu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13020

Electronic design automation (EDA) workflows remain highly fragmented, requiring engineers to coordinate heterogeneous representations, specialised software tools, and iterative verification across multiple design stages. Although large language models have brought more general reasoning and code-generation capabilities to EDA, many existing approaches remain focused on isolated tasks and struggle to plan, verify, and repair designs autonomously within executable engineering environments. Here we introduce ChipOmni, a knowledge-enhanced, context-evolving AI agent framework for EDA. ChipOmni integrates multimodal understanding, dynamic planning, multi-agent collaboration, and verification-driven tool interaction into a unified autonomous system. By continually incorporating feedback from external engineering tools, the framework maintains an evolving task context, integrates domain knowledge, and iteratively refines intermediate designs. Across benchmarks for analog circuit, RTL, testbench, and formal-assertion generation, ChipOmni improves multiple task-specific measures over the evaluated baselines and solves all 30 AnalogCoder-Pro tasks. In a long-horizon case study, ChipOmni advances a natural-language RISC-V specification through three design levels to a micro-SoC GDSII layout that passes physical sign-off, without human intervention after initialization. Together, these results show that ChipOmni can coordinate verification-driven design across heterogeneous tasks and sustain long-horizon engineering workflows, advancing a shift from fragmented, tool-centred processes towards agent-centred, closed-loop systems.


### arXiv 2611.11941 - Flip Anything with Agentic Robotics Self-Learning

- categories: cs.RO
- published: 2026-11-09T06:09:55Z
- authors: Shuangyu Xie, Kaiyuan Chen, Ken Goldberg
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11941

Flipping is a fundamental robotic manipulation skill that requires reorienting a target object in a stable pose on a planar support surface by rotating it 180$^\circ$ about an axis parallel to the support surface -- think of flipping a coin from heads to tails. Although flipping is a common objective in industrial tasks such as parts inspection, mechanical assembly, and tool use, coding a bimanual robot with grippers to flip diverse objects is a significant challenge. In this paper, we show that a coding agent can autonomously develop and refine object-flipping strategies through iterative learning in physics simulations across diverse object geometries. Progressive skill acquisition, from basic handover to toppling and pushing, robust grasping and sensing, and thin-object manipulation with recovery, improves task success from 24.7% to 95.0%. The resulting graph-based code integrates high-level strategy selection with reactive control, enabling multi-stage flipping on physical robots.


### arXiv 2611.11840 - Correct Answers from Insufficient Histories: A Controlled Study of Agent Memory Evaluation

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-09T03:54:25Z
- authors: Quan Xue, Zerui Yang, Chaorui Zhang, Haolin Ruan, Xueyan Niu, Xuanyu Zheng
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11840

An answer can match the reference for a complete agent history even when the reader's visible history does not determine it. We study this distinction using executable device logs. Our protocol separates reference agreement, certified evidence sufficiency, and context-appropriate behavior. Insufficiency requires two legal histories yielding byte-identical visible contexts but different answers; public chronology and query cutoffs certify sufficient prefixes. On 80 confirmation histories, Qwen3-32B matches 87 hidden references among 203 insufficient raw-prefix instances, with only seven appropriate abstentions. A fixed-input DeepSeek-V4-Flash replication matches 85 and abstains on 21 of the same 203 instances. Each reader makes 1,600 requests. Compact CSV's estimated appropriateness effects remain inconclusive: +3.16 percentage points, clustered 95% interval [0.00,6.57], for Qwen and +2.46, [-1.84,6.94], for DeepSeek. A separate held-out judge study achieves 99.38% correctness but retains support errors and truncations; a deterministic baseline solves all constructed cases. These results support an evaluation diagnosis across two hosted reader configurations, not gains for a general memory system or natural-language evidence assessment.


### arXiv 2611.13008 - An Open Implementation Flow for the Xilinx Spartan-3E and Spartan-6 that Runs in the Browser, Checked Byte for Byte Against ISE

- categories: cs.AR, cs.AI, cs.SE, eess.SY
- published: 2026-11-09T01:25:22Z
- authors: Pedro Malo
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.13008

The author describes an open implementation flow, from HDL to a programmable bitstream, for the Xilinx Spartan-3E (XC3S100E to XC3S1200E) and Spartan-6 (XC6SLX16, XC6SLX45), devices that only the discontinued ISE 14.7 supports. The flow runs unchanged in a web browser or in Node.js: Yosys and nextpnr compiled to WebAssembly, with nextpnr's Himbaechel framework given Spartan-3E and Spartan-6 architectures generated from Silinx's own device descriptions, bit database and timing model, and Silinx's own bitstream writer. The bit database was built by black-box observation of ISE's documented XDL and bitgen tools, using codeword-batched experiments; the router is given only switches whose bits were measured, and the writer refuses a feature it has not measured. ISE is the oracle: for the same placed and routed design, the bitstream is byte-identical to the one bitgen writes and ISE's DRC is clean. Placement and routing were first done by an in-house annealer and PathFinder router, which nextpnr replaced once it matched them. On 67 open-source designs for the XC3S1200E, compared with ISE at the same optimisation goal (45 designs built by both), the flow uses 15% fewer slices and has 14% shorter critical paths at goal Area, 21% fewer slices and 8% shorter paths at Speed, and 20% fewer slices but 12% longer paths at Fast (geometric means). XST cannot read 15 of the designs. The flow is published as the npm package silinx-fpga (Apache-2.0).


### arXiv 2611.11696 - Direct GPU Energy Measurements of Batch-Level Sharing in Mixture-of-Experts and Recurrent Models

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-08T23:31:46Z
- authors: Rafael Pina, Varuna De Silva
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11696

Energy claims for efficient neural networks are often inferred from operation counts. We report direct GPU energy measurements on two workloads that batch very differently, recurrent policies in GPU-vectorised multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) and mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models, and ask one question of both: can the rows of a batch be made to share computation, across the batch and over time, at a measured saving? On recurrent policies the answer so far is no. Shared activity patterns exist but rarely last one step, masking a frozen policy fails, and trained-in masks succeed only because a fixed mask is equivalent to a narrow GRU, at 11x the energy of a small dense policy in our implementation. Test-time steering adds nothing beyond re-picking on either workload. On MoE models the answer is yes, and granularity decides how much: on Qwen3-30B-A3B, batch-aware routing reads 25-31% fewer expert weights within KL 0.05, with benchmark changes within noise at 500 items, and uses 12-18% less energy per token on a fused forward. We also introduce persistence-aware OEA, a one-parameter rule that keeps the batch-aware cut with about 40% fewer new experts per step than native routing at batch 32-64, though its energy has not yet been measured. These are pilot measurements on one GPU type, and we state their limits.


### arXiv 2611.11669 - Toward Self-Evolving Geospatial Algorithms: An LLM-Driven Framework with a Case Study on Spatial Regime Discovery

- categories: physics.soc-ph
- published: 2026-11-08T22:49:39Z
- authors: Xiayin Lou, Peng Luo, Liqiu Meng
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11669

Geospatial algorithms have been developed through expert-driven discovery: a researcher spots a limitation, proposes a structural fix, and checks it by hand. With LLM-driven multi-agent systems becoming practical, much of the candidate-generation, evaluation, and selection work can now be largely automated, while the researcher's role shifts upstream toward problem formulation, evaluation design, and interpretation. We refer to this paradigm as Self-Evolving Geospatial Algorithms (SEGA) and propose a framework with three required modules: a two-loop architecture with embedded geographic knowledge as the inner-loop engine; an automatic code formatter that converts any raw geospatial algorithm into the standardized triple required by the inner loop; and a simulation-driven training protocol, in which evolution runs only on synthetic data with known ground truth, and the discovered algorithm is applied unchanged to real data, keeping real-world results out-of-sample. We use spatial regime discovery as a case study, seeded with the Two-stage K-Models algorithm. Evolution uses the Rand Index against the ground-truth partition as fitness signal; the frozen algorithm is then evaluated on 150 simulated grids and 11 real-world datasets across climate, health, hydrology, and politics. The evolved algorithm outperforms its seed, knowledge-free baselines, and four classical regionalization methods. Ablations further indicate that the channel of geographic-knowledge injection, structured retrieval versus prompt-level hints, is itself load-bearing. In this case study, LLM-assisted search sped up the inner loop; whether it does so for other seeds and task families remains open. The geographer's role -- asking the right questions, designing trustworthy evaluation -- grows more central.


### arXiv 2611.11657 - Inside the Trading Loop: Markets, Models, and Moves with LLM Agents

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-08T22:27:56Z
- authors: Renee Jia, Di Mu, Yao Yu, Tianrui Liu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11657

We built a hybrid multi-agent trading system that combines large language model (LLM) analysts with momentum-based portfolio construction and rule-based execution. Company-news and macroeconomic agents return structured reports to a coordinator that sets stock weights and cash reserves. We evaluate the portfolio engine using its broker-recorded paper account, which operated with deterministic fallbacks, and compare LLM-enabled configurations through retrospective replays. From March 11 to August 28, 2026, the account returned 34.5%, compared with 14.4% for the S&P 500 fund SPY over the same dates. This raw return advantage came with a larger maximum drawdown. Reconstructed policies also finish above matched SPY and QQQ references when assessments are used in the same session or deferred to the next. Design comparisons examine cash authority, selective invocation, and response contracts. A constant-cash control gives a return estimate close to stance-based sizing, and shorter report contracts improve observed parsing but can change recommendations.


### arXiv 2611.11581 - Same Error, Different Recovery: How Delivery Changes LLM Agent Behavior

- categories: cs.AI, cs.AR
- published: 2026-11-08T19:52:13Z
- authors: Sunghyeun Woo, Javier Conde, Pedro Reviriego
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11581

A tool-using language model must decide whether to retry a failed call, correct it, or stop and tell the user. The error text alone may not be enough: the same feedback can lead to different actions depending on where it appears in the conversation. We isolate this delivery problem by varying placement, guidance, retained history and turn structure. Two studies with plans fixed before data collection, across 13 open models from 3B to 70B parameters, compare the next action and subsequent task completion, with exact deterministic reruns. A new user turn that retains the failed exchange avoids the severe stopping failure observed in Llama models when that exchange is removed. It is non-inferior to history rewriting in pooled synthetic comparisons, but is not best for every model. We implement this form as an opt-in recovery layer within a deterministic inference engine and evaluate it on public benchmarks and a coding agent. On ToolMaze, it raises success on explicit errors by 14.2 points with 7-8B models and 10.5 points with 32-70B models; Loud/Quiet, 24 multi-turn tasks with visible failures and silent corruption, scored by final-state agreement, shows no measurable gain. A client given the exact fault kind performs better still. The findings support delivery as a distinct factor in recovery, not an engine-exclusive solution or a universal retry policy.


### arXiv 2611.11571 - Treating the Model as an Untrusted Component: Deterministic Policy Enforcement, Human Approval and Taint Tracking Against Prompt Injection in Tool-Using Agents, with Validation on Open-Weights Models

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-08T19:33:25Z
- authors: Mustafa S. Aljumaily, Nawar S. Alseelawi, Raghad Alshabandar
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11571

Tool-using LLM agents act on instructions embedded in the data they read, so prompt injection is a structural rather than a prompt-wording problem. We compare in-prompt defences (hardened prompts, spotlighting, LLM detectors) with deterministic enforcement outside the model (four reference monitors of increasing strength), human approval and taint tracking, judged by one effect-based oracle independent of every defence in an executable environment of 13 tasks and 12 tools, with argument-fuzzing attackers and severity-weighted harm. In an evaluation with real open-weights models (Qwen2.5-1.5B and Qwen2.5-7B; 156 attack episodes per defence and model), the models proposed the attacker's call in 64% and 84% of attack episodes behind the task-bound monitor, which admitted 1.3% and 7.7%; guardrail effects depended on the model (a prompt guardrail moved obedience from 67% to 65% and from 86% to 55%, spotlighting plus a detector to 12% and 0%) against static attacks. Simulation with an assumed model covers what no real run did: under the assumed erosion law a persistent adaptive attacker or a compromised model drives every guardrail stack to 100.0% attack success, whereas the task-bound monitor holds severe actions at 0.0%; its residual (harm 0.69/10) falls by 62% under taint-triggered human review, and a human layer without a deterministic monitor fails against approval spamming unless each episode has a deterministic approval budget. Implementation quality matters: a naive monitor admits 18 of 33 effective attack variants, a hardened one 6 and a capability-bound one 1. The real-model evidence is limited to two open-weights models and static attacks, the human-approval and taint results are simulation-only, and the larger model's detector stack contradicts the assumed erosion law. Code, data, a two-rater oracle audit and a notebook for repeating the real-model evaluation are provided.


### arXiv 2611.11541 - Safeguarding Agentic AI in Radiology Workflows: A Multimodel Evaluation of Rule-based and Large Language Model-based Verification

- categories: cs.AI, cs.CL, cs.CY
- published: 2026-11-08T18:29:04Z
- authors: Semil Eminovic, Robin Schmidt, Bogdan Levita, Maximilian Lindholz, Tobias Penzkofer, Jawed Nawabi
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11541

Purpose: To estimate how often tool-using large language model (LLM) agents record clinically relevant false or unsupported information in simulated radiology documentation and how much safeguards applied before each action reduce these errors. Materials and Methods: In this simulation study (October 2026), 48 synthetic, radiologist-reviewed base scenarios covered finding communication, tumor board preparation and follow-up management. Forty-three also had a misleading-information variant (91 variants). Six LLM agents (Claude Sonnet 5.5, GPT-6.1-Sol, GLM-5.3, MiniMax M3, Gemma-4-31B, Qwen3.8-27B) completed each variant with permissions only, deterministic rules, and rules plus a locally run LLM checker (1638 episodes). The primary outcome was at least one executed high-severity violation per episode. Risk differences were estimated per agent at base-scenario level and, exploratorily, pooled across agents, with analyses of document access and blocks. Results: With permissions only, 7.3%-27.1% of episodes had a high-severity violation (pooled across agents, 19.4%). Pooled, it was 16.7% with rules (-2.8 percentage points; 95% CI: -6.1, 0.5) and 13.2% with rules plus the LLM checker (-6.2; 95% CI: -9.5, -3.0). Adding the LLM checker to rules changed it by -3.5 (95% CI: -6.3, -0.7). No difference between agents was detected (P = .43), and task success did not decrease detectably. In misleading variants, agents opened the altered document in 83% of episodes regardless of error, and 58% of erroneous statements cited it. Most blocked actions (64%-68%) would not have caused a violation and were returned for revision. Conclusion: Layered safeguards reduced but did not prevent high-severity errors. Errors after misleading information reflected misjudged rather than missed information.


### arXiv 2611.11539 - PRISM: Causal Counterfactual Minimization of Agent Tool Privileges via Information Bottleneck Role Induction

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-08T18:24:07Z
- authors: Yang Luo, Qingni Shen, Tairui Wang, Jiajun Li, Zhonghai Wu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11539

This paper shifts the object of role engineering from human users to large language model (LLM) agents, and proposes PRISM to address the over-authorization that arises when an agent obtains full tool privileges through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). PRISM formulates whether a tool is necessary for a task as an intervenable causal proposition, and applies structured interventions in a sandbox to learn a counterfactual necessity model. It then compresses trajectories into discrete latent variables through an information bottleneck, and determines the number of roles from the knee of the rate-distortion curve. Finally, it formalizes pruning as a bilevel Stackelberg game, in which a generative flow network samples diverse near-optimal solutions. On three public benchmarks, AgentDojo, $τ$-bench and ToolEmu, PRISM compresses the number of authorized tools per role by 78.3%, attains a task success rate of 78.2%, reaches a counterfactual necessity accuracy of 88.7%, and lowers the blast radius to 15.9%.


### arXiv 2611.11342 - Agent Self-Improvement with Frozen Weights: The Added Value and Limits of Failure Traces

- categories: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-08T12:39:45Z
- authors: Arkid Mitra, Snehil Saluja
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11342

We evaluate whether an AI agent's harness, the notes around its model, can learn from the agent's failure traces beyond a matched search that never sees them. It can, but less than it looks. We tested an open 9-billion-parameter model with fixed weights; a larger teacher model wrote its notes. On generated customer-service decisions, where both note writers were told the target rules, notes based on the agent's mistakes improved accuracy by 4.0 percentage points over notes from the same process with the mistakes withheld. That process alone, trying and keeping better-scoring notes, reached about 60% of the total improvement. In full customer-service conversations the same notes showed no demonstrated improvement. Separately, a strict screen of single lessons admitted two customer-service lessons, still awaiting tests on new data, and one tool-use lesson, written without seeing the agent's mistakes, which showed no demonstrated gain on new tool-use tasks. A neutral note with no procedure scored higher than the learned lessons (exploratory). A blind audit judged 146 of 240 tool-use test items defective.


### arXiv 2611.11330 - When Credit Turns Against Tools: Transition-Aware Credit Assignment in Agentic Reinforcement Learning

- categories: cs.LG
- published: 2026-11-08T12:11:17Z
- authors: Xiangyu Lu, Zhanke Zhou, Jiazhe Ning, Chentao Cao, Jiangchao Yao, Bo Han
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11330

Agentic reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards trains language models through multi-turn tool interactions, yet their reasoning performance and tool-calling ability can decline during training. We trace this degradation to three coupled mechanisms: (1) tool-token imbalance produces negative overall tool credit when incorrect trajectories contain more tool tokens per turn than correct ones; (2) aggregation amplification can further strengthen this negative signal as token-mean aggregation gives longer incorrect trajectories greater weight in policy updates; and (3) failure-driven retries prolong trajectories as models tend to call tools again after execution errors, extending potential negative credit to recovery attempts. To counter this suppression, we propose Transition-Aware Credit Assignment (TACA), which targets the transition from reasoning to tool use through supervision of both call initiation and generation. Specifically, TACA uses Advantage Projection to enforce a nonnegative mean advantage on tool-call tokens within each prompt group through a minimal uniform shift. Additionally, TACA guides tool-use decisions with Decision Contrast, comparing rewards for tool-assisted and direct answers to the same prompt. Through Supervised Replay, it reinforces tool-call generation using calls from previously sampled correct trajectories. Across models and tasks, TACA improves performance and sustains more reliable tool interaction during training, raising mean accuracy over MT-GRPO on six reasoning benchmarks by 4.61 percentage points for Qwen2.5-1.5B and 3.61 for Qwen2.5-3B. Our code is available at https://github.com/tmlr-group/TACA.


### arXiv 2611.11319 - Verify Before You Fetch: Reliable KV Cache Compression for LLM Inference

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-08T11:43:34Z
- authors: Devanshu Jha, Gaurav Kumar, Agniva Chowdhury, Kumar Krishna Agrawal, Aditya Desai
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11319

Long-context applications, long-lived agents, and multi-agent systems require large language models (LLMs) to process and retain extensive information. In these settings, the KV cache can exceed GPU high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity, necessitating offloading to host memory, where CPU-to-GPU transfers can dominate inference latency. While KV cache compression can reduce data movement, query-agnostic compression that guarantees accurate attention for arbitrary queries is extremely challenging, if not impossible. We introduce VerKV, a hybrid approach that provides $(ε, δ)$ guarantees for approximating attention on any given query. VerKV maintains a compact set of query-agnostic pivotal tokens on the GPU, together with a token-level sketch of the full KV cache, while retaining the complete cache in CPU memory. We introduce a novel stochastic sampling procedure, PoissonComp, to select the pivotal tokens. At inference time, our proposed KVFetch algorithm uses the pivotal tokens and the sketch to estimate the relative error of the resulting attention computation. If the estimate satisfies a user-specified $(ε, δ)$ tolerance, inference proceeds without accessing host memory. Otherwise, VerKV performs query-aware retrieval to fetch additional tokens from the CPU, refining the attention computation to meet the required tolerance. Thus, VerKV enables attention with bounded approximation error while minimizing CPU-to-GPU transfers. We show that PoissonComp outperforms state-of-the-art approaches to KV cache compression. Combined with KVFetch, it achieves near-dense performance (90-100% of dense quality at 32K context length, averaged over RULER and LOFT), using Qwen3-4B, Qwen3.5-9B and Ministral-3-8B, while reducing the GPU memory footprint by 8-32x and keeping CPU-to-GPU memory fetches within 2-23% of the cache per generated token, depending on the model.


### arXiv 2611.11216 - Replay Fidelity in LLM Agent Evaluation: Randomness, Hidden State, and Test Oracles in AppWorld

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-08T08:07:06Z
- authors: You Liu, Quanchao Lu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11216

Reproducibility in large language model (LLM) agent evaluation depends on replay fidelity: which parts of an execution trace are reconstructed, controlled, and compared. This matters for agent debugging, failure analysis, and counterfactual evaluation of stateful tool use. We audit all 71 unsuccessful executions in a fixed historical AppWorld corpus using 284 isolated fixed-code replay attempts. Reconstructed declared settings admit 42 cases under printed-output, pre-oracle database-delta, and native-verdict/test-count checks; resetting the random-number generator before each interaction admits 68. The 26-case gap contracts to four under database/oracle checks, with one endpoint unknown. Mechanism studies identify four materialized-state discrepancies despite matching outputs and verdicts. In one admitted endpoint pair, an identical API call yields matching responses but different row effects; common future Python RNG removes that deterministic difference in two paired repetitions. A separate JWT credential-equality program and alignment controls expose authentication/write differences, with missing repetitions retained. For all 10 affected post-oracle export cases, native export from retained output trackers restores the reference comparison without changing original cached contents. A purposeful 12-case BFCL pilot shows no discrepancy under its tested public-state, return, and verdict checks. We preserve resource censoring and infrastructure deviations. The contribution is a bounded empirical characterization of replay eligibility, hidden state, and test-oracle integration, without estimating debugger accuracy, repair gains, or method-ranking distortion.


### arXiv 2611.11172 - Do Benign-Trained Hidden-State Probes Detect Tool Errors Under Adversarial Conditions?

- categories: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CL
- published: 2026-11-08T06:27:15Z
- authors: Bibek Bhandari, Kshitij Lingthep
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11172

Although hidden-state probes efficiently monitor model operation, it remains unclear whether probes trained on benign tool use remain useful for detecting incorrect actions under adversarial conditions. In this research, we assess this problem from the perspective of operational tool-action correct- ness. we use four AgentDojo suites, four conditions, and three models: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, and GLM- 4.6V-Flash (9B). We perform a five-fold, task-grouped out-of-fold (OOF) evaluation for each model. We train step-specific logistic- regression probes only on benign and non-defended trajectories and evaluate them on held-out trajectories for benign, prompt- injection, system-override, and tool-poisoning conditions. We compare our hidden-state probe to mean tool-call-token entropy, negative mean tool-call-token log-probability, and self-consistency uncertainty. The paired tests highlight six significant differences after Holm correction. All of the tests involve Qwen2.5-14B- Instruct under the system-override condition, with all three scalar uncertainty metrics obtaining higher area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) and average precision (AP) than the hidden-state probe. These results suggest that benign-trained hidden-state probes should not be assumed to reliably detect operational tool-action errors under adversarial conditions.


### arXiv 2611.11104 - Tracing MCP Security Vulnerabilities: Constructing and Analyzing an MCP Security Vulnerability Dataset

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-08T03:36:38Z
- authors: Xu He, Sagar Patel, Shu Wang
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11104

As a core protocol for integrating large language models (LLMs) with external data sources and tools, the Model Context Protocol (MCP) has quickly become a key interface in emerging AI agent systems. As the MCP ecosystem expands, reported vulnerabilities have also increased, creating a growing software-security attack surface. However, MCP vulnerability information remains scattered, noisy, and difficult to study systematically. This paper addresses that gap by constructing a curated MCP vulnerability dataset from CVE disclosures. Starting from 119 MCP-related records retrieved through February 2026, we filter unrelated cases out and obtain a validated snapshot of 24 MCP vulnerabilities. Our filtering methodology explicitly addresses acronym ambiguity and achieves 96.7% inter-rater agreement on a sampled exclusion audit while retaining all known high-severity MCP cases identified in public advisories. Using this initial dataset snapshot, we find that 87.5% of validated vulnerabilities are Critical or High severity, and that injection and path traversal vulnerabilities account for 66.7% of cases. We further identify recurring issues involving insufficient input validation, inadequate access control, and protocol-level security gaps. Through six representative case studies, we illustrate common exploitation patterns and map them to practical defenses. We release the dataset, reproducibility tooling, and a sustainable maintenance workflow as a foundation for future MCP security measurement, benchmarking, and defense research.


### arXiv 2611.10958 - Hybrid Source Code Change Impact Analysis

- categories: cs.SE, cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-07T20:42:00Z
- authors: Katharina Stengg, Christian Macho, Martin Pinzger
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10958

Change impact analysis (CIA) helps developers identify code entities impacted by their initial changes. While several approaches for CIA were proposed, they still struggle with either low precision or low recall, thereby increasing manual effort for developers to verify the results. Furthermore, limitations in the used datasets and experimental setups can bias the results reported by many CIA approaches. In this paper, we first address the dataset limitations by manually labeling 1,931 changed code entities stemming from 263 commits of 22 open-source Java projects. Next, we introduce two variants of katIA, our code change impact analysis approach that combines static impact analysis with the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). The first variant uses a single prompt (katIA-SP) for refining an initial static impact set while the second variant uses three specialized agents (katIA-MA). The evaluation with our curated dataset shows that katIA-SP with Gemini 2.5 Flash achieved an F1-score of 22.4% when matching impacted entities by their fully qualified names (FQN) and 27.0% when matching them by their class and entity names (CE). The latter shows an improvement of 2.0 percentage points over the state-of-the-art. This score is further improved by katIA-MA which, using Gemini 2.5 Flash, yields an FQN-matching F1-score of 29.1% and a CE-matching F1-score of 33.3%. While these improvements come at the cost of increased median generation time from 0.570 s to 11.214 s and median token usage from 1,235 to 6,462 tokens, the improved performance justifies the additional cost. The comparison of three LLMs using katIA-SP finds that Gemini 2.5 Flash is the most consistent model. These results indicate that combining static impact analysis with LLM-based refinement, particularly with the multi-agent variant, can improve code change impact analysis.


### arXiv 2611.10947 - Validating AI-Discovered Cloud Threats on Live Infrastructure: A Multi-Model, Multi-Cloud Study

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-07T20:23:15Z
- authors: Nicholas Pecka, Lotfi Ben Othmane, Renee Bryce
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10947

Cloud infrastructure threat modeling lacks empirical validation of AI-discovered threats. We present a three-phase methodology of multi-agent threat discovery, automated exploit synthesis, and non-destructive live validation, and apply it to three commercial foundation models on two cloud architectures, validating each threat natively on the cloud (AWS, GCP, or Azure) it was discovered against. Of 233 AI-discovered threats, 103 were confirmed and 34 refuted (75.2\% precision, 95\% CI [67.3, 81.7]); 44 were conditional (the named mechanism refuted but an equivalent impact shown through another path) and 52 indeterminate. Multi-model discovery helped through breadth, not agreement: the three models together found 2.2$\times$ the confirmed threats of the best single model at comparable precision, while threats reported by several models were confirmed no more often than single-model ones. False positives were not architecture-specific: they asserted access or system state the deployment did not have. Threats coded in advance as observable configuration resolved cleanly more often than multi-step behavioral threats (72.5\% vs.\ 51.6\%), which were more often conditional, suggesting that models more often misidentify the exploitation mechanism than the underlying weakness. AI-assisted discovery is a practical input to security workflows when paired with empirical validation.


### arXiv 2611.10844 - What Broke & Why: Practical Lessons from Reinforcement Learning Post-Training

- categories: cs.AI
- published: 2026-11-07T16:43:27Z
- authors: Luv Verma
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10844

Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become an important way to improve how language models reason, solve problems, and use tools. But getting it to work in practice is not always straightforward. Training can fail because a model receives no useful reward, learns to exploit the reward system, stops exploring, or appears to improve when the evaluation itself is misleading. In this book, we give a practical introduction to reinforcement learning post-training for language models, focusing on what works, what fails, and why. We begin with the foundations of post-training, explain GRPO and the training loop step by step, and then follow five training programs covering math reasoning, search agents, training stability, mixture-of-experts models, and software engineering tasks. Each program shows the problems encountered, the changes made, and the lessons learned. The book also examines what reinforcement learning actually changes inside a model. The remaining chapters provide a practical reference for reward design, evaluation, training tool-using agents, debugging common failures, reproducibility, rollout engines, and hardware. Written for researchers and practitioners working with open models, the book aims to help readers understand how to train models, recognize when something goes wrong, and decide what to try next.


### arXiv 2611.10843 - Agents of Chaos: A Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) on Malicious Attacks on Agentic AI Systems

- categories: cs.CR
- published: 2026-11-07T16:42:07Z
- authors: Thanh M. Truong-Le, Amber Bennoui, Hoang-Ly Nguyen, Gia-Thang Ho, Thai-Minh Truong, Duc-Ly Vu
- query-set: MCP
- abs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10843

Agentic AI systems extend large language models with tools, memory, retrieval, browser or computer-use interfaces, code execution, external services, and multi-agent coordination. Although these capabilities enable agents to act beyond text generation and perform tasks, they also create attack paths through which adversaries can exploit untrusted content, deceptive metadata, poisoned state, compromised skills, mutable dependencies, or malicious code. Such attacks can manipulate planning and tool use, corrupt memory, abuse code-execution environments, and trigger harmful downstream effects. In this paper, we present a systematic review of malicious attacks targeting agentic AI systems. We screened 186 candidate sources and retained 98. Following independent coding and resolver adjudication, we identified 284 atomic attacks, 452 atomic safeguards, and 660 explicit attack--safeguard relationships. We organize attacks into eight causal-mechanism classes while retaining insertion points, activation conditions, abused capabilities, objectives, impacts, persistence, and evidence as orthogonal dimensions. Our analysis shows that tool, MCP, and action-layer attacks account for the largest share of atomic attacks, while the prompt-injection and action-hijacking class has the broadest paper coverage. We identify distinct class-specific activation patterns and separation between insertion and later activation. Safeguard coverage remains uneven: only 278 of 660 relationships are directly assessed, and strong coverage is uncommon. These findings support lifecycle-aware threat modeling, agent evaluation, tool governance, and defense-in-depth security for agentic AI systems.

### modelcontextprotocol-registry

- v1.8.9 @ 2026-11-10T11:08:07Z :: Optimize incremental-sync pagination by selecting indexed page keys before fetching payloads, while retaining prepared statement reuse. Includes staged production autoscaling configuration and deployment guardrails. See #1840 for validation and rollout details.

- v1.8.8 @ 2026-11-06T14:07:07Z :: This release uses the source commit validated in staging. Different server names may now share a remote URL; consumers should key listings by server name and version rather than assuming URL uniqueness. No database migration is required. ## What's Changed * Stage v1.8.7 in production with prepared reads disabled by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1769 * Enable prepared listing JSON after production backfill by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1770 * Prepare bounded app autoscaling with worker headroom by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1772 * Add sanitized apply diagnostics and a guarded retry path by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/registry/pull/1774 * Filter deployment diagnostics to actual error events by @rdimitrov in https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol

- v1.8.7 @ 2026-10-30T11:24:12Z :: Adds prepared listing JSON to avoid repeated decoding and rebuilding of server payloads on listing reads. Prepared reads are disabled by default and support bounded backfill and flag rollback. Listing rows release their database connection before JSON processing; pool defaults remain 60/10. Focused response/update/delete/backfill/rollback checks passed. Local encoding benchmarks over saved public records measured 63% less encoding time and 88% fewer allocations. These are encoding-cost results, not a peak-capacity guarantee. Broad staging replay remained inconclusive for capacity due to generator port exhaustion and losses.

- v1.8.6 @ 2026-10-27T14:42:37Z :: This release improves registry search performance under mixed search, pagination and publishing traffic. - Select search page keys through covering indexes before fetching complete records. - Plan searches for their actual parameter values to handle broad and rare terms reliably. - Optimize namespace suffix searches and require at least three Unicode characters for nonempty search terms (shorter searches return HTTP 422). - Include reproducible cyclic-load benchmarks and repeated staging validation against a public production-data snapshot. Deployment: prebuild and verify migrations 016/017 indexes concurrently on busy databases. The validated burst capacity uses a 3-CPU PostgreSQL quota on the existing nodes, with the application connection pool unchanged. See deploy/README.md and tools/readbench/STAGING_SEARCH_RESULTS.md for procedure, evidence and limitations.

Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@latest`. * **The fetch guard blocks cloud metadata addresses outside `169.254.0.0/16`**, including `100.100.100.200` and `192.0.0.192` and their decimal and IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms, so neither the `/api/fetch` proxy nor OAuth discovery can be pointed at them ([GHSA-fvg8-f358-w2c8](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/inspector/security/advisories/GHSA-fvg8-f358-w2c8)) * **The network tracker caps the body it records for a POST event-stream response**, so a server streaming an unbounded response can no longer exhaust the web backend's memory and take down every session ([GHSA-9w48

- 1.0.5 @ 2026-11-11T21:39:41Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.5 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **The proxy now refuses two more fixed cloud metadata addresses.** Its connection guard already blocked `169.254.0.0/16`. It now also blocks `100.100.100.200` (Alibaba Cloud ECS) and `192.0.0.192` (Oracle Cloud Classic), including their IPv4-mapped IPv6 forms. The surrounding `100.64.0.0/10` range stays reachable, so Tailscale and other CGNAT targets still work. - 2.14.0 @ 2026-11-04T23:55:33Z :: ## What's Changed ### Security fixes 2.14.0 fixes three vulnerabilities reported privately against v2. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@latest`. - 1.0.4 @ 2026-11-04T23:28:28Z :: ## What's Changed ### v1.x security fixes 1.0.4 is a security release for the deprecated v1 line. Upgrade with `npx @modelcontextprotocol/inspector@v1-latest`, or move to v2 (`@latest`). * **Server-supplied `pattern` regexes are no longer evaluated.** Patterns in a tool's output schema and in an elicitation's requested schema are stripped before validation, including patterns reachable through `$ref`, and the elicitation form no longer passes them to the browser's own field validation. The result says which checks were skipped. A schema the Inspector cannot transform safely, such as one that declares `$id` or anchors, is reported as not validated instead of being evaluated. Because patterns are no longer evaluated, a value that fails a server's `pattern` is now accepted, with that note. Every other schema constraint still applies. Thank you to those who tested the pre-release.**_ In this release we introduce several fixes and improvements on top of [v1.7.0](https://github.com/modelcontextprotocol/go-sdk/releases/tag/v1.7.0). It adds no new protocol revision: the supported set is unchanged, and `2026-07-28` remains the newest version the SDK negotiates. The bulk of the work is hardening the transports against resource exhaustion, closing session leaks, deadlocks and teardown hangs found by users running the new protocol at scale, and giving servers explicit control over which protocol versions they advertise. - v1.7.0 @ 2026-07-28T13:09:53Z :: This release brings full support for protocol version **`2026-07-28`**. The wire protocol is largely rewritten: a stateless model with per-request `_meta`, a new `server/discover` RPC replacing the `initialize` handshake, multi-round-trip requests (MRTR) replacing server-initiated calls, a unified `subscriptions/listen` stream replacing free-floating change notifications, standardised HTTP headers, and the formal deprecation of the roots, sampling, and logging features. The streamable HTTP transport accepts requests at protocol version `2026-07-28` only when `StreamableHTTPOptions.Stateless = true`. If you want to expose the new protocol over HTTP, set `Stateless = true`; if you want to keep stateful sessions, your clients will negotiate down to `2025-11-25`. Backward compatibility with `2025-11-25` and earlier is preserved on every endpoint. The SDK negotiates the highest mutually-suppo

- ai@7.0.173 @ 2026-11-13T22:34:02Z :: ### Patch Changes - 3c09e00: Prevent in-place schema validation or input refinement from changing approved tool arguments during approval replay. - f43ee39: fix(workflow): log the default step limit warning when `WorkflowAgent.generate()` stops at the default limit. - 7ce7ffc: fix(workflow): log provider warnings in `WorkflowAgent` `WorkflowAgent.generate()` and `WorkflowAgent.stream()` now log the warnings of every model call through the AI SDK warning logger, like `ToolLoopAgent`. The `AI_SDK_LOG_WARNINGS` global controls the output. - f6809e3: fix(workflow): align the `prepareStep` results of `WorkflowAgent` with `ToolLoopAgent`. Returned `instructions` (or the deprecated `system`) replace the instructions of the step and carry forward to later steps, returned `providerOptions` are merged with the agent options, and returned `headers` are merged over the headers of the call. - 2559173

- ai@7.0.172 @ 2026-11-13T05:57:52Z :: ### Patch Changes - 79d2004: Require an explicit `experimental_download` hook for remote file URLs in `experimental_extract` and `experimental_decide` instead of silently downloading through global fetch. Callers can use `createDownload({ fetch })` with a DNS-protected transport for untrusted URLs; inline file data continues to work without a hook. - Updated dependencies [6d50947] - @ai-sdk/provider-utils@5.0.81 - @ai-sdk/gateway@4.0.145

- ai@6.0.332 @ 2026-11-13T23:44:02Z :: ### Patch Changes - Updated dependencies [23ff635] - @ai-sdk/gateway@3.0.237

- ai@5.0.290 @ 2026-11-13T23:43:05Z :: ### Patch Changes - 4107bc6: Keep failed tool call error text consistent with the model's message history by default. Set `redactToolErrors: true` to replace it with the result of `onError`. - ec9c246: Prevent excessive memory and CPU usage when validating malformed UI messages. `validateUIMessages` and `safeValidateUIMessages` reported the issues of every part schema for every invalid part, so a small request with many malformed parts could exhaust memory. Each part is now validated against the schemas for its type only, validation stops at the first invalid message or part, and an invalid JSON value in provider metadata is reported as a single issue. - Updated dependencies [09657bb] - Updated dependencies [f046ed8] - @ai-sdk/gateway@2.0.181

## The Decoder - OpenAI reassigns robotics researchers to other teams as general-purpose models might make specialized robotics AI unnecessary (2026-11-14)

OpenAI reassigns robotics researchers to other teams as general-purpose models might make specialized robotics AI unnecessary Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI reassigns robotics researchers to other teams as general-purpose models might make specialized robotics AI unnecessary Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 14, 2026 GPT-Image-2 prompted by THE DECODER Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search OpenAI is apparently disbanding its robotics team again, but this time for different reasons than in 2020. Rather than abandoning robotics, the company wants to accelerate it by folding researchers into other teams. Robotics researchers are moving to the main research team under Chief Research Officer Mark Chen, while the applied robotics and hardware teams join Greg Brockman's Applied organization. OpenAI made the decision on such short notice that it had already booked a booth at the Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL) in Austin, which then sat empty, Fortune reports . OpenAI apparently believes that general-purpose models like GPT-6 Astra can control robots directly , reducing the need for specialized robotics models. Ad In an Nvidia study , an Astra-powered agent achieved a 74.8 percent success rate on simulated robotics tasks, compared to 51 percent for the best specialized models. The tests used a simulated DROID robot, not a physical one. Ad A new robotics benchmark called StationeryBench compared GPT-6 Astra with Ai2's MolmoAct2 on five tasks involving desk objects on dual-arm YAM robotic arms. Across 200 trials, Astra fully completed 7 out of 100 tasks, while MolmoAct2 didn't complete a single one. Astra's average progress score was 46, compared to 12 for MolmoAct2. In another test, Astra running on a humanoid robot navigated and cleaned up a kitchen. It remains unclear how much robotics data OpenAI and others incorporated during training. Other large general-purpose models from Anthropic and Google have also made recent progress on robotics tasks. Specialized robotics models may still have advantages in cost and reliability as well as speed and energy consumption , even if they don't generalize as well. Ad Yoav Artzi , an AI researcher at Cornell University and Google DeepMind, also sees Astra as a major leap in spatial understanding . On the still-unpublished REMAP benchmark, GPT-6 Astra reaches near-human accuracy, according to Artzi. He suspects that OpenAI may have trained the model on large amounts of 3D training data such as Blender scenes. OpenAI and robots, it's complicated OpenAI shut down its robotics division in 2020 because, according to co-founder Wojciech Zaremba, the company lacked the training data it needed and large language models were a faster path to AGI. The company rebuilt the team in 2024. CEO Sam Altman is reportedly targeting a first public robot demo for 2027 , and OpenAI wants to build its own everyday robots in the long term. But the company also faces heavy financial pressure to focus on its core AI business , where it has lost significant ground to Anthropic . ## The Decoder - Anthropic can already retrain Mythos with a fraction of the compute originally needed, says former researcher Coxon (2026-11-14)

Anthropic can already retrain Mythos with "a fraction of the compute originally needed," says former researcher Coxon Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI research Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Anthropic can already retrain Mythos with "a fraction of the compute originally needed," says former researcher Coxon Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 14, 2026 Nano Banana Pro prompted by THE DECODER Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search Jacob Coxon, former OpenAI and Anthropic researcher who brought AI fears into the mainstream, says a slide from Anthropic's IPO roadshow was a major reason he quit. Coxon says a slide Anthropic released for its IPO roadshow was a key reason he left the company after only about four months. The chart shows how much compute Anthropic needs over time to train its models, and the axis is compressed so that a 10x and a 100x increase barely look different. Yet the curve still climbs steeply, which Coxon says shows that algorithmic efficiency is improving not just quickly but at an accelerating rate. "It's an insane plot and I'm surprised they made it public," he writes. Ad Coxon argues that Anthropic's Mythos model, a major capability jump in the spring, was part of this accelerating trend. "Mythos was not a one-time deviation, but a point on a superexponential. They could already retrain Mythos with a fraction of the compute originally needed," he writes. Ad This slide from Anthropic's IPO roadshow shows the "Pre-training compute multiplier" for code over time. The y-axis is logarithmic. Former Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon says the steep climb from July 2024 to October 2026 points to superexponential algorithmic progress. Anthropic notes that the data points are based on internal estimates and are for illustration only. | Image: Anthropic He says algorithmic improvements are "on the verge of being automated," which is why he wants frontier model development paused next year so competitors can catch up. A pause, he writes, would "commoditize human-level intelligence. It would only harm OpenAI and Anthropic." Coxon previously trained AI models at OpenAI and, most recently, Anthropic. He drew wide attention in September with public warnings about the risks of advanced AI systems , echoed by other protagonists in the industry. One of their fears is that advanced, self-improving AI could get out of control. ## The Decoder - Runway open-source Praxis-1 turns video AI into a robot controller (2026-11-14)

Runway's open-source Praxis-1 turns video AI into a robot controller Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI research Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Runway's open-source Praxis-1 turns video AI into a robot controller Tomislav Bezmalinović Nov 14, 2026 Runway Key Points Runway has released its robot model Praxis-1 with open weights and source code under the Apache 2.0 license. Trained on videos and robot data, the model processes camera images, text instructions, and position data to calculate matching robot movements. Praxis-1 can be adapted to new robot systems through additional training, according to Runway. The model does occasionally confuse similar-looking objects, though, and it can't yet reliably plan complex action sequences on its own. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search AI company Runway has released its robot model Praxis-1 with open weights and source code. The model uses what it learned from video to control robots and shows promising results in the company's own tests. Runway has released Praxis-1, its AI model for controlling robots. The company first introduced Praxis-1 in late September but initially gave access only to select partners. Alongside the release, Runway published a detailed research report with new performance tests. Runway's model weights and source code are available under the Apache 2.0 license, which allows commercial use. Ad Praxis-1 takes in camera images, text instructions, and information about a robot's position. From those inputs, it predicts how the environment will change and calculates the right robot movements. As new camera frames come in, the model continuously adjusts its control. Ad Praxis-1 builds on video training to control robots Praxis-1 builds on a video model that learned how objects move and interact from ordinary footage. Runway then fine-tuned it with videos of human actions and robot data. This approach could reduce the need for expensive robot-specific datasets, which have traditionally been collected just to train these kinds of systems. Runway is best known for generative video models like Gen-4.5 . With Praxis-1, the company is bringing its video research into robotics. Runway calls Praxis-1 a World Action Model that predicts how a scene will change and derives matching robot movements from those predictions. Since September, robotics companies Standard Bots, Noble Machines, and Ultra have been testing Praxis-1 on different systems, including a humanoid robot, an industrial robot arm, and a dual-arm warehouse robot. Ad Praxis-1 leads among World Action Models Praxis-1 still needs some specialized robot data. To adapt it to a Franka robot arm, Runway used about 277 hours of recordings from the public DROID research dataset, according to its research report . Those recordings show robots grasping and moving objects, for example. For a dual-arm robot, the company used between 1,400 and 2,600 hours of training material. Runway had already used DROID data and data from other robot systems during earlier training stages. Whether the approach actually needs less robot data overall than comparable models without general video pretraining is still an open question. On Nvidia's RoboLab-120 leaderboard , Praxis-1 scores a 46.2 percent success rate. The benchmark tests how reliably robot models can sort, grasp, or stack objects across 120 tasks in simulation. FLUX 3 Action , also built as a World Action Model, scores 42.9 percent under the same test conditions. That puts Praxis-1 at the top of the World Action Model category. In the overall leaderboard, it ranks fifth behind two AI agents and two robot models with different architectures. Runway ran all the tests itself,

The largest deal involved talks with OpenAI over a $250 billion guarantee. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search A WSJ report suggests Jensen Huang was pushed out more over his business practices than over his affairs. Nvidia built a risky credit program under Huang, the Wall Street Journal reports. The company offered AI cloud providers and data center developers billions of dollars in financial guarantees so they could afford Nvidia chips. Huang's son-in-law Nico Caprez pushed the program and promised partners quick closings. In return, the cloud providers were supposed to give Nvidia a cut of their revenue. "A deer in the headlights" The largest deal involved talks with OpenAI over a $250 billion guarantee for a data center in Ohio. Nvidia shares fell 5% after the deal became public. Interim CEO Colette Kress, who had been CFO, then cut Nvidia's commitment to OpenAI in half . The scaled-back deal was signed in August. Ad According to the WSJ, Kress had already questioned internally how the guarantees were valued and what they would return and had tried to rein in Caprez. In an August meeting, Huang reportedly called her "a deer in the headlights" and dismissed her as a mere "reporting CFO" who didn't help him with strategy or decision-making. Circular AI deals by Nvidia and other cloud and chip companies have drawn criticism since at least last year for inflating a bubble . Ad Nvidia's stock valuation also reportedly frustrated Huang. The company's average monthly stock multiple fell from 23 to 16 between May and October, and Nvidia responded with the largest share buyback in its history. Huang apparently feared he had made a losing bet Major cloud providers were planning to adopt alternative chips and reduce their dependence on Nvidia. With Nvidia committed to buying large volumes of wafers and memory, Huang described the threat as an “avalanche” rolling toward the chipmaker. Ad At the GTC conference in Berlin in October, Huang used a story about a soup vendor in Japan to put pressure on half a dozen cloud CEOs. He said he had stood in a long line and eaten one of the best soups of his life. When he asked for seconds, the vendor said no because too many people were waiting and he was running out of soup. "He's a better businessperson than all of you," Huang told the CEOs, according to the WSJ. The reasoning behind the story was that the soup vendor didn't sell his whole inventory to one or two customers. He kept prices high and his customer base broad. Huang said the cloud providers weren't following that example and were selling too much compute to a handful of big customers instead of spreading it across many buyers. "You are not establishing the value of my products," Huang said. Ad Whether Huang left voluntarily because the situation was slipping away f


## The Decoder - OpenAI rebuilt AI shopping from scratch with ChatGPT Wallet (2026-11-13)

OpenAI rebuilt AI shopping from scratch with ChatGPT Wallet Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab AI in practice Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section OpenAI rebuilt AI shopping from scratch with ChatGPT Wallet Maximilian Schreiner View the LinkedIn Profile of Maximilian Schreiner Nov 13, 2026 OpenAI Key Points OpenAI's new ChatGPT Wallet lets AI agents make and pay for online purchases on behalf of users, launching for Pro subscribers in the US. Every payment requires authorization through Face ID or a fingerprint, and actual card details never reach merchants thanks to tokens generated by partners such as Visa and Mastercard. Competitors including Anthropic, Meta, and Google already offer similar agent shopping features, putting OpenAI in catch-up mode. Ask about this article… <input type="text" id="heise-io-chat-trigger-input" name="search" class="text-(--heise-primary)-70 text-sm rounded-sm focus:ring-blue-500 focus:border-blue-500 block w-full p-1" placeholder="Ask about this article…"> Search OpenAI is taking a second shot at AI shopping with ChatGPT Wallet, catching up with rivals like Anthropic, Meta, and xAI, whose agents can already shop online. ChatGPT Wallet lets AI agents pay on behalf of users. OpenAI built it after scrapping Instant Checkout , its first attempt at letting people buy directly in the chat, because it wasn't flexible enough. At launch, the feature is available only in the US through Dots . Users must be adults and need a ChatGPT Pro subscription and an eligible US bank card. To get started, users add their card once. Before every purchase, the system displays the items, the total, and the selected card. Users then approve each payment with a security check such as Face ID or a fingerprint. Ad Neither the agent nor the online store ever sees the real card details. Instead, payment partners American Express, Mastercard, and Visa generate a payment token, a stand-in card number with its own security code. According to OpenAI, existing card rewards and the usual bank protections for eligible disputes still apply. OpenAI plans to eventually let users hand purchases off to their agents entirely, within set spending limits, product categories, and timeframes. Ad OpenAI maps out a fixed purchase flow from search to checkout According to OpenAI, a purchase starts with the customer describing what they want, after which ChatGPT searches the open web, APIs, or merchant product feeds for options, and the user compares them and picks one. ChatGPT then completes the purchase either by using a browser on the merchant's existing website or through a plugin that supports structured actions such as managing the cart. ChatGPT can handle the entire purchase process on its own, from product search and comparison to the final transaction. Merchants connect their existing website through browser integration or a dedicated plugin, while ChatGPT Wallet and Sign-in with ChatGPT handle payment and authentication. | Image: OpenAI Many websites and payment systems still block AI agents as bots or reject their purchases for security reasons. To address that, OpenAI is also releasing a Commerce Partner Playbook . The guide explains how merchants can adjust their firewalls, provide product data, and work with payment providers such as Stripe, Adyen, and Riskified so agent-driven purchases go through. Ad OpenAI is playing catch-up on payment features for AI agents. Competitors such as Anthropic's Claude Duet, Meta's Muse, and xAI's Grok Bot already have built-in payments and rely in part on single-use cards via Stripe Link . Google and Perplexity also let users buy through their own checkout tools, such as Google Pay and Instant Buy . ## The Decoder - Banks bar Chinese and Hong Kong investors from Anthropic IPO over US export controls (2026-11-13)

Banks bar Chinese and Hong Kong investors from Anthropic IPO over US export controls Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Banks bar Chinese and Hong Kong investors from Anthropic IPO over US export controls Matthias Bastian View the LinkedIn Profile of Matthias Bastian Nov 13, 2026 Investors from mainland China and Hong Kong are being shut out of Anthropic's IPO, according to Bloomberg. Multiple banks have reportedly rejected buy orders from those regions or consider allocations unlikely, with some of the people involved citing ITAR, the US export control rules governing defense-related goods, as the reason. Anthropic declined to comment. Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley are leading the offering, with 20 other banks involved. The stock is expected to trade on Nasdaq under the ticker ANT after pricing on November 18. The company's products aren't officially available in China or Hong Kong, and its broader relationship with the country has been defined by confrontation. Anthropic has blocked firms under Chinese control from its AI services and is fighting workarounds that let Chinese companies access Claude Code . It also exposed large-scale distillation attacks by DeepSeek, Moonshot, and MiniMax involving some 16 million queries. ## The Decoder - Anthropic Claude for Manufacturing plugs AI into factory software (2026-11-13)

Anthropic's Claude for Manufacturing plugs AI directly into factory software to automate industrial workflows Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Anthropic's Claude for Manufacturing plugs AI directly into factory software to automate industrial workflows Manuel Uth Nov 13, 2026 Anthropic has released Claude for Manufacturing, a plugin giving the AI direct access to industrial software to automate factory workflows. The new solution connects via four universal interfaces to Autodesk Fusion, Oracle NetSuite, Microsoft 365, and Infor. Other systems can be integrated through file exports to Microsoft 365. With 17 specific skills , the AI drafts quotes from actual inventory, compares suppliers, generates quality reports and design variants, and analyzes sensor data and manuals during machine downtime. Via an added Infor plugin, Claude can also order from backup suppliers when deliveries are delayed and reschedule production—after human approval. All results are linked to original source documents for employee review before reaching customers or suppliers. The plugin is available to Team and Enterprise customers but doesn't control physical machines yet. For that, Anthropic is separately developing the Model Hardware Standard as a research preview, connecting AI agents to devices like robotic arms and microscopes via standardized drivers. This cuts setup time from weeks or months to hours or minutes but still requires human oversight due to the AI's limited grasp of physical processes. ## The Decoder - Nearly 70 percent of US agencies use generative AI, unions catching up with guardrails (2026-11-13)

Nearly 70 percent of US agencies use generative AI, and unions are finally catching up with guardrails Ad Skip to content The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu The Decoder AI, Menschen, Wirtschaft --> Log In Subscribe DE Switch to German Primary Menu Sign In Register Subscribe Now The Decoder Opens discord in a new tab Opens LinkedIn in a new tab Short News Copy the url to clipboard Share this article Go to comment section Nearly 70 percent of US agencies use generative AI, and unions are finally catching up with guardrails Manuel Uth Nov 13, 2026 US public-sector unions are winning more protections against AI surveillance and automation-driven job losses. The issue is growing more urgent as nearly 70 percent of US government agencies already use generative AI, according to an Ernst and Young survey . The wins are piling up nationwide. After a strike vote in October, IFPTE Local 21 in Oakland secured retraining programs instead of AI-related layoffs. In Pennsylvania, Governor Josh Shapiro guaranteed SEIU Local 668 human oversight during a pilot of OpenAI's ChatGPT Enterprise . The Transport Workers Union in Ohio won veto power over autonomous vehicles. On top of that, California Governor Gavin Newsom banned AI surveillance software, often called "bossware," along with automated firings in September. New York has protected state employees from AI displacement by law since late 2025. Employers are pushing automation to cut costs, according to labor researcher Kung Feng at UC Berkeley. Union leaders like Liz Shuler of the AFL-CIO, Lorena Gonzalez, and TWU's John Samuelsen support AI safety systems where they help workers but insist on mandatory human control over every AI application in the workplace, the Guardian reports .
